The heart is about the size of a fist and beats about 70 times per minute at rest.
The heart is made of cardiac muscle that never gets tired.
The left side of the heart pumps oxygen rich blood to the body.
The right side of the heart pumps oxygen poor blood to the lungs.
Valves in the heart keep blood flowing in one direction.
Blood pressure is the force of blood pushing against the walls of blood vessels.
High blood pressure can damage the heart and blood vessels.
Cholesterol and fat can build up in arteries and block blood flow, causing heart attacks.
A stroke happens when blood flow to part of the brain is blocked.
The trachea, or windpipe, carries air to the lungs.
The epiglottis covers the windpipe when you swallow so food goes down the esophagus.
The bronchi are tubes that branch from the trachea into each lung.
Asthma makes the airways narrow and makes breathing difficult.
Coughing and sneezing remove irritants from the airways.
Peristalsis is the wavelike muscle contractions that move food through the digestive tract.
Enzymes break large food molecules into smaller molecules.
Proteins are broken down into amino acids.
Starches are broken down into sugars.
Fats are broken down into fatty acids.
The gallbladder stores bile made by the liver.
The liver also removes toxins from the blood and stores sugar.
The skin has two main layers, the epidermis and the dermis.
Melanin is a pigment that gives skin its color and protects against ultraviolet light.
Hair and nails are made of a protein called keratin.
Nerve endings in the skin sense touch, pressure, heat, cold, and pain.
The spinal cord carries messages between the brain and the body.
Sensory neurons carry messages from the senses to the brain.
Motor neurons carry messages from the brain to the muscles.
The brain is protected by the skull and cushioned by fluid.
Wearing a helmet protects the brain during biking and sports.
The left side of the brain controls the right side of the body.
Memory, learning, and emotions are controlled by the brain.
Sleep is needed for the brain to rest and form memories.
The eye focuses light on the retina, which has rods for dim light and cones for color.
The optic nerve carries signals from the eye to the brain.
The outer ear collects sound waves and funnels them to the eardrum.
Three tiny bones in the middle ear, the hammer, anvil, and stirrup, carry vibrations to the inner ear.
Taste buds on the tongue detect sweet, sour, salty, bitter, and savory tastes.
The sense of smell is closely connected to the sense of taste.
The femur, or thigh bone, is the longest bone in the body.
The smallest bones are in the middle ear.
Babies have more bones than adults because some bones fuse together as they grow.
Calcium and vitamin D keep bones strong.
A broken bone can heal because bone cells divide and make new bone.
Osteoporosis is a disease that makes bones weak and brittle.
The kneecap protects the knee joint.
Ball and socket joints, like the shoulder and hip, allow movement in many directions.
Hinge joints, like the elbow and knee, allow movement back and forth.
Pivot joints, like the neck, allow turning.
Muscles can only pull, not push.
Exercise makes muscles larger and stronger.
Muscles get tired when they run low on oxygen and energy.
Stretching before exercise helps prevent injuries.
The body needs more food energy during growth and exercise.
Eating too many calories without exercise causes weight gain.
Children need protein, calcium, and other nutrients to grow.
Vitamins A, B, C, D, E, and K each have different jobs in the body.
Vitamin A helps eyesight and is found in carrots.
Lack of vitamin D can cause rickets, a disease of soft bones.
Lack of iron can cause anemia, making a person feel tired.
Salt contains sodium, and too much can raise blood pressure.
Drinking water helps the body control temperature and remove wastes.
Dehydration happens when the body loses more water than it takes in.
Food labels show the nutrients and Calories in food.
Germs can spread through contaminated food and water.
Cooking meat thoroughly kills harmful bacteria.
Hand washing for at least 20 seconds with soap removes germs.
Some diseases, such as malaria and Lyme disease, are spread by insects and ticks.
Rabies is spread by the bite of an infected animal.
Allergies are reactions of the immune system to harmless substances like pollen.
Hay fever is an allergy to pollen.
Doctors use X rays to look at bones.
Doctors listen to the heart and lungs with a stethoscope.
A person's pulse can be felt in the wrist or neck.
Body temperature above normal is a fever, a sign of infection.
Genes can increase the risk of some diseases, such as heart disease and some cancers.
Lifestyle choices such as diet, exercise, and not smoking affect health.
Smoking causes lung cancer, heart disease, and emphysema.
Secondhand smoke harms people who breathe it.
Alcohol slows the nervous system and impairs judgment.
Medicines should be taken only as directed by a doctor.
Over the counter medicines can be bought without a prescription.
The life span of humans has increased because of better medicine, food, and sanitation.
Clean drinking water and sewage treatment have reduced the spread of disease.
Pregnant women need good nutrition for the developing baby.
A human pregnancy lasts about nine months.
Babies are born with reflexes such as grasping and sucking.
Adolescence is a time of rapid growth and change.
Older adults may lose bone density and muscle strength.
A reflex such as pulling a hand away from a hot stove uses the nervous system and the muscular system working together.
In a reflex, sensory nerves send a signal to the spinal cord, which sends a signal through motor nerves to the muscles.
The nervous system and the muscular system work together whenever the body moves.

If the Sun stopped shining, plants could not make food and food chains would collapse.
If Earth did not rotate, one side would always have day and the other always night.
If Earth's axis were not tilted, there would be no seasons.
If the Moon were closer to Earth, tides would be higher.
If there were no atmosphere, Earth would be much colder at night and hotter during the day.
If the ozone layer were destroyed, more ultraviolet radiation would reach the surface and cause skin cancer.
If greenhouse gases increase, Earth's average temperature increases.
If polar ice melts, sea level rises and coastal land floods.
If ocean temperatures rise, coral reefs can bleach and die.
If forests are cut down, erosion increases, habitats are lost, and less carbon dioxide is removed from the air.
If wetlands are drained, flooding can increase and wildlife loses habitat.
If a river is dammed, a lake forms behind the dam and fish migration is blocked.
If fertilizer runs into a lake, algae grow rapidly and then die, and decomposers use up the oxygen, killing fish.
If pesticides are used, they may kill helpful insects and build up in birds and fish.
If a top predator is removed, herbivore populations increase and may overgraze plants.
If herbivores overgraze, plants decrease, soil erodes, and herbivores starve.
If a disease kills many prey, predators that eat them will decrease.
If a new predator is introduced, the prey population will decrease.
If an invasive plant spreads, native plants may lose space, light, and nutrients.
If pollinators such as bees decline, fewer fruits and seeds are produced.
If decomposers were removed, dead material would build up and soil would lose nutrients.
If a drought occurs, plants wilt, crops fail, rivers and lakes shrink, and animals must move or die.
If a pond dries up, fish die and frogs and birds must find water elsewhere.
If there is heavy rain on bare soil, the soil washes away.
If a volcano erupts, ash can cover crops, block sunlight, and cool the climate.
If an earthquake happens under the ocean, it can cause a tsunami.
If a hurricane hits the coast, it brings flooding, storm surge, and wind damage.
If temperature drops below freezing, water in rock cracks freezes and expands, breaking the rock.
If a glacier melts, it leaves behind piles of sediment and boulders.
If rivers slow down, they drop their sediment.
If plates move apart, magma rises and new crust forms.
If plates collide, mountains form or one plate sinks under the other.
If a person does not get enough calcium, bones become weak.
If a person does not get enough vitamin C, they can get scurvy.
If a person does not drink enough water, they become dehydrated.
If a person exercises, heart rate and breathing rate increase.
If a person is frightened, adrenaline increases heart rate and breathing.
If germs enter the body, white blood cells attack them.
If a person is vaccinated, the immune system makes antibodies and memory cells.
If antibiotics are overused, resistant bacteria can survive and multiply.
If a plant does not get light, it cannot make food and turns yellow.
If a plant gets too little water, it wilts.
If a plant is placed on its side, the stem grows upward and the roots grow downward.
If a plant's leaves are removed, it cannot make food.
If the stomata close, less water is lost but less carbon dioxide enters the leaf.
If soil lacks nitrogen, plants grow slowly and leaves turn yellow.
If a seed is buried too deep, it may run out of stored food before reaching light.
If bees are removed from an orchard, fewer apples will be produced.
If the temperature increases, chemical reactions usually speed up.
If a substance is heated, its particles move faster and it may change state.
If a gas is cooled, its particles slow down and it may condense.
If a gas is compressed, its pressure increases.
If a balloon is heated, the gas inside expands and the balloon gets bigger.
If a metal rod is heated, it expands and gets slightly longer.
If more force is applied to an object, it accelerates more.
If the mass of an object increases, more force is needed to move it.
If friction is reduced, objects slide farther.
If a ramp is made steeper, a ball rolls down faster.
If a ramp is made longer and less steep, less force is needed to push an object up it.
If a pendulum string is made longer, the pendulum swings more slowly.
If a guitar string is tightened, its pitch gets higher.
If the amplitude of a sound wave increases, the sound gets louder.
If the frequency of a sound wave increases, the pitch gets higher.
If a light source moves closer to an object, the object's shadow gets larger.
If light passes from air into water, it slows down and bends.
If a circuit is opened by a switch, the current stops and the bulb goes out.
If one bulb burns out in a series circuit, all the bulbs go out.
If one bulb burns out in a parallel circuit, the others stay lit.
If more batteries are added in series, the bulb gets brighter.
If more coils are added to an electromagnet, it gets stronger.
If two north poles are placed together, the magnets push apart.
If a north pole and a south pole are placed together, the magnets pull together.
If a magnet is cut in half, each piece has a north and a south pole.
If an object is less dense than water, it floats.
If salt is added to water, an egg that sank may float.
If sugar is added to hot water and stirred, it dissolves faster.
If iron is exposed to water and air, it rusts.
If a candle is covered with a jar, the flame goes out when the oxygen is used up.
If food is left out of the refrigerator, bacteria grow faster and the food spoils.
If paper is burned, new substances such as ash, smoke, and gases form.
If ice is left in the sun, it melts into water.
If water is left in an open dish, it evaporates.
If water vapor touches a cold surface, it condenses into droplets.
If a habitat changes, organisms that cannot adapt may die or move away.
If an animal has a helpful variation, it is more likely to survive and pass on its genes.
If a population has more variation, it is more likely to survive environmental change.
If two parents have a dominant and a recessive allele, their child might show the recessive trait.
If the environment changes slowly, species may adapt through natural selection.
If the environment changes quickly, many species may become extinct.
If a scientist changes more than one variable, the results cannot show which variable caused the effect.
If an experiment is repeated with the same results, the results are more reliable.
If a hypothesis is not supported by data, the scientist should revise it.
An atom is the smallest unit of an element that keeps the properties of that element.
Atoms are made of protons, neutrons, and electrons.
Protons have a positive charge, electrons have a negative charge, and neutrons have no charge.
Protons and neutrons are found in the nucleus at the center of the atom.
Electrons move around the nucleus in energy levels or an electron cloud.
Most of the mass of an atom is in its nucleus.
Most of an atom is empty space.
The atomic number of an element is the number of protons in each atom.
The number of protons determines what element an atom is.
The mass number is the number of protons plus neutrons.
Isotopes are atoms of the same element with different numbers of neutrons.
A neutral atom has the same number of protons and electrons.
An ion is an atom that has gained or lost electrons and has an electric charge.
Valence electrons are the electrons in the outer energy level and determine how an atom bonds.
A chemical bond holds atoms together in a molecule or compound.
In a covalent bond, atoms share electrons.
The atoms in oxygen gas, O2, share two pairs of electrons in a double covalent bond.
In an ionic bond, electrons are transferred from one atom to another, forming oppositely charged ions that attract.
Table salt, sodium chloride, is held together by ionic bonds.
Metallic bonds hold metal atoms together with a sea of shared electrons.
The periodic table organizes the elements by increasing atomic number.
Dmitri Mendeleev created the first periodic table.
Elements in the same group, or column, of the periodic table have similar chemical properties.
A row of the periodic table is called a period.
Metals are on the left side of the periodic table, nonmetals on the right, and metalloids along the zigzag line.
Most elements are metals.
Metals are shiny, conduct heat and electricity, and are malleable and ductile.
Nonmetals are dull, brittle when solid, and poor conductors of heat and electricity.
Metalloids such as silicon have properties of both metals and nonmetals and are used in computer chips.
Noble gases such as helium, neon, and argon are very unreactive.
Alkali metals such as sodium and potassium are very reactive and react with water.
Halogens such as fluorine and chlorine are very reactive nonmetals.
Hydrogen is the lightest and most common element in the universe.
Helium is a light, unreactive gas used to fill balloons.
Oxygen is a gas needed for burning and for respiration.
Carbon is found in all living things and in fuels such as coal, oil, and natural gas.
Diamond and graphite are both made of pure carbon.
Nitrogen makes up most of the air.
Iron is a metal that is magnetic and rusts.
Gold is a metal that does not rust or tarnish.
Mercury is a metal that is liquid at room temperature.
Silicon is used to make computer chips and is found in sand.
Chemical symbols are one or two letter abbreviations for elements, such as H for hydrogen, O for oxygen, C for carbon, and Na for sodium.
Fe is the symbol for iron, Au for gold, Ag for silver, Cu for copper, and Pb for lead.
A chemical formula shows the elements and the number of atoms of each in a compound.
H2O means each water molecule has two hydrogen atoms and one oxygen atom.
CO2 has one carbon atom and two oxygen atoms.
NaCl is sodium chloride, table salt.
C6H12O6 is glucose, a sugar.
A chemical reaction is a process in which substances change into new substances.
Reactants are the starting substances in a chemical reaction, and products are the new substances formed.
In a chemical reaction, atoms are rearranged but not created or destroyed.
A balanced chemical equation has the same number of each type of atom on both sides.
The law of conservation of mass states that mass is neither created nor destroyed in a chemical reaction.
Exothermic reactions release heat energy, such as burning.
Endothermic reactions absorb heat energy, such as a cold pack.
Combustion, or burning, is a chemical reaction of a fuel with oxygen that releases heat and light.
Burning needs fuel, oxygen, and heat; this is called the fire triangle.
A fire can be put out by removing heat, fuel, or oxygen.
Covering a fire with a blanket or sand removes oxygen.
Burning wood produces ash, smoke, carbon dioxide, and water vapor.
Burning methane or natural gas produces carbon dioxide and water.
Raising the temperature makes most chemical reactions go faster.
Increasing the concentration or surface area of reactants speeds up a reaction.
A catalyst speeds up a chemical reaction without being used up.
Enzymes are biological catalysts.
Acids taste sour, turn blue litmus paper red, and have a pH less than 7.
Lemon juice, vinegar, and stomach acid are acids.
Bases taste bitter, feel slippery, turn red litmus paper blue, and have a pH greater than 7.
Baking soda, soap, ammonia, and bleach are bases.
Pure water is neutral and has a pH of 7.
The pH scale measures how acidic or basic a substance is, from 0 to 14.
Neutralization is when an acid and a base react to form water and a salt.
Antacids are bases that neutralize stomach acid.
Indicators such as litmus paper change color in acids and bases.
Baking soda and vinegar react to produce carbon dioxide gas.
Photosynthesis and respiration are chemical reactions in living things.
Electrolysis uses electricity to split water into hydrogen and oxygen gases.
Radioactive elements such as uranium give off radiation as their nuclei break down.
Nuclear fission splits large atoms and releases energy.
Nuclear fusion joins small atoms and releases energy, as in the Sun.
Alloys are mixtures of metals, such as steel, brass, and bronze.
Steel is made of iron and carbon.
Bronze is made of copper and tin, and brass is made of copper and zinc.
Polymers are long chains of repeating molecules, such as plastics, rubber, and proteins.
Hydrocarbons are compounds of hydrogen and carbon found in fossil fuels.
Organic compounds contain carbon.
Salt water conducts electricity because it contains ions.
John Dalton proposed that matter is made of atoms.
J. J. Thomson discovered the electron.
Ernest Rutherford discovered that atoms have a small dense positive nucleus.
Niels Bohr proposed that electrons travel in energy levels around the nucleus.
Scientific models of the atom have changed as new evidence was discovered.
Hydrogen has atomic number 1 and is the first element in the periodic table.
Helium has atomic number 2, carbon 6, nitrogen 7, oxygen 8, sodium 11, and iron 26.
An oxygen atom has 8 protons and 8 electrons.
A carbon atom has 6 protons.
A hydrogen atom has 1 proton and 1 electron and usually no neutrons.
The number of electrons in a neutral atom equals the number of protons.
Elements in the same column of the periodic table have the same number of valence electrons.
The noble gases are in the last column of the periodic table and rarely react.
The alkali metals are in the first column of the periodic table and react violently with water.
Sodium and potassium are soft, shiny, very reactive metals.
Chlorine is a yellow green poisonous gas used to disinfect water.
Fluorine is added to toothpaste and water to prevent tooth decay.
Neon glows in signs when electricity passes through it.
Argon is used in light bulbs because it does not react.
Carbon forms more compounds than any other element.
Calcium is found in bones, teeth, shells, and limestone.
Silicon and oxygen are the most common elements in Earth's crust.
Iron and nickel make up most of Earth's core.
Hydrogen and helium make up most of the Sun and stars.
Nitrogen and oxygen make up most of Earth's atmosphere.
Carbon, hydrogen, oxygen, and nitrogen make up most of the human body.
Oxygen is the most abundant element in the human body by mass.
There are more than 100 known elements.
About 90 elements occur naturally on Earth.
Elements combine to form millions of different compounds.
A water molecule is made of two hydrogen atoms bonded to one oxygen atom.
A carbon dioxide molecule is made of one carbon atom and two oxygen atoms.
Methane, CH4, is made of one carbon atom and four hydrogen atoms.
Ammonia, NH3, is made of one nitrogen atom and three hydrogen atoms.
Ozone, O3, is made of three oxygen atoms.
Oxygen gas, O2, is made of two oxygen atoms.
Glucose, C6H12O6, contains carbon, hydrogen, and oxygen.
Table sugar is sucrose, a compound of carbon, hydrogen, and oxygen.
Sodium chloride is made of sodium and chlorine.
Rust, iron oxide, is made of iron and oxygen.
Hydrogen peroxide breaks down into water and oxygen.
Water can be broken down into hydrogen and oxygen gases by electricity.
When hydrogen burns in oxygen it forms water.
When methane burns it produces carbon dioxide and water.
When wood or paper burns, it combines with oxygen and produces carbon dioxide, water vapor, and ash.
A candle flame needs oxygen; covering a candle with a jar makes it go out.
A substance that speeds up a reaction without being changed is a catalyst.
Heating, stirring, and using smaller pieces make reactions happen faster.
Food spoils more slowly in the refrigerator because cold slows chemical reactions.
Cooking an egg is an irreversible chemical change.
Melting ice is a reversible physical change.
Most physical changes can be reversed, while most chemical changes cannot easily be reversed.
Mixtures can be separated by physical methods, but compounds can only be separated by chemical reactions.
Air is a mixture of gases.
Salt water is a mixture that can be separated by evaporation.
Ocean water is a solution of salt and other minerals dissolved in water.
Brass is a solid solution of copper and zinc.
Steel is an alloy that is stronger than pure iron.
Stainless steel contains chromium, which keeps it from rusting.
Galvanized steel is coated with zinc to prevent rust.
Gold and platinum are used in jewelry because they do not react with air or water.
Aluminum is used in airplanes because it is light and strong.
Copper turns green over time when it reacts with air, as on the Statue of Liberty.
Silver tarnishes and turns black when it reacts with sulfur in the air.
A substance with a pH of 2 is a strong acid, and a substance with a pH of 13 is a strong base.
Acids react with metals and produce hydrogen gas.
Acids react with limestone and marble and produce carbon dioxide.
Rain is naturally slightly acidic because carbon dioxide dissolves in it.
Vinegar contains acetic acid.
Citrus fruits such as lemons, limes, and oranges contain citric acid.
Milk of magnesia and baking soda are bases that can neutralize acids.
Litmus paper turns red in acids and blue in bases.
Red cabbage juice can be used as an indicator because it changes color in acids and bases.
Soaps and detergents are bases that help remove grease and dirt.
Bleach and ammonia should never be mixed because they produce a poisonous gas.
Household chemicals should be stored in labeled containers away from children.
A solute dissolves in a solvent to make a solution.
Salt dissolves in water, but it does not dissolve in oil.
Oil and water do not mix; the oil floats on top.
Soap helps oil and water mix.
A concentrated solution has a lot of solute; a dilute solution has a little solute.
Adding more water to a solution makes it more dilute.
Evaporating water from a solution makes it more concentrated.
Crystals of salt or sugar form when water evaporates from a saturated solution.
Rock candy is made of sugar crystals that form from a saturated sugar solution.
Temperature affects how much solute can dissolve; more sugar dissolves in hot water.
The particles in a solution are too small to see and do not settle out.
A colloid such as milk has particles that stay mixed and scatter light.
Physical properties of a substance include its boiling point, which can identify it.
Pure water boils at 100 degrees Celsius and freezes at 0 degrees Celsius at sea level.
Salt water boils at a higher temperature and freezes at a lower temperature than pure water.
The density of a substance can help identify it.
Gold has a high density of about 19 grams per cubic centimeter.
Aluminum has a density of about 2.7 grams per cubic centimeter.
Ice has a density of about 0.92 grams per cubic centimeter, which is less than water.
Cork and wood have densities less than 1 gram per cubic centimeter.
Lead is very dense and is used for weights.
A substance's density stays the same no matter how much of it there is.
To find density, measure mass with a balance and volume with a graduated cylinder, then divide mass by volume.
An object with a mass of 20 grams and a volume of 10 cubic centimeters has a density of 2 grams per cubic centimeter.
Jupiter is the largest planet.
Mercury is the smallest planet.
Mercury is closest to the Sun.
Neptune is farthest from the Sun.
Venus has the hottest surface of any planet.
Neptune and Uranus are the coldest planets.
Saturn has the most visible rings.
Jupiter and Saturn have the most moons.
Earth is the densest planet.
Saturn has the lowest density of the planets.
Mercury has the shortest year and Neptune has the longest year.
Jupiter has the shortest day of the planets, about 10 hours.
The Sun is the largest object in the solar system.
The Moon is smaller than Earth.
Earth is smaller than the Sun.
The Sun is much larger than the Moon but looks about the same size because it is much farther away.
Stars are much farther away than planets.
Light travels much faster than sound.
Sound travels faster in solids than in liquids, and faster in liquids than in gases.
Gases have the most space between particles.
Solids have the least space between particles.
Solids are usually denser than liquids.
Liquids are denser than gases.
Ice is less dense than liquid water, which is unusual.
Salt water is denser than fresh water.
Cold water is denser than warm water.
Cold air is denser than warm air.
Gold is denser than iron.
Iron is denser than aluminum.
Iron is denser than water, so it sinks; wood is less dense than water, so it floats.
Oil is less dense than water and floats on top of it.
Diamond is harder than quartz.
Quartz is harder than glass.
Talc is softer than gypsum.
Gypsum is softer than calcite.
Granite is harder than sandstone and limestone.
Clay particles are smaller than silt particles.
Silt particles are smaller than sand particles.
Boulders are larger than cobbles, cobbles are larger than pebbles.
Pebbles are larger than sand.
The inner core is the hottest layer of Earth and the crust is the coolest.
The mantle is the thickest layer of Earth and the crust is the thinnest.
Oceanic crust is younger and denser than continental crust.
The Pacific Ocean is larger and deeper than the Atlantic Ocean.
Antarctica is colder than the Arctic.
The equator receives more direct sunlight than the poles.
Deserts receive less rain than rain forests.
Tropical rain forests have more species than any other biome.
The tundra and deserts have the fewest species.
Blue whales are the largest animals.
Cheetahs are the fastest land animals.
Giraffes are the tallest animals.
Elephants are the largest land animals.
Hummingbirds are among the smallest birds.
Ostriches are the largest birds.
Bacteria are much smaller than plant and animal cells.
Viruses are smaller than bacteria.
Atoms are smaller than molecules, molecules are smaller than cells.
A cell is smaller than a tissue, a tissue is smaller than an organ.
An organ is smaller than an organ system.
An electron is much smaller and lighter than a proton.
Protons and neutrons have about the same mass.
Gamma rays have more energy than X rays, which have more energy than ultraviolet light.
Visible light has more energy than infrared light and radio waves.
Blue light has a shorter wavelength than red light.
A high pitched sound has a higher frequency than a low pitched sound.
A loud sound has a larger amplitude than a soft sound.
A heavier object has more inertia than a lighter object.
A faster object has more kinetic energy than a slower object of the same mass.
A higher object has more gravitational potential energy than a lower object of the same mass.
Metals conduct heat better than wood, plastic, or glass.
Copper conducts electricity better than iron.
Rubber and plastic are better insulators than metals.
Dark surfaces absorb more heat than light surfaces.
Land heats and cools faster than water.
Sand heats up faster than water in sunlight.
Warm water evaporates faster than cold water.
Hot water dissolves sugar faster than cold water.
Small pieces dissolve faster than large pieces.
Powdered sugar dissolves faster than a sugar cube.
A steep slope has faster runoff and more erosion than a gentle slope.
Fast moving water carries more sediment than slow moving water.
Larger particles settle out of water faster than smaller particles.
Glaciers move more slowly than rivers.
Coal, oil, and natural gas release more pollution than solar, wind, and hydroelectric power.
Renewable resources can be replaced faster than nonrenewable resources.
Mammals and birds keep a constant body temperature, while reptiles, amphibians, and fish do not.
Fish and amphibians lay more eggs than mammals have young.
Insects have six legs.
Spiders have eight legs.
Monocots have one seed leaf.
Dicots have two seed leaves.
Animal cells lack the cell wall and chloroplasts that plant cells have.
Sexual reproduction produces more variation than asexual reproduction.
Asexual reproduction is faster than sexual reproduction.
A series circuit has one path.
A parallel circuit has more than one path.
A longer ramp requires less force but more distance than a shorter ramp.
A pulley system with more pulleys needs less force.
An electromagnet with more coils is stronger than one with fewer coils.
Weather changes quickly.
Climate changes slowly over many years.
Weathering breaks rocks down, while erosion moves the pieces.
Mass stays the same everywhere, while weight changes with gravity.
Speed is distance per time.
Velocity includes direction.
A hypothesis is a testable idea.
A theory is well supported by evidence.
An observation uses the senses.
An inference is a conclusion drawn from observations.
Plants make their own food using energy from sunlight.
Green plants use the Sun's energy to change carbon dioxide and water into sugar.
Leaves capture sunlight to make food for the plant.
During photosynthesis, oxygen is given off as a waste product.
Photosynthesis produces sugar and oxygen.
Photosynthesis uses up carbon dioxide and water.
The food made by plants is stored as starch.
Without sunlight, plants cannot carry out photosynthesis.
Photosynthesis happens faster in bright light than in dim light.
Increasing light, carbon dioxide, or water can increase the rate of photosynthesis.
Plants give off oxygen during the day when they photosynthesize.
Animals depend on plants for the oxygen they breathe.
Respiration in cells uses oxygen and sugar and produces carbon dioxide, water, and energy.
Breathing brings oxygen into the body for cellular respiration.
Energy for living things comes from food, which comes from plants, which get energy from the Sun.
The energy in a hamburger originally came from the Sun.
The energy in food chains starts with sunlight captured by plants.
When an animal eats a plant, it gets the energy stored in the plant.
When a hawk eats a snake, energy is transferred from the snake to the hawk.
Arrows in a food web show the direction energy flows.
A food chain shows who eats whom.
Each link in a food chain passes energy to the next link.
Removing one species from a food web affects many other species.
Plants are eaten by herbivores, which are eaten by carnivores.
Living things get energy from the food they eat or make.
Organisms that make their own food are called producers or autotrophs.
Organisms that eat other organisms are called consumers or heterotrophs.
Organisms that break down dead matter are called decomposers.
Decomposers return nutrients from dead organisms to the soil.
Bacteria and fungi decompose dead leaves, logs, and animals.
Heat makes particles move faster; cooling makes them move slower.
Adding thermal energy to matter increases the motion of its particles.
Removing thermal energy from matter decreases the motion of its particles.
When matter is heated it usually expands, and when it is cooled it usually contracts.
When water freezes, its particles slow down and lock into a pattern.
When ice melts, heat energy is absorbed and the particles move more freely.
Evaporation happens at the surface of a liquid at any temperature.
Boiling happens throughout a liquid at its boiling point.
Water vapor is an invisible gas in the air.
Liquid water turns into water vapor when it evaporates.
Water vapor turns into liquid water when it condenses.
Solid ice turns into liquid water when it melts.
Liquid water turns into solid ice when it freezes.
Matter changes state when energy is added or removed.
A physical change does not change what a substance is made of.
A chemical change makes a new substance.
Mass is conserved in every physical and chemical change.
The total mass of the reactants equals the total mass of the products.
Gravity pulls everything toward Earth's center.
Gravity is the force that makes things fall down.
Gravity holds the Moon in orbit around Earth and Earth in orbit around the Sun.
Gravity gives objects weight.
The heavier an object, the greater the pull of gravity on it.
Friction slows moving objects and produces heat.
Friction happens when two surfaces rub against each other.
A force can make an object start moving, stop moving, or change direction.
A push or a pull can change the motion of an object.
An object will not move unless a force acts on it.
A larger force produces a larger change in motion.
Objects in motion keep moving until a force stops them.
Energy can be transferred from one object to another.
Energy can be changed from one form to another.
Energy is conserved; it is never created or destroyed.
Heat flows from warmer things to cooler things.
Heat moves by conduction through solids, by convection through fluids, and by radiation through space.
Light travels in straight lines until it hits something.
Light can be reflected, absorbed, or transmitted by objects.
Sound is made by vibrating objects.
Sound travels as vibrations through solids, liquids, and gases.
Sound cannot travel through empty space.
Electricity flows in a closed loop called a circuit.
Electricity needs a complete path to flow.
Magnets have two poles, north and south.
Opposite poles attract and like poles repel.
Magnets attract things made of iron.
Earth rotates on its axis, causing day and night.
Earth revolves around the Sun, taking one year.
The tilt of Earth's axis causes seasons.
The Moon revolves around Earth, causing the phases of the Moon.
The Moon's gravity causes the tides.
The Sun is a star that gives Earth light and heat.
The planets revolve around the Sun.
Rocks break down into soil through weathering.
Moving water, wind, and ice carry away weathered rock in erosion.
Sediment is dropped in new places by deposition.
Earth's surface changes slowly through weathering, erosion, and deposition.
Earth's surface changes quickly through earthquakes, volcanoes, landslides, and floods.
Water moves in a cycle of evaporation, condensation, and precipitation.
Clouds are made of water droplets.
Rain, snow, sleet, and hail are kinds of precipitation.
Weather changes from day to day.
Climate is the usual weather of a place over many years.
Fossils show what life was like long ago.
Fossils are usually found in sedimentary rocks.
Rock layers show the order of events in Earth's history.
Living things are made of cells.
Cells carry out the functions needed for life.
Cells come from other cells.
Traits are passed from parents to offspring by genes.
Offspring are similar but not identical to their parents.
Animals have body parts that help them survive in their environment.
Plants have parts that help them survive in their environment.
Organisms that are well adapted survive and reproduce.
When the environment changes, some organisms survive and others die.
Animals respond to changes in their environment.
Plants respond to light, water, gravity, and touch.
People can protect the environment by reducing, reusing, and recycling.
People use natural resources for food, shelter, clothing, energy, and transportation.
Some resources are renewable and some are nonrenewable.
Pollution harms living things and their habitats.
Bacteria come in three main shapes: rods, spheres, and spirals.
Some bacteria can form spores that survive harsh conditions.
Bacteria need food, water, and the right temperature to grow.
Most bacteria grow best at warm temperatures around body temperature.
Boiling water kills most bacteria.
Freezing slows bacteria but does not kill all of them.
Salt and sugar preserve food by drawing water out of bacteria.
Vinegar preserves pickles because most bacteria cannot grow in acid.
Bacteria in the roots of legumes fix nitrogen.
Bacteria are used to clean up oil spills and sewage.
Bacteria are used to make medicines such as insulin.
E. coli is a bacterium that lives in the intestines; some kinds cause food poisoning.
Salmonella bacteria can cause food poisoning from undercooked chicken or eggs.
Strep throat and tuberculosis are caused by bacteria.
Lyme disease is caused by bacteria spread by ticks.
Viruses are not cells and have no cytoplasm or organelles.
A virus is made of genetic material surrounded by a protein coat.
Viruses attach to host cells, inject their genetic material, and make the cell produce new viruses.
Viruses cause colds, flu, measles, mumps, polio, chicken pox, and AIDS.
HIV is the virus that causes AIDS by attacking the immune system.
Rabies is a virus spread by animal bites.
Some viruses infect plants or bacteria.
Vaccines exist for many viral diseases such as measles, polio, and flu.
Antiviral medicines can slow some viruses, but antibiotics do not work on viruses.
Protists include algae, amoebas, paramecia, and slime molds.
Plant like protists, such as algae and diatoms, make food by photosynthesis.
Animal like protists, such as amoebas, eat other organisms.
Fungus like protists, such as slime molds, absorb nutrients.
Plankton includes many protists and is food for ocean animals.
Diatoms are algae with glass like shells.
Fungi are made of threadlike structures called hyphae.
The mushroom is the reproductive structure of a fungus that releases spores.
Penicillium mold produces penicillin.
Fungi and bacteria are the main decomposers on Earth.
Some fungi form partnerships with plant roots that help the plants absorb water and minerals.
Cells are the building blocks of all living things.
Cells were not discovered until the microscope was invented.
The cell theory was developed by Schleiden, Schwann, and Virchow.
Most cells are too small to see without a microscope, but some egg cells can be seen easily.
The largest single cell is an ostrich egg yolk.
Nerve cells can be over a meter long.
Cell size is limited because materials must move in and out through the membrane.
Plant cells are often rectangular because of their cell walls.
Animal cells are often round or irregular in shape.
Muscle cells contain many mitochondria because they need a lot of energy.
Leaf cells contain many chloroplasts for photosynthesis.
Root cells have no chloroplasts because they get no light.
Red blood cells are disk shaped to carry oxygen.
White blood cells can change shape to surround germs.
Skin cells are flat and fit tightly together to form a protective layer.
Bone cells are surrounded by hard minerals.
Fat cells store energy.
Sperm cells have tails to swim toward the egg.
Egg cells contain stored food for the developing embryo.
Stem cells can become many different kinds of cells.
Cells use energy for growth, repair, movement, and making new materials.
Cells take in nutrients and oxygen and give off wastes such as carbon dioxide.
Water moves into and out of cells by osmosis.
A plant cell placed in salt water loses water and shrinks.
A plant cell placed in fresh water takes in water and becomes firm.
Wilting happens when plant cells lose water.
Before a cell divides, it copies its DNA.
In mitosis, a cell divides into two daughter cells with identical chromosomes.
In meiosis, a cell divides twice to make four sex cells with half the chromosomes.
Fertilization restores the full number of chromosomes.
Cells in different parts of the body look different because they use different genes.
Every cell in your body has the same DNA, except sex cells and red blood cells.
A gene is a set of instructions for making a protein.
Proteins build structures and do jobs in cells.
DNA is made of nucleotides containing sugar, phosphate, and bases.
In DNA, adenine pairs with thymine and guanine pairs with cytosine.
DNA can be extracted from strawberries, bananas, and onions in a classroom.
Heredity explains why children resemble their parents.
Genes come in pairs, one from each parent.
Some traits are controlled by a single gene, while others are controlled by many genes.
A dominant allele is expressed even if only one copy is present.
A recessive allele is expressed only when two copies are present.
In pea plants, tall is dominant over short, and purple flowers are dominant over white.
If a tall pea plant Tt is crossed with a short pea plant tt, half of the offspring are expected to be tall.
If both parents are Tt, the chance of a tt offspring is one in four, or 25 percent.
If one parent is TT, all offspring will show the dominant trait.
A Punnett square has four boxes showing possible gene combinations.
Each box in a Punnett square represents a 25 percent chance.
Mendel crossed pea plants and counted the offspring to find patterns of inheritance.
Mendel found that recessive traits can skip a generation.
Sex linked traits such as color blindness are more common in males.
Down syndrome is caused by an extra chromosome.
Genetic counselors help families understand inherited disorders.
Cloning produces an organism with the same DNA as its parent.
Dolly the sheep was the first mammal cloned from an adult cell.
Biotechnology uses living things to make products.
Animals reproduce sexually when a sperm fertilizes an egg.
Some animals such as sponges and hydras can reproduce by budding.
Some animals such as sea stars and flatworms can regrow from pieces.
Some insects such as aphids can reproduce without mating.
Plants reproduce sexually when pollen fertilizes an ovule.
Self pollination happens when pollen from a flower fertilizes the same flower.
Cross pollination happens when pollen moves from one plant to another plant.
Cross pollination produces more variation in offspring.
Gardeners use cuttings to produce plants identical to the parent.
Farmers use selective breeding to produce crops and animals with useful traits.
Earth's history is divided into the Precambrian, Paleozoic, Mesozoic, and Cenozoic.
The Precambrian makes up about 88 percent of Earth's history.
Stromatolites are layered rocks formed by ancient bacteria and are among the oldest fossils.
The first multicellular organisms appeared in the oceans more than 600 million years ago.
Trilobites were arthropods that lived in Paleozoic seas.
Ammonites were sea animals with coiled shells that lived during the Mesozoic.
The first fish appeared in the Paleozoic.
Amphibians appeared after fish, and reptiles appeared after amphibians.
Dinosaurs lived during the Triassic, Jurassic, and Cretaceous periods.
The first birds appeared in the Jurassic period.
Mammals first appeared during the Triassic period but were small.
Woolly mammoths lived during the ice ages and are related to elephants.
Saber toothed cats were predators that lived during the ice ages.
Fossils found in the same rock layer lived at about the same time.
Fossils in lower layers are older than fossils in higher layers.
Comparing fossils from different layers shows how life changed over time.
Fossils of simple organisms are found in older rocks than fossils of complex organisms.
Footprint fossils can show how fast an animal walked and whether it traveled in groups.
Coprolites are fossilized animal droppings that show what the animal ate.
Fossilized pollen shows what plants grew in an area long ago.
A fossil of a palm tree found in Wyoming shows the climate there was once warmer.
Fossils of the same animals on different continents suggest the continents were once connected.
Glossopteris was a fern like plant whose fossils are found on several southern continents.
Plate movements over millions of years changed the positions of continents and their climates.
Fossil fuels are found in rocks from the Carboniferous period when great swamps covered the land.
A geologic cross section shows rock layers below the surface.
An intrusion of igneous rock is younger than the layers it cuts across.
Erosion surfaces in rock layers show times when layers were removed.
Index fossils must be widespread, abundant, and have lived for a short time.
Electric charges exist in all matter as protons and electrons.
Electrons flow through a wire like water flows through a pipe.
A battery is like a pump that pushes electrons around a circuit.
Resistance in a circuit is like a narrow section of pipe that slows the flow.
A closed switch completes a circuit, and an open switch breaks it.
A dimmer switch changes resistance to make lights dimmer or brighter.
An electric motor contains a coil of wire that spins between magnets.
Electric motors are used in fans, washing machines, cars, toys, and refrigerators.
Generators in power plants are turned by steam, water, or wind.
Most power plants boil water to make steam that spins turbines.
Electrical energy travels from power plants to homes through power lines.
Transformers increase voltage for long distance transmission and decrease it for homes.
Homes in the United States use 120 volt alternating current.
Batteries produce direct current.
A short circuit can make wires dangerously hot.
Damaged cords and overloaded outlets can cause electrical fires.
Ground fault outlets near sinks protect people from shocks.
Electric eels can produce electric shocks to stun prey.
Nerves carry electrical signals in the body.
The heart's beat is controlled by electrical signals.
Lightning heats the air to about 30,000 degrees Celsius, hotter than the Sun's surface.
Lightning travels from cloud to ground, ground to cloud, or cloud to cloud.
Static electricity can damage computer parts.
A Van de Graaff generator builds up static charge that makes hair stand on end.
An electroscope detects static charge.
Electric fields surround charged objects.
Magnetic fields surround magnets and wires carrying current.
A coil of wire carrying current acts like a bar magnet.
Speakers use electromagnets and permanent magnets to make a cone vibrate.
Maglev trains use magnets to float above the tracks and reduce friction.
MRI machines use strong magnets to make images of the inside of the body.
Credit cards and hard drives store information using magnetism.
Compasses do not work well near large iron objects.
The Earth's magnetic field is produced by its liquid iron outer core.
Aurora borealis is caused by solar particles guided by Earth's magnetic field to the poles.
The rock cycle has no beginning or end.
Sedimentary rock can become metamorphic rock when it is buried deeply and heated.
Metamorphic rock can melt to become magma.
Igneous rock can be weathered into sediment that forms sedimentary rock.
Uplift brings buried rocks to the surface where they can be weathered.
Lithification is the process of turning sediment into rock by compaction and cementation.
Minerals dissolved in water act as cement that glues sediment grains together.
Clastic sedimentary rocks are made of broken pieces of other rocks.
Chemical sedimentary rocks, such as rock salt, form when minerals come out of solution.
Organic sedimentary rocks, such as coal and some limestone, form from the remains of living things.
Breccia is a sedimentary rock made of sharp angular fragments.
Siltstone is a sedimentary rock made of silt.
Schist is a metamorphic rock with shiny flat mica crystals.
Anthracite is a hard metamorphic form of coal.
Rhyolite is a light colored fine grained igneous rock.
Andesite is an igneous rock common in volcanoes above subduction zones.
Diorite and gabbro are coarse grained intrusive igneous rocks.
Scoria is a dark igneous rock with many holes.
Igneous rocks with no crystals, such as obsidian, cooled very quickly.
Igneous rocks with gas holes, such as pumice and scoria, formed from lava rich in gas.
The minerals in a rock determine its color.
Light colored igneous rocks are rich in silica, like granite.
Dark colored igneous rocks are rich in iron and magnesium, like basalt.
Sediments are deposited in horizontal layers.
Ripple marks and mud cracks preserved in sedimentary rock show past environments.
Mud cracks show that the sediment was once wet and then dried out.
Ripple marks show that water or wind once moved across the sediment.
Cross bedding in sandstone shows ancient sand dunes or river currents.
Coal beds show that there were once swamps in the area.
Limestone with coral fossils shows that the area was once a warm shallow sea.
Rock salt layers show that an ancient sea evaporated.
Glacial striations show the direction a glacier moved.
The ages of rocks on the ocean floor increase with distance from the mid ocean ridge.
Continental rocks are much older than ocean floor rocks.
Earth's crust is broken into about a dozen large plates and many smaller ones.
The North American plate includes most of North America and part of the Atlantic Ocean floor.
The Pacific plate is the largest tectonic plate.
Earthquakes along the west coast of South America are caused by subduction of the Nazca plate.
Tectonic plates move because of convection in the mantle, ridge push, and slab pull.
Ridge push happens as new crust forms at ridges and pushes plates apart.
Slab pull happens when a sinking plate pulls the rest of the plate along.
Volcanic island arcs such as Japan and the Aleutians form at subduction zones.
Earthquake depth increases away from a trench where a plate is subducting.
Shallow earthquakes happen at mid ocean ridges and transform faults.
Normal faults form when crust is pulled apart.
Reverse faults form when crust is pushed together.
Strike slip faults form when rocks slide past each other horizontally.
Fault block mountains such as the Sierra Nevada form when crust is pulled apart and blocks tilt.
Folded mountains such as the Appalachians form when rock layers are squeezed.
Dome mountains form when magma pushes up the crust without breaking through.
Volcanic mountains form from erupted lava and ash.
A caldera is a large crater that forms when a volcano collapses.
Lava plateaus form from many layers of runny lava.
Magma that cools underground can form dikes, sills, and batholiths.
Volcanic eruptions can trigger mudflows called lahars.
Pyroclastic flows are fast moving clouds of hot gas and ash.
Volcanic gases include water vapor, carbon dioxide, and sulfur dioxide.
Seismic waves are recorded on a seismogram.
The difference in arrival time between P waves and S waves tells how far away an earthquake was.
The shadow zone where S waves are not detected shows that the outer core is liquid.
Liquefaction happens when shaking makes wet soil act like a liquid.
Tsunamis can travel across entire oceans at the speed of a jet plane.
Tsunamis become very tall when they reach shallow water near the coast.
The water at a beach may pull back far from shore just before a tsunami arrives.
Weathering happens faster at the corners and edges of rocks, rounding them.
Exfoliation is weathering in which outer layers of rock peel off like onion skin.
Spheroidal weathering rounds rocks.
Talus is a pile of broken rock at the base of a cliff.
Creep is the very slow movement of soil downhill, which can tilt fences and trees.
Slump is a mass of soil that slides down a curved surface.
Mass wasting is the movement of rock and soil downhill by gravity.
Sheet erosion removes thin layers of soil across a field.
Gullies form when running water cuts channels into soil.
The load of a stream is the sediment it carries.
Streams carry sediment as dissolved material, suspended particles, and rolling bed load.
The mouth of a river is where it empties into a larger body of water.
The source of a river is where it begins, often in mountains.
Tributaries are smaller streams that flow into a larger river.
Rivers are faster and carve V shaped valleys in mountains, and slower with wide valleys on plains.
Waterfalls form where a river flows over hard rock above softer rock.
Groundwater moves slowly through pores and cracks in rock.
Hard water contains dissolved minerals such as calcium and magnesium.
Wind deposits fine particles called loess.
Desert pavement is a layer of stones left after wind blows away the sand.
Ventifacts are rocks shaped by wind blown sand.
Longshore currents move sand along the shore.
Headlands are eroded by waves into sea stacks.
Valley glaciers form in mountains, and continental glaciers cover large areas of land.
Cirques are bowl shaped hollows carved by glaciers at the heads of valleys.
Horns are sharp mountain peaks carved by glaciers, like the Matterhorn.
Drumlins are long smooth hills of glacial till.
Eskers are long ridges of sediment deposited by streams under glaciers.
Outwash plains are made of sediment sorted by meltwater streams from glaciers.
Till is unsorted sediment deposited directly by glacial ice.
The last ice age ended about 10,000 to 12,000 years ago.
Paleontologists date fossils by the rock layers they are found in.
Radiometric dating measures the decay of radioactive elements such as uranium and potassium.
After one half life, half of a radioactive sample has decayed.
After two half lives, one quarter of the radioactive sample remains.
Geologic time is divided based on major changes in fossils.
The Cambrian explosion was a time when many kinds of animals appeared about 540 million years ago.
The Permian extinction 250 million years ago was the largest mass extinction.
The Cretaceous extinction 66 million years ago ended the age of dinosaurs.
Mammals became the dominant land animals after the dinosaurs died out.
Flowering plants appeared during the Cretaceous period.
The first land plants appeared about 470 million years ago.
Weather fronts are shown on maps with symbols: blue triangles for cold fronts and red half circles for warm fronts.
Low pressure systems are shown with an L and high pressure systems with an H on weather maps.
Winds blow counterclockwise around low pressure in the Northern Hemisphere.
Winds blow clockwise around high pressure in the Northern Hemisphere.
The prevailing winds in most of the United States are westerlies that blow from west to east.
Polar easterlies blow near the poles.
The doldrums are calm areas near the equator.
The horse latitudes are calm areas near 30 degrees latitude where many deserts are found.
Air near the equator is heated, rises, and creates a band of rain.
Precipitation is greatest near the equator and lowest near 30 degrees latitude and the poles.
Ocean water is warmest near the equator and coldest near the poles.
The California Current is a cold current that flows south along the west coast of the United States.
Cold ocean currents make nearby coastal climates cooler and drier.
Warm ocean currents make nearby coastal climates warmer and wetter.
Organisms are classified into three domains: Bacteria, Archaea, and Eukarya.
Archaea are single celled organisms that can live in extreme environments such as hot springs.
The six kingdoms are animals, plants, fungi, protists, eubacteria, and archaebacteria.
Organisms in the same genus are closely related.
The more classification levels two organisms share, the more closely related they are.
A dichotomous key asks a series of yes or no questions about traits to identify an organism.
Scientists classify animals by their body structures, such as backbones, body coverings, and number of legs.
Scientists now also use DNA to classify organisms.
Chordates are animals with a nerve cord, and most are vertebrates.
Annelids are segmented worms such as earthworms and leeches.
Flatworms such as planaria and tapeworms have flat bodies.
Roundworms have round bodies and many are parasites.
Cnidarians such as jellyfish, corals, and sea anemones have stinging cells.
Echinoderms such as sea stars and sea urchins have spiny skin and tube feet.
Arthropods are the largest group of animals and include insects, spiders, and crustaceans.
Insects are the most numerous animals on Earth.
Beetles are the largest group of insects.
Centipedes and millipedes are arthropods with many legs.
Vertebrates have an internal skeleton called an endoskeleton.
Fish were the first vertebrates.
Amphibians were the first vertebrates to live on land.
Reptiles were the first vertebrates to lay eggs with shells on land.
Birds evolved from reptiles and have scales on their legs.
Mammals have hair, mammary glands, and three middle ear bones.
Monotremes are mammals that lay eggs, such as the platypus.
Placental mammals develop inside the mother attached to a placenta.
Primates include monkeys, apes, and humans and have grasping hands.
Rodents such as mice, rats, squirrels, and beavers have front teeth that never stop growing.
Carnivorous mammals have sharp canine teeth for tearing meat.
Herbivorous mammals have flat molars for grinding plants.
Cows have stomachs with four chambers to digest grass.
Insects breathe through tiny holes called spiracles.
Spiders breathe through book lungs.
Fish have a swim bladder that helps them float.
Fish have a lateral line that senses movement in the water.
Birds have air sacs that help them breathe while flying.
Birds have a crop to store food and a gizzard to grind it.
Snakes can unhinge their jaws to swallow large prey.
Lizards can lose their tails to escape predators and grow new ones.
Frogs absorb water through their skin and do not need to drink.
Salamanders can regrow lost limbs.
Turtles have shells that are part of their skeleton.
Crocodiles have eyes and nostrils on top of their heads so they can see and breathe while mostly underwater.
Plants are classified into nonvascular plants, seedless vascular plants, gymnosperms, and angiosperms.
Angiosperms are the largest group of plants and have flowers.
Monocots, such as grasses and corn, have parallel leaf veins and one seed leaf.
Dicots, such as beans and oaks, have branching leaf veins and two seed leaves.
Gymnosperms such as pines, firs, and spruces have naked seeds in cones.
Mosses absorb water directly through their surfaces.
Ferns have roots, stems, and leaves called fronds, but no seeds.
Plants need light, water, carbon dioxide, and minerals.
Plants make glucose, which they use for energy and to build cellulose.
Cellulose is the main material of plant cell walls.
Wood is made of xylem cells with thick walls.
The tip of a root is protected by a root cap.
Some plants store water in their leaves, such as aloe.
Some plants climb using tendrils, such as peas and grapes.
Ivy and vines climb walls and trees to reach light.
Some plants have shallow roots, while others have deep taproots that reach groundwater.
Mangrove trees have roots that grow above the water to get oxygen.
Plants in cold climates are often small and grow close to the ground.
Some plants close their flowers at night.
Morning glories open their flowers in the morning.
Flowers that bloom at night are often white and scented to attract moths.
Red tube shaped flowers often attract hummingbirds.
Plants can be grown from seeds, cuttings, bulbs, tubers, or runners.
Grafting joins part of one plant onto another.
Seeds can stay dormant for many years until conditions are right.
Some seeds need fire or freezing to germinate.
Fruits can be fleshy like apples or dry like nuts.
A peanut is a seed that grows underground.
Animal behaviors include feeding, mating, defending territory, caring for young, and communicating.
Courtship behaviors help animals attract mates.
Territorial behavior helps animals protect food and nesting areas.
Social animals such as wolves, elephants, and bees live and work in groups.
Circadian rhythms are daily cycles of behavior such as sleeping and waking.
Some animals respond to the Moon's cycles, such as some corals spawning.
Animals respond to stimuli such as light, temperature, sound, touch, and chemicals.
Moths fly toward light.
Earthworms move away from light.
Pill bugs prefer dark, damp places.
Plants and animals respond to changes in day length with the seasons.
Chickens produce more eggs when days are longer.
Stimulus and response help organisms survive.
Cells in a multicellular organism communicate with chemical signals such as hormones.
Organ systems work together to keep the body in balance.
The nervous system and endocrine system both send signals that control the body.
Homeostasis keeps body temperature, water, and blood sugar in balance.
When blood sugar rises, the pancreas releases insulin.
When the body is too hot, blood vessels near the skin widen and sweating begins.
When the body is too cold, blood vessels near the skin narrow and shivering begins.
The kidneys keep water and salt levels in balance.
Thirst signals the body that it needs water.
Hunger signals the body that it needs food.
The genetic material DNA is copied before a cell divides.
In sexual reproduction, each parent passes on half of its genetic material.
Genetic variation comes from mutations and the shuffling of genes during sexual reproduction.
Purebred organisms have two identical alleles for a trait.
Hybrids have two different alleles for a trait.
The probability of a child being a boy or a girl is about 50 percent.
A carrier has one recessive allele for a disorder but does not show it.
Traits that are influenced by the environment include height, weight, and skin color from sun exposure.
Plants grown in poor soil may be shorter even if they have genes for tallness.
Animals of the same species can look different because of variation.
Dogs of different breeds are the same species.
A mule is the offspring of a horse and a donkey and is usually sterile.
Electric charge can be positive or negative.
Electrons can move from one object to another when the objects are rubbed together.
An object that gains electrons becomes negatively charged.
An object that loses electrons becomes positively charged.
A charged object can attract small bits of paper or a stream of water.
A neutral object has equal amounts of positive and negative charge.
Static charges build up more easily in dry air.
Grounding allows extra charge to flow safely into the Earth.
Static discharge is the sudden movement of charges, like a spark or lightning.
An electric current is measured with an ammeter connected in series.
Voltage is measured with a voltmeter connected in parallel.
Current increases when voltage increases or resistance decreases.
A resistor reduces the current in a circuit.
A light bulb filament is a resistor that glows when current passes through it.
Batteries in a flashlight are placed end to end in series to add their voltages.
A dead battery has used up its chemical energy.
Rechargeable batteries can be recharged by running current through them in reverse.
A generator in a power plant spins a coil of wire inside a magnetic field.
Power plants send electricity through high voltage transmission lines.
Electric meters measure how much electrical energy a home uses.
Kilowatt hours measure electrical energy use.
A 100 watt bulb uses more energy per hour than a 60 watt bulb.
Leaving electronics on standby still uses some electricity.
A permanent magnet keeps its magnetism, while a temporary magnet loses it.
Magnetic materials have tiny regions called domains that line up when magnetized.
Lodestone is a natural magnet made of magnetite.
Ancient sailors used lodestone compasses to navigate.
Earth's magnetic north pole is near, but not exactly at, the geographic North Pole.
Animals such as birds and sea turtles may use Earth's magnetic field to navigate during migration.
A force has both size and direction.
Forces are shown with arrows; a longer arrow means a stronger force.
When forces are balanced, the net force is zero and motion does not change.
A rocket in space keeps moving without engines because there is no friction.
Mass is a measure of inertia.
Doubling the force on an object doubles its acceleration.
Doubling the mass of an object halves its acceleration for the same force.
Every force has an equal and opposite reaction force.
A jet engine pushes air backward, and the air pushes the jet forward.
When a gun is fired, the gun recoils backward.
A person stepping off a boat pushes the boat away from the dock.
Static friction keeps a resting object from starting to move.
Sliding friction acts on an object sliding across a surface.
Rolling friction is smaller than sliding friction.
Fluid friction acts on objects moving through water or air.
Streamlined shapes reduce fluid friction.
Cars, boats, and airplanes are streamlined to reduce drag.
Lubricants such as oil and grease make surfaces slippery and reduce friction.
Friction between tires and the road lets a car turn and stop.
Wet or icy roads reduce friction and make cars skid.
Gravity on Earth makes falling objects accelerate at about 9.8 meters per second every second.
A ball rolling on a flat floor eventually stops because of friction.
An object's speed is how fast it moves; its velocity includes direction.
Average speed equals total distance divided by total time.
Distance equals speed multiplied by time.
Time equals distance divided by speed.
A car traveling at 60 kilometers per hour goes 60 kilometers in one hour.
A runner who covers 400 meters in 80 seconds has a speed of 5 meters per second.
A train going 100 kilometers per hour for 3 hours travels 300 kilometers.
A speed time graph with a rising line shows an object speeding up.
Work equals force times distance.
Lifting a 10 newton box 2 meters does 20 joules of work.
Machines can change the size of a force, the direction of a force, or the distance over which it acts.
A lever with the fulcrum close to the load needs less effort force.
A class one lever has the fulcrum between the effort and the load, like a seesaw.
A class two lever has the load between the fulcrum and the effort, like a wheelbarrow.
A class three lever has the effort between the fulcrum and the load, like a broom or your forearm.
A movable pulley attached to the load cuts the effort force in half.
A block and tackle uses several pulleys to lift very heavy loads.
The threads of a screw are an inclined plane wrapped around a post.
A zipper uses wedges to open and close.
The teeth of a saw are wedges.
A ramp for wheelchairs is an inclined plane.
A doorknob is a wheel and axle.
Gears can increase speed or increase force.
A bicycle chain connects gears to transfer force from the pedals to the wheel.
Thermal energy flows until two objects reach thermal equilibrium at the same temperature.
A calorimeter measures the heat released or absorbed in a reaction.
Melting ice absorbs heat from its surroundings, which is why ice cools a drink.
Evaporation is a cooling process because the fastest particles escape.
Refrigerators move heat from inside to outside using a liquid that evaporates and condenses.
A heat pump moves heat from one place to another.
Insulation in an attic keeps heat in during winter and out during summer.
Thermal energy from the Sun warms the oceans, land, and air.
Energy stored in a battery is chemical potential energy.
Energy stored in a dam's reservoir is gravitational potential energy.
Energy stored in a stretched bow is elastic potential energy.
Energy stored in the nucleus of uranium atoms is nuclear energy.
Electrical energy can be stored in batteries and capacitors.
Wind is moving air that has kinetic energy.
Ocean waves have kinetic energy.
Moving water in a river has kinetic energy.
Light energy can be changed into heat when it is absorbed.
Light energy can be changed into electrical energy by solar cells.
Light energy can be changed into chemical energy by plants.
Sound energy can be changed into electrical energy by a microphone.
Electrical energy can be changed into sound energy by a speaker.
Mechanical energy can be changed into electrical energy by a generator.
Electrical energy can be changed into mechanical energy by a motor.
Chemical energy can be changed into electrical energy by a battery.
Chemical energy can be changed into thermal energy by burning.
Nuclear energy can be changed into thermal energy in a nuclear reactor.
Kinetic energy can be changed into thermal energy by friction.
A wave's energy depends on its amplitude.
The speed of a wave depends on the medium it travels through.
Light slows down when it enters water or glass.
Lenses work by refraction.
Mirrors work by reflection.
A concave mirror can make an image larger, as in a makeup mirror.
A convex lens can focus sunlight onto a small spot and start a fire.
Microscopes use two convex lenses to magnify tiny objects.
Refracting telescopes use lenses, and reflecting telescopes use mirrors.
Light waves can be polarized, which is how some sunglasses reduce glare.
An echo is used by bats, dolphins, and sonar to locate objects.
Sound reflects well from hard flat surfaces like walls and cliffs.
Ultrasonic sounds are too high for humans to hear.
Infrasonic sounds are too low for humans to hear; elephants and whales use them.
Noise canceling headphones use sound waves that cancel other waves.
Hearing loss can be caused by loud music and machinery.
Wearing ear protection reduces damage from loud sounds.
Musical instruments produce different pitches by changing the length, tension, or thickness of the vibrating part.
A trombone changes pitch by changing the length of its tube.
A flute changes pitch when the player covers holes, changing the length of the air column.
A xylophone has bars of different lengths; shorter bars make higher notes.
A larger drum makes a lower sound than a smaller drum.
Climate is the long term pattern of weather in an area, including average temperature and precipitation.
Latitude, altitude, distance from oceans, ocean currents, and mountains affect climate.
Places near the equator have warm climates because they receive direct sunlight all year.
Places near the poles have cold climates because sunlight strikes them at a low angle.
The higher the altitude, the colder the climate.
Mountains can block moist air, causing heavy rain on the windward side and a dry rain shadow on the leeward side.
Deserts often form in the rain shadow of mountains.
Coastal areas have milder climates than inland areas because water heats and cools slowly.
The Gulf Stream is a warm ocean current that carries warm water from the Gulf of Mexico north along the Atlantic coast toward Europe.
The Gulf Stream makes the climate of western Europe warmer than other places at the same latitude.
Ocean currents carry heat from the equator toward the poles.
Surface ocean currents are driven mainly by wind.
Deep ocean currents are driven by differences in water density caused by temperature and salinity.
Cold, salty water is denser and sinks, which drives deep ocean currents.
Upwelling brings cold, nutrient rich water from the deep ocean to the surface, which supports many fish.
Ocean water is salty because rivers carry dissolved salts and minerals from rocks into the ocean.
Salinity is the amount of salt dissolved in water.
The average salinity of ocean water is about 35 parts per thousand.
Salt water is denser than fresh water, so objects float more easily in salt water.
Salt water freezes at a lower temperature than fresh water.
The ocean covers about 71 percent of Earth's surface.
The Pacific Ocean is the largest and deepest ocean.
The continental shelf is the shallow part of the ocean floor next to a continent.
The continental slope drops steeply from the shelf down to the deep ocean floor.
The abyssal plain is the flat deep ocean floor.
Sunlight only reaches the upper part of the ocean, so photosynthesis happens only near the surface.
Ocean water gets colder, darker, and has higher pressure with depth.
Organisms in the deep ocean are adapted to darkness, cold, and high pressure.
Coral reefs form in warm, shallow, clear tropical ocean water.
Coral reefs are built by tiny animals called coral polyps that make limestone skeletons.
Estuaries are where fresh river water mixes with salt ocean water and are nurseries for many fish.
Tides are the regular rise and fall of ocean water levels.
Tides are caused mainly by the gravitational pull of the Moon on Earth's oceans.
Most coastal places have two high tides and two low tides about every 24 hours.
Spring tides are the highest high tides and occur at new moon and full moon when the Sun, Moon, and Earth line up.
Neap tides have the smallest difference between high and low tide and occur at first and third quarter moon.
Ocean waves are mostly caused by wind blowing over the water surface.
In a water wave, the water moves up and down in circles while the energy moves forward.
Global warming is the increase in Earth's average surface temperature.
Climate change is a long term change in Earth's climate, including temperature and precipitation patterns.
The greenhouse effect is the trapping of heat in the atmosphere by greenhouse gases.
Greenhouse gases include carbon dioxide, methane, water vapor, and nitrous oxide.
Greenhouse gases let sunlight in but absorb infrared radiation, or heat, given off by Earth's surface.
Without the natural greenhouse effect, Earth would be too cold for most life.
Burning fossil fuels such as coal, oil, and gasoline adds carbon dioxide to the atmosphere.
The amount of carbon dioxide in the atmosphere has increased since the Industrial Revolution.
Deforestation increases carbon dioxide because fewer trees remove carbon dioxide by photosynthesis.
Cattle and landfills release methane, a greenhouse gas.
Global warming causes glaciers and polar ice to melt.
Melting glaciers and ice sheets and the expansion of warming water cause sea level to rise.
Rising sea levels can flood coastal areas and islands.
Climate change can cause more droughts, heat waves, stronger storms, and changes in where species can live.
Polar bears are threatened because sea ice they use for hunting is melting.
Reducing the use of fossil fuels and using renewable energy reduces greenhouse gas emissions.
Planting trees removes carbon dioxide from the air.
Ice cores from glaciers contain trapped air bubbles that show past climate and carbon dioxide levels.
Tree rings can show past climate, with wide rings in wet years and narrow rings in dry years.
Fossils of plants and animals give evidence of past climates.
Ice ages were long periods of time when Earth was much colder and glaciers covered large areas.
Large volcanic eruptions can lower global temperatures by putting ash and particles in the atmosphere.
Changes in Earth's orbit and tilt over thousands of years cause natural climate changes.
Biomes are large regions with similar climate, plants, and animals.
Tropical rain forests are hot and wet all year and have the greatest biodiversity of any biome.
Deserts receive less than 25 centimeters of rain per year and have extreme temperatures.
Hot deserts are hot during the day and cold at night because dry air does not hold heat.
Grasslands, prairies, and savannas have grasses and few trees because of moderate rainfall.
Temperate deciduous forests have four seasons and trees that lose their leaves in fall.
The taiga, or boreal forest, has long cold winters and coniferous evergreen trees.
The tundra is a cold, dry, treeless biome with permafrost, a layer of permanently frozen ground.
Tundra plants are short, such as mosses, lichens, and small shrubs.
Polar regions are very cold with ice and snow most of the year.
Freshwater biomes include lakes, ponds, rivers, streams, and wetlands.
Wetlands such as marshes and swamps filter water, control floods, and provide habitats.
Marine biomes include oceans, coral reefs, and estuaries.
Tropical regions near the equator receive the most direct solar energy.
The equator receives about twelve hours of daylight every day of the year.
Arid means very dry, and humid means moist.
A fossil is the preserved remains or traces of an organism that lived long ago.
Fossils form when organisms are buried quickly by sediment such as mud, sand, or volcanic ash.
Hard parts such as bones, shells, and teeth are more likely to become fossils than soft parts.
A mold fossil is a hollow space in rock left after an organism decays.
A cast fossil forms when minerals fill a mold and harden into the shape of the organism.
Petrified fossils form when minerals replace the tissues of an organism, such as petrified wood.
Trace fossils, such as footprints, burrows, and tracks, show the activities of organisms.
Organisms can be preserved whole in amber, which is hardened tree sap, or in ice or tar pits.
Insects trapped in tree sap can be preserved in amber.
Carbon films are thin fossils of leaves or soft bodied animals left as a layer of carbon.
Fossils provide evidence of organisms that lived in the past and how environments have changed.
Fossils show that many kinds of organisms that once lived on Earth are now extinct.
Fossils of fish or shells in a desert show that the area was once covered by water.
Fossils of tropical plants in a cold place show that the climate there was once warm.
Fossils of ferns and palm trees show a warm, wet climate in the past.
Paleontologists are scientists who study fossils.
The fossil record shows that life on Earth has changed over time.
The fossil record is incomplete because most organisms decay before they can become fossils.
Extinction happens when every member of a species dies out.
Dinosaurs became extinct about 66 million years ago, probably after a large asteroid struck Earth.
Mass extinctions are events when many species die out in a short time.
Species can become extinct because of climate change, habitat loss, disease, new predators, or competition.
The dodo bird, the passenger pigeon, and the woolly mammoth are extinct.
Endangered species are species at risk of becoming extinct.
In undisturbed rock layers, the oldest layers are at the bottom and the youngest layers are at the top.
The law of superposition states that each rock layer is older than the layer above it.
Relative dating determines whether a rock or fossil is older or younger than another.
Absolute dating uses radioactive decay to find the actual age of a rock in years.
Radioactive elements decay at a steady rate called a half life.
Carbon 14 dating is used to find the age of once living things up to about 50,000 years old.
Index fossils are fossils of organisms that lived for a short time over a wide area and are used to date rock layers.
Trilobites are common index fossils of ancient sea animals.
Rock layers in different places can be matched using index fossils.
An intrusion of igneous rock is younger than the rock layers it cuts through.
A fault is younger than the rock layers it cuts across.
An unconformity is a gap in the rock record where layers were eroded away.
The geologic time scale divides Earth's history into eons, eras, periods, and epochs.
The Precambrian is the longest span of Earth's history.
