The Paleozoic Era is known for the rise of fish, amphibians, and land plants.
The Mesozoic Era is called the age of reptiles because dinosaurs lived then.
The Cenozoic Era is the age of mammals and is the era we live in now.
The first living things on Earth were simple single celled organisms like bacteria.
Cyanobacteria were early organisms that produced oxygen by photosynthesis and changed Earth's atmosphere.
Early Earth's atmosphere had little oxygen.
Life first appeared in the oceans.
Simple organisms appear in older rock layers and more complex organisms appear in younger layers.
Humans appeared very recently in Earth's history.
Coal formed from ancient swamp plants buried and compressed over millions of years.
Oil and natural gas formed from the remains of tiny sea organisms buried and heated over millions of years.
Comparing fossils with living organisms shows how species have changed.
The fossils of horses show how horses changed in size and foot structure over millions of years.
Fossil evidence shows that whales evolved from land mammals.
Archaeopteryx is a fossil that shows features of both reptiles and birds.
Birds are thought to have evolved from dinosaurs.
Mammoths and saber toothed cats lived during the last ice age.
The La Brea Tar Pits preserved many ice age animals.
Scientists use fossils to learn what ancient organisms ate and how they lived.
Sharp pointed teeth in a fossil skull suggest the animal was a meat eating carnivore.
Flat grinding teeth in a fossil suggest the animal was a plant eating herbivore.
Earth has layers: the crust, the mantle, the outer core, and the inner core.
The crust is the thin, solid, outermost layer of Earth.
The crust is the thinnest layer of Earth.
Oceanic crust is thinner and denser than continental crust and is made mostly of basalt.
Continental crust is thicker and less dense than oceanic crust and is made mostly of granite.
The mantle is the thickest layer of Earth, found between the crust and the core.
The mantle is made of hot rock that flows slowly like a thick liquid.
The asthenosphere is the soft, partly melted layer of the upper mantle on which plates move.
The lithosphere is the rigid outer layer made of the crust and the uppermost mantle.
The outer core is made of liquid iron and nickel.
The inner core is a solid ball of iron and nickel and is the hottest layer of Earth.
The inner core is solid because of the very high pressure, even though it is extremely hot.
Temperature and pressure increase with depth inside Earth.
The movement of liquid iron in the outer core produces Earth's magnetic field.
Scientists learn about Earth's interior by studying seismic waves from earthquakes.
Seismic waves change speed and direction when they pass through different layers of Earth.
P waves are primary waves that travel through solids, liquids, and gases and are the fastest seismic waves.
S waves are secondary waves that travel only through solids and cannot pass through the liquid outer core.
Surface waves travel along Earth's surface and cause the most damage during an earthquake.
The theory of plate tectonics states that Earth's lithosphere is broken into large plates that move slowly.
Tectonic plates move a few centimeters each year, about as fast as fingernails grow.
Convection currents in the mantle move the tectonic plates.
Heat from Earth's interior drives convection currents in the mantle.
Alfred Wegener proposed the hypothesis of continental drift.
Continental drift is the idea that the continents were once joined and have slowly moved apart.
Pangaea was a supercontinent made of all the continents joined together about 200 million years ago.
Evidence for continental drift includes matching fossils, rock types, and coastlines on different continents.
The coastlines of South America and Africa fit together like puzzle pieces.
Fossils of the same land reptile, Mesosaurus, found in Africa and South America support continental drift.
Fossils of tropical plants found in Antarctica show that the continent was once in a warmer place.
Coal deposits found in Antarctica show that it once had a warm climate with swamps.
Sea floor spreading happens at mid ocean ridges where magma rises and forms new oceanic crust.
Rocks are youngest near a mid ocean ridge and get older farther away from the ridge.
Magnetic stripes on the ocean floor are evidence of sea floor spreading.
The Mid Atlantic Ridge is a divergent boundary where the North American and Eurasian plates move apart.
At a divergent boundary, plates move apart from each other and new crust forms.
At a convergent boundary, plates move toward each other and collide.
At a transform boundary, plates slide past each other sideways.
The San Andreas Fault in California is a transform boundary where plates slide past each other.
Subduction happens when a denser oceanic plate sinks beneath another plate into the mantle.
Deep ocean trenches form where one plate is subducted under another.
The Mariana Trench is the deepest part of the ocean and formed at a subduction zone.
When two continental plates collide, the crust folds and pushes up to form tall mountains.
The Himalaya Mountains formed when the Indian plate collided with the Eurasian plate.
Mount Everest is the tallest mountain on Earth and is in the Himalayas.
Volcanic mountains and island arcs form where an oceanic plate subducts under another plate.
The Andes Mountains formed where an oceanic plate subducts under South America.
Rift valleys form where continental plates pull apart, such as the East African Rift.
The Ring of Fire is a zone of many volcanoes and earthquakes around the edges of the Pacific Ocean.
Most earthquakes and volcanoes occur along the boundaries of tectonic plates.
An earthquake is the shaking of the ground caused by the sudden movement of rock along a fault.
A fault is a break or crack in Earth's crust where rocks move.
Stress builds up in rocks along a fault until the rocks suddenly slip and release energy as an earthquake.
The focus of an earthquake is the point underground where the rock first breaks.
The epicenter is the point on Earth's surface directly above the focus of an earthquake.
A seismograph is an instrument that detects and records earthquake waves.
Scientists use data from at least three seismograph stations to locate the epicenter of an earthquake.
The magnitude of an earthquake is a measure of the energy it releases.
The Richter scale and the moment magnitude scale measure the strength of earthquakes.
Each increase of one on the magnitude scale means about 32 times more energy is released.
Earthquakes can cause landslides, tsunamis, building collapse, and fires.
A tsunami is a huge ocean wave caused by an earthquake, landslide, or volcanic eruption under the sea.
Buildings can be made safer in earthquakes by using flexible materials and strong foundations.
Earthquakes cannot be predicted exactly, but scientists know where they are likely to happen.
Aftershocks are smaller earthquakes that follow a large earthquake.
A volcano forms where magma rises through the crust and erupts at the surface.
Magma rises because it is less dense than the surrounding solid rock.
Volcanoes erupt lava, ash, rock fragments, and gases such as water vapor, carbon dioxide, and sulfur dioxide.
Volcanic ash in the atmosphere can block sunlight and cool Earth's climate for a while.
Explosive eruptions happen when thick magma traps gas, like Mount St. Helens in 1980.
Quiet eruptions with runny lava flows happen when magma is thin, like in Hawaii.
Shield volcanoes are wide with gentle slopes built by many flows of runny lava, such as Mauna Loa.
Cinder cone volcanoes are small steep volcanoes made of ash and cinders.
Composite volcanoes, or stratovolcanoes, are tall cones made of layers of lava and ash.
The Hawaiian Islands formed as the Pacific plate moved over a hot spot in the mantle.
A hot spot is an area where magma rises from deep in the mantle far from a plate boundary.
The Hawaiian islands are older toward the northwest and the youngest island has active volcanoes.
New land can form when lava from volcanoes cools and hardens.
Volcanic soil is rich in minerals and good for farming.
Geysers and hot springs form where groundwater is heated by magma underground.
An active volcano is erupting or likely to erupt, a dormant volcano could erupt again, and an extinct volcano will not erupt.
Scientists who study volcanoes are called volcanologists.
Scientists monitor volcanoes by measuring small earthquakes, gas, and swelling of the ground.
Folding happens when rock layers bend under pressure without breaking.
Uplift is the raising of land by forces inside Earth.
Rock layers that are tilted, folded, or faulted were moved after they formed.
Fossils of sea animals found high in mountains show that the rock once formed under the sea and was uplifted.
Earth's crust is constantly changing because of plate movement, weathering, and erosion.
Earth is about 4.6 billion years old.
The core is made mostly of iron and nickel.
The crust is made mostly of oxygen and silicon, found in silicate minerals.
Oxygen is the most common element in Earth's crust.
Earth's interior is hot because of heat left over from its formation and the decay of radioactive elements.
Geothermal energy is heat energy from inside Earth.
Latitude lines run east and west and measure distance north or south of the equator in degrees.
Longitude lines run north and south and measure distance east or west of the prime meridian.
The equator is at 0 degrees latitude, and the North Pole is at 90 degrees north latitude.
The prime meridian is at 0 degrees longitude and passes through Greenwich, England.
A map scale shows how distance on a map relates to actual distance on Earth.
A map legend, or key, explains the symbols used on a map.
A compass rose shows directions on a map: north, south, east, and west.
A topographic map shows the elevation and shape of the land with contour lines.
Elevation is the height of land above sea level.
Relief is the difference in elevation between the highest and lowest points in an area.
A watershed is an area of land that drains into a particular river or lake.
A divide is high land that separates watersheds.
Rivers flow downhill from higher elevation to lower elevation.
Streams join to form rivers, and rivers flow into lakes or oceans.
Earth is the only planet with plate tectonics known to have liquid water oceans.
Earth's shape is a sphere slightly flattened at the poles.
Evidence that Earth is round includes ships disappearing over the horizon hull first and Earth's curved shadow on the Moon during a lunar eclipse.
Photographs from space show that Earth is round.
Earth's circumference is about 40,000 kilometers.
The continents are North America, South America, Europe, Asia, Africa, Australia, and Antarctica.
The oceans are the Pacific, Atlantic, Indian, Arctic, and Southern oceans.
Asia is the largest continent.
The Nile is one of the longest rivers in the world.
The Sahara is the largest hot desert in the world.
Antarctica is the coldest, windiest, and driest continent and is covered by an ice sheet.
New York State landscapes include the Adirondack Mountains, the Catskills, the Hudson River, the Finger Lakes, and Long Island.
The Finger Lakes in New York were carved by glaciers.
Long Island was formed from glacial deposits called moraines.
The Adirondack Mountains are made of very old metamorphic and igneous rock.
Niagara Falls formed as water eroded soft rock under a layer of hard rock.
Niagara Falls is slowly moving upstream because of erosion.
The Hudson River flows south to New York Harbor and the Atlantic Ocean.
Glaciers left many boulders and lakes across New York State.
The Great Lakes contain about one fifth of the fresh surface water on Earth.
Lake Erie and Lake Ontario border New York State.
The Appalachian Mountains are old mountains that have been worn down by erosion.
The Rocky Mountains are younger and taller than the Appalachian Mountains.
The Mississippi River is the largest river system in North America.
Yellowstone National Park has geysers and hot springs heated by magma below.
Old Faithful is a geyser in Yellowstone that erupts regularly.
The Grand Canyon exposes rock layers that record millions of years of Earth's history.
Death Valley is the lowest and hottest place in North America.
Mount Everest is the highest point on Earth's surface.
The Dead Sea is very salty, so people float easily in it.
The Great Salt Lake in Utah is saltier than the ocean because water evaporates and leaves salt behind.
Hawaii is made of volcanic islands in the middle of the Pacific Ocean.
Alaska has many glaciers, volcanoes, and earthquakes.
Florida has a warm humid climate and many hurricanes.
Texas has tornadoes, hurricanes, deserts, and plains.
California has earthquakes, wildfires, droughts, and a long coastline.
The Pacific Northwest has a rainy climate because moist air from the ocean rises over mountains.
Tornado Alley includes states such as Texas, Oklahoma, Kansas, and Nebraska.
The Gulf of Mexico is a source of warm, moist air for storms in the southern United States.
Air masses from Canada bring cold, dry air to the United States in winter.
Lake effect snow falls downwind of the Great Lakes when cold air passes over warmer lake water.
Buffalo, New York gets heavy lake effect snow from Lake Erie.
Ocean temperatures change more slowly than land temperatures.
The deepest part of the ocean is the Mariana Trench in the Pacific Ocean.
Mid ocean ridges are underwater mountain ranges.
Most of Earth's volcanoes are under the ocean.
The ocean floor is made of basalt.
Seamounts are underwater volcanoes.
Coral atolls form around sinking volcanic islands.
Barrier islands protect the mainland coast from storms.
Sand on beaches comes from weathered and eroded rocks and shells.
Black sand beaches in Hawaii are made of eroded basalt.
White sand beaches are often made of quartz or broken shells and coral.
Erosion of the Appalachian Mountains supplied sediment to the coastal plain.
Bedrock in an area can be seen in road cuts and outcrops.
Rock layers can be bent into folds or broken by faults.
Joints are cracks in rock where no movement has occurred.
The oldest rocks on Earth are about 4 billion years old.
Meteorites help scientists estimate the age of the solar system.
Earth formed about 4.6 billion years ago from a cloud of gas and dust.
Early Earth was very hot and covered with volcanoes and molten rock.
Oceans formed as water vapor from volcanoes cooled and condensed into rain.
Earth's early atmosphere had little oxygen until photosynthetic organisms added it.
Ozone formed in the atmosphere after oxygen built up, protecting land life from ultraviolet rays.
Over geologic time, continents have collided and separated many times.
India was once an island that moved north and collided with Asia.
The Atlantic Ocean is getting wider each year because of sea floor spreading.
The Pacific Ocean is getting smaller because its floor is subducting at trenches.
The Himalayas are still rising because India continues to push into Asia.
A seismic wave travels faster through solid rock than through melted rock.
The Moho is the boundary between the crust and the mantle.
Earth's magnetic field has reversed many times in the past.
Rocks on the sea floor record reversals of Earth's magnetic field.
Ice sheets once covered northern North America and Europe about 20,000 years ago.
As glaciers melted at the end of the ice age, sea level rose.
Sea level rises when glaciers melt and falls when glaciers grow.
Groundwater can dissolve rock and form caves.
Water flows faster through gravel than through clay.
A spring is a place where groundwater flows out onto the surface.
An artesian well flows without pumping because of pressure in the aquifer.
Pumping too much groundwater can lower the water table and dry up wells.
Saltwater can seep into wells near the coast when too much groundwater is pumped.
Permafrost is permanently frozen soil found in the tundra.
Thawing permafrost releases methane and carbon dioxide.
Weathering is faster in hot humid climates such as rain forests.
Soils in tropical rain forests are thin and poor in nutrients because heavy rain washes nutrients away.
Grassland soils are deep and fertile.
The prairies of the central United States have rich soil good for growing wheat and corn.
Rocks are made of one or more minerals.
A mineral is a naturally occurring inorganic solid with a definite chemical composition and a crystal structure.
Minerals are identified by properties such as hardness, luster, streak, color, cleavage, and crystal shape.
Hardness is a mineral's resistance to being scratched.
The Mohs hardness scale ranks minerals from 1, the softest, to 10, the hardest.
Talc is the softest mineral on the Mohs scale with a hardness of 1.
Diamond is the hardest mineral with a hardness of 10 on the Mohs scale.
A harder mineral can scratch a softer mineral.
Quartz has a hardness of 7 and can scratch glass.
A fingernail has a hardness of about 2.5 and a steel nail or knife about 5.5.
Gypsum has a hardness of 2 and can be scratched by a fingernail.
Calcite has a hardness of 3 and fizzes or bubbles when acid such as vinegar is dropped on it.
The acid test identifies calcite and limestone because they fizz and give off carbon dioxide bubbles.
Luster describes how the surface of a mineral reflects light, such as metallic, glassy, or dull.
Streak is the color of the powder a mineral leaves when it is rubbed on a streak plate of white tile.
The streak of a mineral is more reliable for identification than its color.
Hematite has a reddish brown streak even when the sample looks black or silver.
Pyrite is called fool's gold because it looks like gold but has a greenish black streak.
Cleavage is the tendency of a mineral to split along flat smooth surfaces.
Fracture is when a mineral breaks with rough or curved uneven surfaces.
Mica splits into thin flat sheets because of its cleavage.
Magnetite is a mineral that is magnetic and attracts a magnet.
Halite is rock salt and has cubic crystals.
Crystals form when melted rock or a solution cools and the atoms arrange in a repeating pattern.
Large crystals form when magma cools slowly deep underground.
Small crystals form when lava cools quickly at the surface.
Density of a mineral is its mass divided by its volume.
There are three main types of rock: igneous, sedimentary, and metamorphic.
Igneous rock forms when melted rock, magma or lava, cools and hardens.
Magma is melted rock below Earth's surface.
Lava is melted rock that reaches Earth's surface.
Intrusive igneous rocks form from magma that cools slowly underground and have large crystals, such as granite.
Extrusive igneous rocks form from lava that cools quickly on the surface and have small crystals, such as basalt.
Granite is a coarse grained intrusive igneous rock made of quartz, feldspar, and mica.
Basalt is a dark fine grained extrusive igneous rock that forms from lava and makes up the ocean floor.
Obsidian is volcanic glass that forms when lava cools so fast that no crystals form.
Pumice is a light igneous rock full of holes from gas bubbles and it can float on water.
Sedimentary rock forms when sediments are deposited, compacted, and cemented together.
Sediments are small pieces of rock, sand, mud, and the remains of living things.
Compaction and cementation turn layers of sediment into sedimentary rock.
Sedimentary rocks form in layers, often at the bottom of oceans, lakes, and rivers.
Most fossils are found in sedimentary rock.
Sandstone is a sedimentary rock made of sand grains cemented together.
Shale is a sedimentary rock made of clay and mud particles.
Conglomerate is a sedimentary rock made of rounded pebbles and gravel cemented together.
Limestone is a sedimentary rock made mostly of calcite, often from shells and skeletons of sea organisms.
Coal is a sedimentary rock formed from the remains of plants buried in swamps millions of years ago.
Rock salt and gypsum form when sea water evaporates and leaves minerals behind.
Metamorphic rock forms when existing rock is changed by heat and pressure deep inside Earth.
Metamorphic rocks form without melting the rock.
Marble is a metamorphic rock that forms from limestone.
Slate is a metamorphic rock that forms from shale.
Quartzite is a metamorphic rock that forms from sandstone.
Gneiss is a banded metamorphic rock that can form from granite.
Foliated metamorphic rocks have minerals lined up in bands or layers.
The rock cycle is the process by which rocks change from one type into another over time.
Any type of rock can become any other type of rock in the rock cycle.
Weathering and erosion break igneous, metamorphic, and sedimentary rock into sediments.
Melting turns rock into magma, which cools to form igneous rock.
Heat and pressure change igneous or sedimentary rock into metamorphic rock.
The rock cycle is driven by energy from Earth's interior heat and by energy from the Sun.
Rocks are classified by how they formed.
The texture of a rock describes the size, shape, and arrangement of its grains or crystals.
Geologists are scientists who study rocks, minerals, and the history of Earth.
Ores are rocks that contain useful metals such as iron, copper, or aluminum that can be mined.
Gems such as diamonds, rubies, and emeralds are valuable minerals.
Soil is a mixture of weathered rock particles, humus, minerals, water, and air.
Humus is the dark organic material in soil made from decayed plants and animals.
Decomposers such as bacteria, fungi, and earthworms break down dead matter and add nutrients to soil.
Earthworms loosen soil and make tunnels that let air and water reach plant roots.
Soil forms slowly from the weathering of bedrock over hundreds or thousands of years.
Topsoil is the top layer of soil and contains the most humus and nutrients.
Subsoil lies below topsoil and contains more clay and minerals but less humus.
Bedrock is the solid rock layer beneath the soil.
Sand particles are the largest soil particles, silt is medium, and clay particles are the smallest.
Sandy soil drains water quickly and does not hold much water.
Clay soil holds water and drains slowly because its particles are tiny and packed tightly.
Loam is a soil that is a mixture of sand, silt, clay, and humus and is good for growing plants.
Soil with a lot of humus holds water well and is rich in nutrients for plants.
Plant roots hold soil in place and help prevent erosion.
Soil provides plants with water, nutrients, minerals, and support for their roots.
The permeability of soil or rock is how easily water can flow through it.
Porosity is the amount of open space between particles in soil or rock.
Gravel and sand are permeable and let water pass through quickly.
Clay is nearly impermeable and water passes through it very slowly.
Groundwater is water that soaks into the ground and fills the spaces between rock and soil particles.
An aquifer is an underground layer of permeable rock or sediment that holds groundwater.
A well is drilled down into an aquifer to bring up groundwater.
The water table is the top of the zone where the ground is saturated with water.
Composting turns food scraps and yard waste into humus that enriches soil.
Crop rotation, contour plowing, terracing, and planting cover crops help conserve soil.
Planting trees and grass helps stop soil erosion by wind and water.
Rocks can be sorted by color, size, texture, shape, hardness, and the minerals they contain.
A hand lens or magnifying glass helps observe the grains and crystals in a rock.
Pebbles in a river are smooth and rounded because they are worn down by tumbling in moving water.
Sharp angular rock pieces have not been carried far by water.
Fossil fuels such as coal, oil, and natural gas are found in sedimentary rock.
Geodes are hollow rocks lined with crystals.
Minerals form when magma cools, when water evaporates, or when dissolved minerals come out of solution.
Stalactites and stalagmites form in caves when dripping water deposits calcite.
Caves often form when acidic groundwater dissolves limestone.
Sinkholes form when underground limestone dissolves and the ground above collapses.
Gold, silver, and copper are metals that are found as minerals in rocks.
Mining removes useful rocks, minerals, and fuels from the ground.
Iron ore is mined to make steel.
Bauxite is the ore of aluminum.
Clay is used to make bricks, pottery, and ceramics.
Sand is used to make glass and concrete.
Granite and marble are used as building stone.
Rocks and minerals are nonrenewable resources because they form very slowly.
The Mohs scale in order is talc 1, gypsum 2, calcite 3, fluorite 4, apatite 5, feldspar 6, quartz 7, topaz 8, corundum 9, and diamond 10.
Topaz, corundum, and diamond are harder than quartz and can scratch quartz.
Talc, gypsum, and calcite are softer than quartz and are scratched by quartz.
Fluorite has a hardness of 4 and apatite has a hardness of 5.
Feldspar has a hardness of 6 and is one of the most common minerals in Earth's crust.
Corundum, which includes rubies and sapphires, has a hardness of 9.
A mineral can scratch any mineral with a lower hardness number.
A copper penny has a hardness of about 3.5.
Glass has a hardness of about 5.5.
Weathering is the breaking down of rocks into smaller pieces called sediment.
Erosion is the movement of weathered rock and soil from one place to another.
Deposition is the dropping or settling of sediment carried by water, wind, or ice in a new place.
Weathering breaks rock down, erosion carries it away, and deposition drops it somewhere else.
Mechanical weathering, or physical weathering, breaks rock into smaller pieces without changing its composition.
Chemical weathering changes the minerals in rock into new substances.
Ice wedging happens when water seeps into cracks in rock, freezes, expands, and breaks the rock apart.
Water expands when it freezes, so repeated freezing and thawing cracks rocks and roads.
Frost wedging is most common in places where temperatures go above and below freezing often.
Potholes in roads form when water in cracks freezes and thaws repeatedly.
Plant roots growing into cracks in rocks can break rocks apart, which is mechanical weathering.
Animals that burrow, such as moles and prairie dogs, help weather and loosen rock and soil.
Abrasion is the wearing away of rock by other rock particles carried by wind, water, or ice.
Wind blowing sand against rocks wears them down by abrasion, which causes erosion in deserts.
Running water rounds and smooths rocks and pebbles by abrasion as they tumble in a stream.
Acid rain causes chemical weathering by dissolving rock such as limestone and marble.
Oxidation is chemical weathering in which oxygen combines with iron in rock to form rust.
Rust is iron oxide that forms when iron reacts with oxygen and water.
Carbonic acid forms when carbon dioxide dissolves in rainwater and it slowly dissolves limestone.
Lichens and mosses produce acids that slowly weather rock.
Chemical weathering happens faster in warm wet climates.
Mechanical weathering by ice is fastest in cold climates with freezing and thawing.
Weathering happens faster on rocks that have more surface area exposed.
Soft rocks such as sandstone and limestone weather faster than hard rocks such as granite.
Gravity causes erosion by pulling rock and soil downhill in landslides, mudslides, rockfalls, and slumps.
A landslide is the rapid movement of rock and soil down a steep slope caused by gravity.
Mudslides often happen after heavy rain on steep slopes with few plants.
Water is the main agent of erosion on Earth.
Rivers and streams carry sediment downstream and carve valleys and canyons.
The Grand Canyon was formed by erosion from the Colorado River over millions of years.
Fast moving water can carry larger particles than slow moving water.
When a river slows down it deposits sediment, dropping the largest and heaviest particles first.
A delta is a landform made of sediment deposited where a river flows into a lake or ocean.
The Mississippi River deposits sediment and builds a large delta at the Gulf of Mexico.
An alluvial fan is a fan shaped deposit of sediment where a stream flows out of mountains onto flat land.
A floodplain is the flat land next to a river that is covered with water and sediment during floods.
Floods deposit rich soil on floodplains, which makes the land good for farming.
Meanders are curves or loops that form in a slow moving river.
An oxbow lake forms when a meander of a river is cut off.
A V shaped valley is carved by a river.
A U shaped valley is carved by a glacier.
A glacier is a large mass of ice that moves slowly over land.
Glaciers erode land by plucking and scraping rock as they move.
Glaciers carry rocks and sediment and deposit them when the ice melts.
A moraine is a ridge of rocks and sediment deposited by a glacier.
Erratics are large boulders carried by glaciers and left far from where they came from.
Glaciers leave scratches and grooves, called striations, in bedrock.
The Great Lakes were formed by glaciers that carved basins during the ice age.
Long Island and Cape Cod were formed from sediment deposited by glaciers.
Wind erosion is greatest in dry areas with loose sand and few plants, such as deserts.
Sand dunes are hills of sand deposited by wind.
Windbreaks such as rows of trees reduce wind erosion of soil.
The Dust Bowl happened when drought and poor farming let wind blow away topsoil.
Ocean waves erode the shoreline and form cliffs, sea caves, and sea arches.
Waves and currents move sand along beaches and deposit it to form sandbars and barrier islands.
Beaches are made of sand deposited by waves.
Jetties, seawalls, and breakwaters are built to reduce beach erosion by waves.
Planting grass on sand dunes helps prevent erosion of beaches.
Plants reduce erosion because their roots hold soil in place.
Removing trees and plants from land increases soil erosion.
Terraces on hillsides slow the flow of water and reduce erosion.
Contour plowing across a slope reduces soil erosion by water.
A canyon is a deep narrow valley with steep sides carved by a river.
A mesa is a flat topped landform with steep sides formed by erosion.
A plateau is a large area of flat land that is raised high above the surrounding land.
A plain is a large area of flat or gently rolling land.
A mountain is a landform that rises high above the surrounding land.
A valley is low land between hills or mountains.
An island is land surrounded by water on all sides.
A peninsula is land surrounded by water on three sides.
A volcano is a mountain formed by lava and ash erupting from an opening in Earth's crust.
Landforms are shaped by constructive forces and destructive forces.
Constructive forces build up land, such as deposition, volcanoes, and mountain building.
Destructive forces wear down land, such as weathering, erosion, earthquakes, and landslides.
Erosion and weathering are usually slow processes that take a long time.
Earthquakes, volcanic eruptions, landslides, and floods can change Earth's surface quickly.
Over millions of years, weathering and erosion wear down tall sharp mountains into low rounded hills.
Old mountains such as the Appalachians are rounded and lower because of erosion.
Young mountains such as the Rocky Mountains and the Himalayas are tall with sharp peaks.
Sediment deposited in layers builds up over time and may become sedimentary rock.
A stream table is a model used to study erosion and deposition by running water.
The steeper the slope, the faster water flows and the more erosion it causes.
Heavy rainfall increases runoff and soil erosion.
Runoff is water that flows over the land surface instead of soaking into the ground.
Paved surfaces such as parking lots increase runoff because water cannot soak in.
Water soaks into soil more easily where there are plants and loose soil.
Sediment in rivers makes the water muddy.
Caves and caverns are formed by groundwater dissolving limestone.
Hoodoos and arches in deserts are shaped by wind and water erosion and weathering.
Weathering of rock produces soil over long periods of time.
Erosion can remove fertile topsoil from farms.
A sandbar is a ridge of sand built up by waves or currents.
Icebergs are large pieces of glacier ice that break off and float in the ocean.
Ice sheets cover most of Antarctica and Greenland.
During the ice age glaciers covered much of North America.
Kettle lakes formed where blocks of glacier ice melted in depressions.
Sediment sorted by size suggests it was deposited by water.
Unsorted sediment of many sizes mixed together was deposited by a glacier.
Earth's four main spheres are the geosphere, hydrosphere, atmosphere, and biosphere.
The biosphere includes all living things on Earth and the places where they live.
The hydrosphere includes all the water on Earth, in oceans, lakes, rivers, groundwater, ice, and water vapor.
The geosphere, or lithosphere, is the solid rock and soil part of Earth, including the crust, mantle, and core.
The atmosphere is the layer of gases surrounding Earth.
The cryosphere is the frozen water part of Earth, including glaciers, ice caps, and sea ice.
Earth's spheres interact; for example, rain from the atmosphere weathers rocks of the geosphere.
Plants in the biosphere take carbon dioxide from the atmosphere.
Volcanoes in the geosphere release gases into the atmosphere.
Ocean water in the hydrosphere evaporates into the atmosphere.
Animals in the biosphere drink water from the hydrosphere.
Earth is a system made of parts that interact and affect each other.
A system is a set of connected parts that work together, such as the solar system, an ecosystem, or the human body.
A closed system does not exchange matter with its surroundings, while an open system does.
Earth is mostly a closed system for matter but an open system for energy, receiving energy from the Sun.
A change in one part of a system can affect the other parts.
Cycles in nature include the water cycle, rock cycle, carbon cycle, nitrogen cycle, and oxygen cycle.
In the carbon cycle, carbon moves from the atmosphere into plants by photosynthesis.
In the carbon cycle, carbon returns to the atmosphere when organisms respire, decompose, or burn.
Carbon is stored in fossil fuels, limestone rocks, oceans, forests, and soil.
Burning fossil fuels moves carbon from underground into the atmosphere.
Oceans absorb carbon dioxide from the atmosphere, which makes ocean water more acidic.
In the oxygen cycle, plants release oxygen and animals use it in respiration.
In the nitrogen cycle, bacteria convert nitrogen gas into forms that plants can use.
Lightning can change nitrogen in the air into compounds that fall to the ground with rain.
Animals get nitrogen by eating plants or other animals.
Decomposers return nitrogen to the soil from dead organisms and wastes.
The phosphorus cycle moves phosphorus from rocks to soil to living things.
Earth's energy budget balances incoming solar energy and outgoing heat.
Earth absorbs about half of the sunlight that reaches it; the rest is reflected or absorbed by the atmosphere.
Snow and ice reflect most sunlight, while dark oceans and forests absorb more.
The albedo of a surface is how much sunlight it reflects; fresh snow has a high albedo.
When ice melts, darker land or water is exposed and absorbs more heat, causing more warming.
Clouds can reflect sunlight back to space and also trap heat near the surface.
Sunlight is the source of energy for photosynthesis, the water cycle, and weather.
Weathering and erosion are part of the rock cycle and are driven by the Sun's energy and gravity.
Heat from Earth's interior drives the formation of igneous and metamorphic rocks.
The geologic processes that form mountains are balanced by weathering and erosion that wear them down.
The amount of water on Earth stays about the same because it is recycled in the water cycle.
Water changes state as it moves through the water cycle.
The ocean stores and moves huge amounts of heat around Earth.
The atmosphere and ocean work together to distribute heat from the equator to the poles.
Changes in ocean temperatures, such as El Nino, affect weather patterns worldwide.
Human activities affect all of Earth's spheres.
Natural hazards include earthquakes, volcanic eruptions, tsunamis, landslides, floods, droughts, hurricanes, tornadoes, and wildfires.
Scientists study the history of natural hazards in an area to predict future hazards.
Early warning systems, building codes, and evacuation plans reduce harm from natural hazards.
People should not build homes on floodplains or on unstable slopes to avoid floods and landslides.
Earth's resources are unevenly distributed because of geologic processes.
Oil and natural gas are found where ancient seas once existed.
Metal ores often form near volcanoes and plate boundaries.
Fresh water is unevenly distributed, so some regions have water shortages.
Fertile soil is found in river valleys and floodplains.
Many cities were built near rivers and coasts for water and transportation.
The water cycle is the continuous movement of water between Earth's surface and the atmosphere.
The Sun provides the energy that drives the water cycle.
Gravity pulls rain and snow down to Earth and makes water flow downhill in the water cycle.
Evaporation is the change of liquid water into water vapor, a gas.
Heat from the Sun causes water to evaporate from oceans, lakes, rivers, and soil.
Most evaporation in the water cycle happens from the oceans.
Condensation is the change of water vapor into liquid water when it cools.
Clouds form when water vapor in the air cools and condenses into tiny water droplets or ice crystals.
Precipitation is water that falls from clouds to the ground as rain, snow, sleet, or hail.
Transpiration is the release of water vapor from plant leaves into the air.
Runoff is water that flows over the ground into streams, rivers, lakes, and oceans.
Infiltration is water soaking into the ground to become groundwater.
Water vapor condenses on a cold glass or can, forming drops of water on the outside.
Dew is water vapor that condenses on cool surfaces such as grass at night or early morning.
Frost forms when water vapor turns directly into ice on surfaces colder than freezing.
Fog is a cloud that forms near the ground.
Sublimation is the change of a solid directly into a gas, such as dry ice or snow turning to vapor.
Water evaporates faster when it is warmer, windier, and the air is drier.
A puddle dries up because the water evaporates into the air.
Wet clothes dry faster on a sunny, windy day because of evaporation.
About 97 percent of Earth's water is salt water in the oceans.
Only about 3 percent of Earth's water is fresh water.
Most of Earth's fresh water is frozen in glaciers and ice caps.
Only a small amount of fresh water is available in lakes, rivers, and groundwater for people to use.
Weather is the condition of the atmosphere at a certain place and time.
Weather includes temperature, air pressure, humidity, wind, cloud cover, and precipitation.
Climate is the average weather of a region over many years.
Meteorologists are scientists who study and forecast weather.
A thermometer measures air temperature.
A barometer measures air pressure.
An anemometer measures wind speed.
A wind vane, or weather vane, shows the direction from which the wind blows.
A rain gauge measures the amount of rainfall.
A hygrometer or psychrometer measures humidity.
Weather balloons, satellites, and radar collect data about weather.
Doppler radar tracks the location and movement of precipitation and storms.
Weather satellites take pictures of clouds and storms from space.
Weather maps show fronts, high and low pressure systems, and temperatures.
Isobars are lines on a weather map that connect places with equal air pressure.
Humidity is the amount of water vapor in the air.
Relative humidity is how much water vapor the air holds compared to the most it could hold at that temperature.
Warm air can hold more water vapor than cold air.
The dew point is the temperature at which water vapor condenses into liquid water.
Air pressure is the force of air pushing down on Earth's surface.
Air pressure decreases as altitude increases.
Falling air pressure usually means cloudy, rainy, or stormy weather is coming.
Rising air pressure usually means clear, fair weather is coming.
A high pressure system usually brings clear skies and calm, dry weather.
A low pressure system usually brings clouds, wind, and precipitation.
Wind is the movement of air from areas of high pressure to areas of low pressure.
Wind is caused by the uneven heating of Earth's surface by the Sun.
Warm air is less dense than cold air, so warm air rises and cold air sinks.
A sea breeze blows from the sea to the land during the day because land heats faster than water.
A land breeze blows from the land to the sea at night because land cools faster than water.
Land heats up and cools down faster than water.
Water has a high specific heat, so it heats and cools slowly.
Large bodies of water make the climate of nearby land milder, with cooler summers and warmer winters.
An air mass is a large body of air with similar temperature and humidity throughout.
Air masses that form over oceans are moist, and those that form over land are dry.
Air masses that form near the poles are cold, and those that form near the equator are warm.
A front is the boundary between two different air masses.
A cold front forms when a cold air mass pushes under a warm air mass.
Cold fronts move quickly and bring thunderstorms, heavy rain, and then cooler temperatures.
A warm front forms when a warm air mass moves over a cold air mass.
Warm fronts bring steady light rain or drizzle over a long period and then warmer weather.
A stationary front forms when two air masses meet and neither moves, bringing many days of rain.
An occluded front forms when a cold front overtakes a warm front.
Cumulus clouds are puffy white clouds that usually mean fair weather.
Cumulonimbus clouds are tall dark clouds that bring thunderstorms, lightning, and heavy rain.
Stratus clouds are flat gray layers of clouds that cover the sky and may bring drizzle.
Cirrus clouds are thin wispy clouds high in the sky made of ice crystals.
Nimbus or nimbostratus clouds are dark rain clouds.
Thunderstorms form when warm moist air rises quickly.
Lightning is a giant discharge of static electricity between clouds or between clouds and the ground.
Thunder is the sound caused by lightning rapidly heating and expanding the air.
We see lightning before we hear thunder because light travels much faster than sound.
Hail forms when raindrops are carried up into freezing parts of a thunderstorm again and again and freeze in layers.
Sleet is rain that freezes into ice pellets as it falls through cold air near the ground.
Snow forms when water vapor turns directly into ice crystals in cold clouds.
Freezing rain falls as liquid and freezes when it hits cold surfaces.
A tornado is a rapidly spinning column of air that extends from a thunderstorm cloud to the ground.
Tornadoes have the fastest winds of any storm but cover a small area.
Tornado Alley in the central United States has many tornadoes in spring.
During a tornado, people should go to a basement or an interior room without windows.
A hurricane is a large powerful storm that forms over warm tropical ocean water.
Hurricanes get their energy from warm ocean water and weaken when they move over land or cold water.
The eye of a hurricane is the calm center of the storm.
Hurricanes bring strong winds, heavy rain, flooding, and storm surges.
A storm surge is a rise of ocean water pushed onto the shore by a hurricane's winds.
Hurricanes in the Pacific Ocean are called typhoons.
A blizzard is a severe snowstorm with strong winds and low visibility.
A drought is a long period with little or no rain.
A flood happens when water overflows onto land that is normally dry.
A watch means severe weather is possible, and a warning means severe weather is happening or about to happen.
Weather forecasts are based on data about temperature, air pressure, wind, and humidity.
Weather in the United States usually moves from west to east because of the prevailing westerlies.
The jet stream is a fast band of wind high in the atmosphere that steers weather systems.
The atmosphere is the layer of gases that surrounds Earth.
Air is a mixture of gases, mostly nitrogen and oxygen.
Air is about 78 percent nitrogen, 21 percent oxygen, and small amounts of argon, carbon dioxide, and water vapor.
Nitrogen is the most abundant gas in Earth's atmosphere.
The atmosphere protects living things from harmful ultraviolet radiation and meteors.
The atmosphere traps heat and keeps Earth warm enough for life.
The troposphere is the lowest layer of the atmosphere, where almost all weather occurs.
Temperature decreases as altitude increases in the troposphere.
The stratosphere is the layer above the troposphere and contains the ozone layer.
The ozone layer absorbs harmful ultraviolet radiation from the Sun.
CFCs, chemicals once used in aerosol sprays and refrigerators, damaged the ozone layer.
The mesosphere is the layer where most meteors burn up.
The thermosphere is the hottest layer of the atmosphere, and auroras happen there.
The exosphere is the outermost layer of the atmosphere, where satellites orbit.
The atmosphere becomes thinner, with less air, at higher altitudes.
Mountain climbers need extra oxygen because the air is thin at high altitudes.
The Coriolis effect is caused by Earth's rotation and curves winds and ocean currents.
Global winds are caused by uneven heating of Earth and are curved by Earth's rotation.
The trade winds blow toward the equator in the tropics.
Clouds at night keep the ground warmer by trapping heat.
Clear nights are colder than cloudy nights because heat escapes into space.
Rain is liquid precipitation that falls when cloud droplets grow heavy.
Water vapor is an invisible gas.
Steam that we see is actually tiny droplets of condensed water.
A rainbow forms when sunlight is refracted and reflected by raindrops, separating white light into colors.
A rainbow is seen when the Sun is behind the observer and rain is in front.
Temperature is often measured in degrees Celsius or degrees Fahrenheit.
Water freezes at 0 degrees Celsius, or 32 degrees Fahrenheit.
Water boils at 100 degrees Celsius, or 212 degrees Fahrenheit, at sea level.
Normal human body temperature is about 37 degrees Celsius, or 98.6 degrees Fahrenheit.
Room temperature is about 20 to 25 degrees Celsius.
Record daily high and low temperatures, precipitation, and wind to describe weather patterns.
A weather station collects weather data such as temperature, pressure, humidity, wind, and precipitation.
In a terrarium or closed jar, water evaporates and condenses on the lid, modeling the water cycle.
Water vapor that rises cools in the upper atmosphere and condenses to form clouds.
Snow melts in spring and the water flows into rivers or soaks into the ground.
El Nino is a warming of the Pacific Ocean surface that changes weather around the world.
Monsoons are seasonal winds that bring heavy rain in summer.
Smog is a haze of air pollution that forms when sunlight reacts with car exhaust and smoke.
Temperature inversions trap air pollution near the ground.
When relative humidity reaches 100 percent the air is saturated, and fog, dew, clouds, or rain are likely.
Low relative humidity means dry air and usually clear weather.
High humidity and falling pressure often come before rain.
Electricity is the flow of electric charges, usually electrons.
Electric current is the flow of electrons through a conductor.
A circuit is a closed path through which electric current flows.
A complete circuit needs a power source such as a battery, wires, and a load such as a bulb.
A closed circuit is complete and current flows; an open circuit has a break and current does not flow.
A switch opens and closes a circuit to turn the current off and on.
If a circuit is broken, the bulb will not light.
A series circuit has only one path for current to flow.
In a series circuit, if one bulb burns out or is removed, all the other bulbs go out.
Adding more bulbs to a series circuit makes each bulb dimmer.
A parallel circuit has more than one path for current to flow.
In a parallel circuit, if one bulb burns out, the other bulbs stay lit.
Homes are wired with parallel circuits so that each appliance can work independently.
A battery is a source of electrical energy that changes chemical energy into electrical energy.
Batteries have a positive terminal and a negative terminal.
Voltage is the push that moves electric current through a circuit and is measured in volts.
Current is measured in amperes, or amps.
Resistance is the opposition to the flow of electric current and is measured in ohms.
Ohm's law states that voltage equals current times resistance.
Thin, long wires have more resistance than thick, short wires.
Electrical conductors such as copper, silver, aluminum, and gold allow current to flow easily.
Electrical insulators such as rubber, plastic, glass, and wood block the flow of current.
Wires are made of copper covered with plastic or rubber insulation.
The plastic coating on wires protects people from electric shock.
Water, especially salt water, conducts electricity, so electrical devices should be kept away from water.
A short circuit happens when current takes an unintended path with little resistance and can cause fires.
Fuses and circuit breakers protect circuits by breaking the circuit when too much current flows.
Static electricity is the buildup of electric charge on the surface of an object.
Rubbing a balloon on hair transfers electrons and gives the balloon a static charge so it sticks to a wall.
Objects with like charges repel each other, and objects with opposite charges attract each other.
Two negatively charged objects repel each other.
A positive charge and a negative charge attract each other.
Lightning is a discharge of static electricity.
A lightning rod protects buildings by safely carrying lightning to the ground.
Benjamin Franklin flew a kite in a storm to show that lightning is electricity.
Thomas Edison invented a long lasting practical light bulb.
Electricity is generated in power plants using generators.
Electric power is measured in watts and electrical energy use is measured in kilowatt hours.
Appliances that produce heat, such as heaters and ovens, use a lot of electrical energy.
A magnet is an object that attracts iron and some other metals.
Magnets attract objects made of iron, steel, nickel, and cobalt.
Wood, plastic, glass, paper, aluminum, copper, and gold are nonmagnetic materials.
Every magnet has a north pole and a south pole.
Like poles of magnets repel each other, and opposite poles attract each other.
Two north poles repel, and a north pole and a south pole attract.
The magnetic force is strongest at the poles of a magnet.
If a magnet is broken in half, each piece has its own north and south poles.
A magnetic field is the area around a magnet where magnetic force acts.
Iron filings sprinkled around a magnet show the pattern of its magnetic field lines.
Magnetic force can act through some materials such as paper, plastic, and water without touching.
Magnetism and gravity are forces that can act at a distance.
Earth acts like a giant magnet with a magnetic north pole and a magnetic south pole.
A compass needle is a small magnet that points north because it lines up with Earth's magnetic field.
A compass is used to find direction.
A compass needle moves when it is near a magnet or a wire carrying current.
An electromagnet is a magnet made by wrapping a coil of wire around an iron core and running electric current through it.
An electromagnet can be turned on and off by turning the current on and off.
The strength of an electromagnet increases with more coils of wire, more current, or a stronger iron core.
Electromagnets are used in cranes in junkyards, doorbells, speakers, motors, and MRI machines.
Electric current flowing in a wire produces a magnetic field.
Moving a magnet inside a coil of wire produces an electric current; this is electromagnetic induction.
A generator uses magnets and coils of wire to change motion into electricity.
An electric motor uses electromagnets to change electrical energy into motion.
Michael Faraday discovered that a changing magnetic field produces electric current.
Magnets are used in refrigerator doors, compasses, motors, and generators.
Rubbing a steel nail with a magnet can make the nail a temporary magnet.
Heating or dropping a magnet can weaken it.
Electrons flow from the negative terminal of a battery through the circuit to the positive terminal.
Direct current flows in one direction, as from a battery.
Alternating current changes direction many times each second and is used in homes.
Transformers change the voltage of electricity for power lines.
Solar cells, batteries, and generators are sources of electrical energy.
A circuit diagram uses symbols to show the parts of a circuit.
An ammeter measures electric current, and a voltmeter measures voltage.
More batteries in series give more voltage and make a bulb brighter.
A bulb lights when current flows through its filament, which glows from heat.
Electrical safety rules include not touching outlets with wet hands and not overloading outlets.
Energy is the ability to do work or cause change.
Energy cannot be created or destroyed, only changed from one form to another; this is the law of conservation of energy.
Forms of energy include mechanical, thermal, chemical, electrical, light, sound, and nuclear energy.
Kinetic energy is the energy of motion.
A moving object has kinetic energy; the faster it moves and the more mass it has, the more kinetic energy it has.
Potential energy is stored energy due to position or condition.
Gravitational potential energy increases as an object is raised higher.
A ball at the top of a hill or a book on a high shelf has gravitational potential energy.
Elastic potential energy is stored in stretched or compressed objects such as springs and rubber bands.
A stretched rubber band or a compressed spring has elastic potential energy.
Chemical energy is stored in the bonds of molecules, such as in food, fuel, and batteries.
Food contains chemical energy that the body uses.
Batteries store chemical energy and change it into electrical energy.
Thermal energy is the total kinetic energy of the particles in a substance.
Heat is the transfer of thermal energy from a warmer object to a cooler object.
Heat always flows from a hotter object to a colder object until they reach the same temperature.
Temperature is a measure of the average kinetic energy of the particles in a substance.
When a hot object touches a cold object, the hot object cools and the cold object warms.
Ice in a drink melts because heat flows from the warm drink into the ice.
Electrical energy is the energy of moving electric charges.
Light energy, or radiant energy, travels in waves and can move through empty space.
Sound energy is carried by vibrations through matter.
Nuclear energy is stored in the nucleus of atoms.
Mechanical energy is the sum of kinetic and potential energy of an object.
As a ball falls, its potential energy changes into kinetic energy.
As a ball is thrown upward, its kinetic energy changes into potential energy.
A roller coaster has the most potential energy at the top of the highest hill and the most kinetic energy at the bottom.
A swinging pendulum changes potential energy into kinetic energy and back again.
A pendulum moves fastest at the bottom of its swing.
Friction changes kinetic energy into thermal energy, or heat.
Rubbing your hands together makes them warm because friction changes motion energy into heat.
In an energy transformation, some energy is always changed into heat that is lost to the surroundings.
No machine is 100 percent efficient because some energy is lost as heat.
Efficiency is the percent of input energy that is changed into useful output energy.
A light bulb changes electrical energy into light and heat energy.
An incandescent bulb wastes most of its energy as heat, while an LED bulb is more efficient.
A toaster changes electrical energy into thermal energy.
An electric fan or motor changes electrical energy into mechanical energy, or motion.
A speaker or radio changes electrical energy into sound energy.
A battery powered flashlight changes chemical energy into electrical energy and then into light.
A generator changes mechanical energy into electrical energy.
An electric motor changes electrical energy into mechanical energy.
Solar cells change light energy into electrical energy.
Plants change light energy into chemical energy during photosynthesis.
Burning wood changes chemical energy into heat and light energy.
A car engine changes the chemical energy in gasoline into mechanical energy and heat.
When you eat, your body changes chemical energy in food into mechanical energy and heat.
A wind turbine changes wind's kinetic energy into electrical energy.
In a hydroelectric dam, the potential energy of water is changed into kinetic energy and then electrical energy.
Microphones change sound energy into electrical energy.
A stove burner changes electrical or chemical energy into heat.
Heat is transferred in three ways: conduction, convection, and radiation.
Conduction is the transfer of heat through direct contact between objects or particles.
A metal spoon in hot soup gets hot by conduction.
Touching a hot pan transfers heat to your hand by conduction.
Convection is the transfer of heat by the movement of fluids, liquids or gases.
In convection, warm fluid rises and cool fluid sinks, forming a convection current.
Boiling water in a pot circulates heat by convection.
Hot air rising from a heater warms a room by convection.
Convection currents occur in the air, the oceans, and Earth's mantle.
Radiation is the transfer of energy by electromagnetic waves, which can travel through empty space.
Heat from the Sun reaches Earth by radiation.
Feeling the warmth of a campfire from a distance is heat transfer by radiation.
Conductors are materials that allow heat or electricity to flow through them easily.
Metals such as copper, aluminum, iron, and silver are good conductors of heat.
Insulators are materials that slow or block the flow of heat or electricity.
Wood, plastic, rubber, glass, cloth, foam, and air are good insulators.
Pot handles are made of wood or plastic because they are insulators that do not get hot.
Cooking pans are made of metal because metal conducts heat well.
Wool clothing, feathers, and fur keep animals and people warm because they trap air, which is an insulator.
A thermos keeps drinks hot or cold by reducing heat transfer.
Foam cups keep drinks hot longer than metal cups.
Double paned windows and insulation in walls reduce heat loss from buildings.
Dark colors absorb more light and heat than light colors.
Black objects absorb more sunlight and get hotter than white objects.
White and light colored objects reflect more light and stay cooler.
Wearing light colored clothing in summer keeps you cooler.
Shiny surfaces such as aluminum foil reflect heat.
Water heats up and cools down more slowly than land or sand.
Sand on a beach gets hotter than the water on a sunny day.
Specific heat is the amount of energy needed to raise the temperature of a substance.
Thermal energy depends on both the temperature and the mass of a substance.
A large pot of warm water can have more thermal energy than a small cup of hot water.
Adding heat to a substance makes its particles move faster and its temperature rise.
A thermometer works because the liquid inside expands when heated and rises.
Energy from the Sun is the source of energy for food chains, wind, the water cycle, and fossil fuels.
Energy is measured in joules.
Power is the rate at which energy is used, measured in watts.
Work is done when a force moves an object a distance; work equals force times distance.
Work is measured in joules.
No work is done if the object does not move.
Solar energy can heat water and buildings directly.
Dark solar collectors absorb sunlight to heat water.
A greenhouse stays warm because glass lets sunlight in and traps heat.
A car parked in the Sun gets hot inside because sunlight enters through the windows and heat is trapped.
Energy in a closed system is conserved.
Heat flows out of a warm house in winter and into a cool house in summer.
Ice packs absorb heat to cool an injury.
Steam can cause worse burns than boiling water because it releases heat when it condenses.
Thermal energy moves from warm hands to a cold metal railing, making the railing feel cold.
Metal feels colder than wood at the same temperature because metal conducts heat away from your hand faster.
Bread rises because yeast produces carbon dioxide gas bubbles in the dough.
Popcorn pops because water inside the kernel turns to steam and bursts the hull.
Ice cream is made by freezing a mixture of cream, sugar, and flavoring.
Adding salt to ice makes the ice colder, which is used to make homemade ice cream.
Cooking food changes it chemically and kills bacteria.
Boiling an egg makes the proteins change permanently.
Toasting bread turns it brown because of a chemical change.
Milk left out of the refrigerator spoils because bacteria grow in it.
Fruit ripens faster in a paper bag because of a gas called ethylene.
Cutting onions releases chemicals that irritate the eyes.
Soap removes grease because one end of a soap molecule attracts water and the other attracts oil.
Hot water cleans better than cold water because it dissolves grease faster.
Bleach removes color stains by a chemical reaction.
Sugar dissolves in tea, but sand does not dissolve in water.
Stirring makes sugar dissolve faster.
A teaspoon of salt dissolves completely in a glass of water.
Ice cubes float in a drink because ice is less dense than water.
Steam from a kettle is invisible right at the spout and becomes a visible cloud as it condenses.
A cold soda can sweats because water vapor from the air condenses on it.
Breath fogs a cold window because water vapor in the breath condenses.
A wet floor dries faster when a fan blows on it.
Clothes dry faster on a hot windy day.
A sunny window sill warms up because sunlight is absorbed and changed to heat.
A car's dashboard gets very hot because sunlight is trapped inside the car.
A metal slide in a playground gets hot in summer sunlight.
Asphalt gets hotter than grass on a sunny day.
Walking barefoot on hot sand hurts because sand heats up quickly.
Swimming pools stay cooler than the surrounding concrete during the day.
A glass of ice water warms to room temperature over time.
A hot bowl of soup cools to room temperature over time.
A refrigerator keeps food cold by removing heat from inside it.
A freezer keeps food frozen at temperatures below 0 degrees Celsius.
Frozen water pipes can burst because ice takes up more space.
Lakes in the north freeze in winter, but the water below the ice stays liquid.
Snow can melt on a sunny day even if the air is below freezing because sunlight warms it.
Salt trucks spread salt on icy roads to melt the ice.
Sand is spread on icy sidewalks to increase friction.
Snow tires and chains increase traction on snowy roads.
Antifreeze in a car keeps the water in the engine from freezing.
A thermostat controls a furnace to keep a house at a set temperature.
Opening a window on a cold day lets warm air escape.
Closing curtains at night keeps heat inside a house.
A fireplace heats a room mostly by radiation.
A radiator heats a room mostly by convection.
A heating pad warms skin by conduction.
A fan cools people by helping sweat evaporate.
An air conditioner removes heat and moisture from a room.
Bicycle tires lose air pressure when it gets cold.
A ball bounces higher on a hard floor than on grass.
A ball rolls down a hill because of gravity.
A ball thrown straight up slows down, stops, and falls back down.
A paper airplane glides because air pushes up on its wings.
A kite flies when wind pushes against it.
A sailboat moves when wind pushes on its sail.
A swing goes higher when you pump your legs, adding energy.
A seesaw balances when the heavier person sits closer to the middle.
A heavy person and a light person can balance on a seesaw if the light person sits farther from the fulcrum.
Wheels make it easier to move heavy objects because they reduce friction.
A wagon is easier to pull on a smooth sidewalk than on grass.
Pushing a stalled car is easier on flat ground than uphill.
Riding a bike uphill takes more energy than riding on flat ground.
Coasting down a hill on a bike requires no pedaling because gravity pulls the bike down.
A skateboarder slows down on rough pavement because of friction.
Sneakers have rubber soles with patterns to increase friction.
Ice skates have thin blades that glide easily on ice.
Swimmers wear smooth suits and caps to reduce drag in water.
A parachute opens to slow a skydiver's fall.
A feather falls slowly because of air resistance.
A rock dropped into a pond makes waves that spread out in circles.
Shouting into a canyon produces an echo.
Covering your ears makes sounds quieter.
Sound travels through walls, which is why you can hear neighbors.
You can hear a train coming by putting your ear to the rail because sound travels well through solids.
A stethoscope carries sound from the body to the doctor's ears.
A guitar sounds louder because its hollow body makes the sound vibrations stronger.
A dog whistle is too high pitched for humans to hear.
Thunder sounds after lightning because sound travels slower than light.
Counting the seconds between lightning and thunder tells how far away the storm is.
The sky turns orange and red at sunset because sunlight passes through more air.
Shadows are darker on sunny days than on cloudy days.
A flashlight beam spreads out the farther it travels.
A mirror can be used to reflect sunlight into a dark place.
A periscope uses two mirrors to see over a wall.
Sunlight through a window can fade colors of furniture because of ultraviolet light.
Plants on a windowsill lean toward the light.
Seeds sprout faster in warm soil than in cold soil.
Tomato plants need lots of sunlight to make fruit.
Weeds compete with garden plants for water and nutrients.
Mulch keeps soil moist and reduces weeds.
Earthworms in a garden make the soil better for plants.
Compost adds nutrients to garden soil.
Bees in a garden help pollinate vegetables and flowers.
Ladybugs in a garden eat aphids that damage plants.
Birds at a feeder eat seeds and may spread seeds to other places.
Squirrels gather acorns in the fall.
Ants follow scent trails to find food.
Spiders build webs in corners to catch insects.
Mosquitoes breed in standing water, so emptying containers reduces mosquitoes.
Flies can spread germs to food.
Moths are attracted to porch lights at night.
Crickets chirp faster when it is warmer.
Frogs croak to attract mates in spring.
Robins return in spring after migrating south for the winter.
Geese fly in a V formation during migration.
Bears eat lots of food in fall before hibernating.
Deer grow thicker coats in winter.
Dogs shed their thick winter coats in spring.
Cats have whiskers to sense their surroundings in the dark.
A dog's nose is very sensitive to smells.
Pets need food, water, shelter, and care.
Fish in an aquarium need clean water with oxygen.
Plants in an aquarium give off oxygen that fish use.
An aquarium with too many fish may run out of oxygen.
Hamsters are nocturnal and are active at night.
Chickens lay eggs, and some eggs hatch into chicks if fertilized.
Caterpillars in a classroom can be watched as they turn into butterflies.
Tadpoles in a classroom tank grow legs and become frogs.
Bean seeds in a wet paper towel sprout roots in a few days.
Mold grows on bread kept in a warm, dark, damp place.
Melting ice is an example of a physical change.
Boiling water is an example of a physical change.
Freezing water is an example of a physical change.
Cutting paper is an example of a physical change.
Tearing paper is an example of a physical change.
Crushing a can is an example of a physical change.
Dissolving sugar in water is an example of a physical change.
Breaking glass is an example of a physical change.
Chopping wood is an example of a physical change.
Folding clothes is an example of a physical change.
Sharpening a pencil is an example of a physical change.
Evaporating water is an example of a physical change.
Mixing sand and salt is an example of a physical change.
Stretching a rubber band is an example of a physical change.
Bending a wire is an example of a physical change.
Grinding pepper is an example of a physical change.
Making ice cubes is an example of a physical change.
Melting butter is an example of a physical change.
Melting chocolate is an example of a physical change.
Shredding cheese is an example of a physical change.
Burning wood is an example of a chemical change.
Rusting iron is an example of a chemical change.
Baking a cake is an example of a chemical change.
Frying an egg is an example of a chemical change.
Toasting bread is an example of a chemical change.
Digesting food is an example of a chemical change.
Rotting fruit is an example of a chemical change.
Souring milk is an example of a chemical change.
Burning gasoline is an example of a chemical change.
Tarnishing silver is an example of a chemical change.
Fireworks exploding is an example of a chemical change.
A match burning is an example of a chemical change.
Leaves changing color is an example of a chemical change.
Mixing baking soda and vinegar is an example of a chemical change.
Photosynthesis is an example of a chemical change.
Cellular respiration is an example of a chemical change.
A candle wick burning is an example of a chemical change.

A force is a push or a pull.
Forces can change an object's speed, direction of motion, or shape.
Force is measured in newtons using a spring scale.
A spring scale measures force and weight in newtons.
Balanced forces are equal in size and opposite in direction and do not change an object's motion.
Unbalanced forces cause an object to start moving, stop, speed up, slow down, or change direction.
The net force is the combination of all the forces acting on an object.
Gravity is a force of attraction between all objects that have mass.
Gravity pulls objects toward the center of Earth.
Gravity causes objects to fall to the ground when dropped.
The strength of gravity depends on the masses of the objects and the distance between them.
Objects with more mass have stronger gravitational pull.
Gravity gets weaker as the distance between objects increases.
Without air resistance, all objects fall at the same rate regardless of their mass.
Objects fall at the same rate in a vacuum, so a feather and a hammer dropped on the Moon land at the same time.
Air resistance is a type of friction that slows objects moving through air.
A parachute increases air resistance and slows a falling skydiver.
A crumpled sheet of paper falls faster than a flat sheet because it has less air resistance.
Friction is a force that opposes motion between two surfaces that are touching.
Friction acts in the direction opposite to motion.
Rough surfaces produce more friction than smooth surfaces.
Friction produces heat.
Friction helps us walk, and it lets car tires grip the road.
Brakes use friction to slow down and stop a bicycle or car.
Ice is slippery because it has very little friction.
Sand or salt on icy roads increases friction.
Oil, grease, and other lubricants reduce friction between moving parts.
Wheels and ball bearings reduce friction.
A ball rolls farther on a smooth floor than on carpet or grass because there is less friction.
Treads on shoes and tires increase friction to prevent slipping.
Isaac Newton described three laws of motion.
Newton's first law, the law of inertia, states that an object at rest stays at rest and an object in motion stays in motion unless acted on by an unbalanced force.
Inertia is the tendency of an object to resist changes in its motion.
Objects with more mass have more inertia.
Seat belts protect passengers because their inertia keeps them moving forward when a car stops suddenly.
Newton's second law states that force equals mass times acceleration.
The greater the force, the greater the acceleration; the greater the mass, the smaller the acceleration for the same force.
It takes more force to accelerate a heavy object than a light object.
Newton's third law states that for every action there is an equal and opposite reaction.
When you push on a wall, the wall pushes back on you with equal force.
A rocket launches because hot gases are pushed down and the gases push the rocket up.
When a swimmer pushes water backward, the water pushes the swimmer forward.
A balloon flies forward when air rushes out the back.
Motion is a change in position over time.
A reference point is used to describe the position and motion of an object.
Speed is the distance an object travels divided by the time it takes.
Speed equals distance divided by time.
A car that travels 100 kilometers in 2 hours has an average speed of 50 kilometers per hour.
Velocity is speed in a given direction.
Acceleration is the rate at which velocity changes, including speeding up, slowing down, or changing direction.
An object moving in a circle at a constant speed is accelerating because its direction changes.
Momentum is mass times velocity.
A heavy truck moving fast has more momentum than a small car moving at the same speed.
On a distance time graph, a straight sloped line shows constant speed and a flat line shows the object is not moving.
A steeper line on a distance time graph means a greater speed.
Weight is the force of gravity on an object and depends on gravity; mass does not change with location.
An object weighs less on the Moon than on Earth because the Moon's gravity is weaker.
Weight is measured in newtons and mass is measured in kilograms.
Magnetic force and electric force can push or pull without touching.
Friction and applied forces are contact forces, while gravity and magnetism are non contact forces.
Buoyant force is the upward push of a fluid on an object.
An object floats when the buoyant force equals its weight.
Pressure is force divided by the area over which it acts.
Snowshoes spread weight over a large area so people do not sink into snow.
A sharp knife cuts easily because the force is concentrated on a small area.
Centripetal force keeps an object moving in a circle.
Gravity provides the centripetal force that keeps planets and moons in orbit.
A simple machine is a device that makes work easier by changing the size or direction of a force.
The six simple machines are the lever, wheel and axle, pulley, inclined plane, wedge, and screw.
Simple machines do not reduce the amount of work but make it easier by reducing the force needed.
A machine that reduces the force needed must increase the distance the force is applied.
Mechanical advantage is how many times a machine multiplies the input force.
A lever is a rigid bar that pivots on a fixed point called a fulcrum.
A seesaw, crowbar, scissors, and a bottle opener are levers.
Moving the fulcrum closer to the load makes the load easier to lift with a lever.
A wheelbarrow and a nutcracker are second class levers.
A broom, fishing rod, and tweezers are third class levers.
A pulley is a wheel with a rope or chain around it used to lift loads.
A single fixed pulley changes the direction of the force, such as on a flagpole.
Using several pulleys together reduces the force needed to lift a heavy load.
An inclined plane is a flat sloping surface, such as a ramp.
A ramp makes it easier to move a heavy object up to a higher place by using less force over a longer distance.
A longer, less steep ramp requires less force.
A wedge is two inclined planes joined together that split or cut objects, such as an axe, knife, or doorstop.
A screw is an inclined plane wrapped around a cylinder, such as a jar lid or a bolt.
A wheel and axle is a wheel attached to a rod, such as a doorknob, steering wheel, or screwdriver.
A compound machine is made of two or more simple machines, such as a bicycle or scissors.
A bicycle uses wheels and axles, levers, and gears.
Gears are wheels with teeth that transfer motion and force.
Work is done when a force moves an object through a distance in the direction of the force.
Pushing on a wall that does not move does no work.
Power is the rate at which work is done.
An object thrown horizontally falls in a curved path because gravity pulls it down.
Objects that roll down a steeper ramp reach the bottom with greater speed.
A heavier object needs a greater force to move than a lighter object on the same surface.
A ball thrown up slows down, stops at the top, and speeds up as it falls because of gravity.
Terminal velocity is reached when air resistance equals the force of gravity on a falling object.
Tension is the pulling force in a rope or string.
Elastic force pushes or pulls a stretched or compressed spring back to its original shape.
Magnets, springs, and rubber bands can store energy and exert forces.
A soccer ball changes direction when it is kicked because an unbalanced force acts on it.
A ball rolling across the ground slows down and stops because of friction.
In space, a moving object keeps moving in a straight line because there is no friction or air resistance.
A car going around a curve changes velocity even if its speed stays the same.
Measuring the time it takes to travel a known distance lets you calculate speed.
A stopwatch measures time, and a meter stick or measuring tape measures distance.
Helmets and air bags reduce injury by spreading out and slowing down the force of an impact.
The heavier the load a truck carries, the longer it takes to stop.
Sir Isaac Newton described the law of universal gravitation.
Galileo showed that heavy and light objects fall at the same rate.
Absorption is the taking in of light, heat, sound, water, or nutrients by a material or organism.
Acceleration is a change in speed or direction of motion over time.
An acid is a substance that tastes sour and has a pH below 7.
Acid rain is rain made acidic by air pollution from burning fossil fuels.
An adaptation helps a living thing survive in its environment.
Aerobic respiration uses oxygen to release energy from food.
Anaerobic respiration releases energy without oxygen, such as in yeast making alcohol or muscles making lactic acid.
Agriculture is the science and practice of farming, growing crops and raising livestock.
Air is the mixture of gases that makes up Earth's atmosphere.
Air pressure is the weight of the atmosphere pressing down on an area.
An alloy is a mixture of two or more metals, or a metal and a nonmetal.
Altitude is the height above sea level.
Amplitude is the maximum height of a wave from its resting position.
An anemometer is a weather instrument with spinning cups that measures wind speed.
An antibiotic is a medicine that kills bacteria.
An antibody is a protein that helps the body fight a specific germ.
An aquifer is underground rock or sediment that holds water.
An artery is a blood vessel that carries blood away from the heart.
