Astronomy is the study of stars, planets, and space.
The atmosphere is the blanket of air around Earth.
An atom is the smallest particle of an element.
An axis is an imaginary line through the center of a planet around which it spins.
Bacteria are one celled microorganisms without a nucleus.
A balance is a tool used to measure mass by comparing an object with known masses.

A base is a substance that tastes bitter, feels slippery, and has a pH above 7.

A carnivore is an animal that eats only meat.

Celsius is the metric temperature scale on which water freezes at 0 degrees and boils at 100 degrees.

A circuit is a path for electricity to flow.

Competition is when two or more organisms try to get the same resource.

A constellation is a group of stars that make a pattern.
A consumer is an organism that eats other organisms.
Continental drift is the slow movement of continents over Earth's surface.

A control is the part of an experiment that is not changed and is used for comparison.

The core is the center of the Earth.

A crystal is a solid with atoms arranged in a repeating pattern.

Dissolve means to mix completely into a liquid to form a solution.

Evaporation is the process of a liquid changing into a gas.

Galileo Galilei improved the telescope and observed the moons of Jupiter, the phases of Venus, and craters on the Moon.
Galileo's observations supported the idea that Earth and the planets orbit the Sun.
Nicolaus Copernicus proposed that the Sun, not Earth, is at the center of the solar system.
Ptolemy's geocentric model placed Earth at the center of the universe.
Johannes Kepler discovered that the planets travel in elliptical orbits around the Sun.
Isaac Newton developed the laws of motion and the law of universal gravitation.
Newton showed that white light is made of many colors by using a prism.
Albert Einstein developed the theory of relativity and the equation E equals m c squared.
Edwin Hubble discovered that there are other galaxies and that the universe is expanding.
Charles Darwin proposed the theory of evolution by natural selection after his voyage on the Beagle to the Galapagos Islands.
Gregor Mendel experimented with pea plants and discovered the basic laws of heredity.
James Watson and Francis Crick described the double helix structure of DNA, using X ray images made by Rosalind Franklin.
Louis Pasteur showed that microorganisms cause disease and food spoilage and invented pasteurization.
Robert Hooke used a microscope to observe cork and named the small boxes he saw cells.
Anton van Leeuwenhoek made simple microscopes and was the first to see bacteria and protists.
Carolus Linnaeus created the system of naming organisms with a genus and species name.
Edward Jenner created the smallpox vaccine using cowpox.
Alexander Fleming discovered penicillin, the first antibiotic, from mold.
Jonas Salk developed the first vaccine for polio.
Marie Curie studied radioactivity and discovered the elements polonium and radium.
Jane Goodall studied the behavior of chimpanzees in the wild and observed them using tools.
Rachel Carson wrote Silent Spring, which warned about the dangers of pesticides like DDT.
George Washington Carver developed hundreds of uses for peanuts and promoted crop rotation to improve soil.
John Muir worked to protect wilderness areas and helped start national parks.
Alfred Wegener proposed continental drift, the idea that continents move.
James Hutton is called the father of modern geology and said Earth's features formed by slow processes over long times.
Charles Richter developed a scale for measuring the magnitude of earthquakes.
Benjamin Franklin experimented with electricity and invented the lightning rod and bifocal glasses.
Thomas Edison invented the phonograph and improved the electric light bulb.
Alexander Graham Bell invented the telephone.
The Wright brothers built and flew the first successful powered airplane in 1903.
Samuel Morse invented the telegraph and Morse code.
Michael Faraday discovered electromagnetic induction, which led to the electric generator.
Nikola Tesla developed alternating current electricity and the induction motor.
James Watt improved the steam engine, which powered the Industrial Revolution.
Johannes Gutenberg invented the printing press with movable type.
Henry Ford used the assembly line to mass produce cars.
Antoine Lavoisier is called the father of modern chemistry and showed that mass is conserved in chemical reactions.
John Dalton proposed the atomic theory that all matter is made of atoms.
Dmitri Mendeleev arranged the elements into the periodic table and predicted undiscovered elements.
Ernest Rutherford discovered the nucleus of the atom with his gold foil experiment.
Niels Bohr developed a model of the atom with electrons in energy levels.
Archimedes discovered the principle of buoyancy and explained levers.
Aristotle classified living things as plants or animals.
Hippocrates is called the father of medicine.
Mae Jemison was the first African American woman to travel in space.
Sally Ride was the first American woman in space.
Yuri Gagarin was the first person to travel into space in 1961.
Neil Armstrong and Buzz Aldrin walked on the Moon in 1969.
Technology is the application of science to meet human needs and solve problems.
Engineers use science and math to design solutions to problems.
An invention is a new device, method, or process.
The steam engine, the light bulb, the telephone, and the computer are inventions that changed society.
The invention of the microscope allowed scientists to discover cells and microorganisms.
The invention of the telescope allowed scientists to discover moons and other features of planets.
Computers allow scientists to process large amounts of data and model complex systems.
The Internet allows scientists around the world to share information quickly.
Cell phones send and receive signals using radio waves.
GPS uses signals from satellites to find locations on Earth.
Robots can do jobs that are dangerous, repetitive, or too precise for people.
Space probes and rovers explore places too far or too dangerous for people to visit.
X ray machines, MRI scanners, and ultrasound let doctors see inside the body without surgery.
Artificial hearts, limbs, and hearing aids help people with medical problems.
Refrigerators keep food cold and slow the growth of bacteria.
Canning food in sealed containers after heating it keeps food from spoiling.
Freeze drying removes water from food so microorganisms cannot grow.
Plastics are useful because they are light, strong, cheap, and waterproof, but they create waste and pollution.
New materials such as Kevlar, Velcro, and carbon fiber were developed through research.
Bulletproof vests are made of strong fibers such as Kevlar.
Velcro was inspired by burrs that stick to animal fur.
Studying nature to design technology is called biomimicry.
Airplane wings are curved so that air moving over them creates lift.
Submarines dive by filling tanks with water and rise by pushing the water out with air.
Hot air balloons rise when the air inside is heated.
Bridges are designed to hold heavy loads and resist wind and earthquakes.
Triangles are strong shapes often used in bridges and towers.
Steel and concrete are used to build strong buildings and bridges.
Engineers test models of buildings and bridges before building them.
Wind tunnels test how air flows around cars and airplanes.
Seatbelts, airbags, and crumple zones make cars safer in a crash.
Helmets for biking are made with foam that absorbs the force of an impact.
Insulated walls and double paned windows reduce energy use for heating and cooling.
Light colored roofs reflect sunlight and keep buildings cooler.
Energy efficient appliances do the same work while using less electricity.
Fluorescent and LED bulbs produce less waste heat than incandescent bulbs.
Hybrid cars use both a gasoline engine and an electric motor to save fuel.
Water treatment uses filtering, settling, and chlorine to make water safe to drink.
Sewage treatment plants clean wastewater before it is released into rivers.
Dams store water for drinking and irrigation and control floods.
Irrigation systems allow crops to grow in dry areas.
Tractors and other farm machines allow fewer farmers to grow more food.
Genetic engineering can produce crops that resist pests or drought.
Pesticides kill insects that damage crops but can harm other organisms.
Fertilizers increase crop yields but can pollute water through runoff.
Greenhouses allow plants to grow in cold seasons.
Hydroponics grows plants without soil.
Recycling plants sort and process glass, metal, paper, and plastic into new materials.
Composting is a natural way to recycle food and yard waste.
Scientists and engineers must consider the costs, benefits, and risks of new technology.
Some technologies cause unexpected problems, such as pollution from cars and factories.
Technology helps scientists make more precise measurements.
Communication satellites relay television, phone, and internet signals around the world.
Weather satellites and radar have made weather forecasts more accurate and saved lives.
Seismographs and warning systems help warn people about earthquakes and tsunamis.
Smoke detectors warn people of fires.
Fire extinguishers put out fires by removing oxygen or heat.
Lightning rods protect buildings from lightning strikes.
Levees and sea walls protect cities from floods.
A thermostat turns a heater on and off to keep the temperature steady.
A battery powered car does not burn gasoline and produces no exhaust.
Solar ovens use reflective surfaces to focus sunlight and cook food.
A periscope uses mirrors to see over or around objects.
A kaleidoscope uses mirrors to make patterns.
A camera captures images using a lens and light sensor.
Eyeglasses and contact lenses correct vision problems using lenses.
A hearing aid makes sounds louder for people with hearing loss.
Stethoscopes help doctors listen to the heart and lungs.
Thermometers help doctors check for fever.
The scientific method includes asking a question, forming a hypothesis, testing it with an experiment, collecting and analyzing data, and drawing a conclusion.
A hypothesis is a testable prediction or possible explanation that can be tested by an experiment.
A hypothesis is often written as an if then statement.
A prediction is a statement about what will happen based on observations or a hypothesis.
An observation is information gathered using the senses or instruments.
An inference is a conclusion or explanation based on observations and prior knowledge.
Qualitative observations describe qualities such as color, smell, or texture.
Quantitative observations are measurements that use numbers.
An experiment is a test done to answer a question or test a hypothesis under controlled conditions.
A variable is a factor that can change in an experiment.
The independent variable, or manipulated variable, is the factor that the scientist changes on purpose.
The dependent variable, or responding variable, is the factor that is measured and changes in response to the independent variable.
Controlled variables, or constants, are factors kept the same in every trial so the test is fair.
A fair test changes only one variable at a time and keeps all other conditions the same.
A control group is the group that does not receive the treatment and is used for comparison.
The experimental group is the group that receives the variable being tested.
Testing only one variable at a time lets scientists know what caused the result.
Repeating an experiment several times, or doing multiple trials, makes the results more reliable.
Repeated trials reduce the effect of errors and make data more accurate.
A larger sample size makes results more reliable.
Other scientists should be able to replicate an experiment and get similar results.
Scientists repeat each other's experiments to check whether results are valid.
Recording data accurately in a table or notebook is important in an investigation.
Data are the facts, measurements, and observations collected during an experiment.
A data table organizes data in rows and columns.
A line graph shows how one variable changes over time or in relation to another continuous variable.
A bar graph compares amounts or quantities in different categories.
A pie chart, or circle graph, shows parts of a whole as percentages.
The independent variable is usually plotted on the x axis and the dependent variable on the y axis.
Graphs help scientists see patterns and trends in data.
A conclusion states whether the data support the hypothesis.
If the data do not support the hypothesis, the scientist should revise the hypothesis and test again.
Scientists share their results by publishing them so other scientists can review them.
Peer review is when other scientists check a scientist's work before it is published.
Scientific knowledge can change when new evidence is discovered.
A scientific theory is a well tested explanation supported by a large amount of evidence.
A scientific law describes what happens in nature, often as a pattern or equation, without explaining why.
A model is a representation of an object or process that helps explain or predict it.
Models can be physical models, drawings, diagrams, computer simulations, or mathematical equations.
Models help scientists study things that are too large, too small, too dangerous, or too slow to study directly.
A globe is a model of Earth.
A map is a flat model of an area of Earth's surface.
A topographic map uses contour lines to show elevation and the shape of the land.
Contour lines close together show a steep slope, and lines far apart show a gentle slope.
Limitations of models are that they are not exactly like the real thing.
Evidence is information or data that supports or refutes a claim.
Scientists should be honest and report all data, even data that do not support the hypothesis.
Bias can affect results, so scientists use controls and repeated trials.
Scientists use technology to collect more accurate data.
The metric system, or International System of Units, is used by scientists around the world.
Length is measured in meters, centimeters, millimeters, and kilometers.
A meter stick or ruler measures length.
Mass is measured in grams and kilograms using a balance or scale.
A triple beam balance or electronic balance measures mass.
Volume of liquid is measured in liters and milliliters using a graduated cylinder or beaker.
A graduated cylinder gives a more accurate measure of liquid volume than a beaker.
Read the volume in a graduated cylinder at the bottom of the meniscus at eye level.
Temperature is measured in degrees Celsius with a thermometer.
Time is measured in seconds, minutes, and hours with a stopwatch or clock.
Force is measured in newtons with a spring scale.
A kilometer is 1,000 meters, a centimeter is one hundredth of a meter, and a millimeter is one thousandth of a meter.
A kilogram is 1,000 grams, and a liter is 1,000 milliliters.
A hand lens or magnifying glass makes small objects appear larger.
A microscope is used to observe very small things such as cells and microorganisms.
A telescope is used to observe distant objects such as stars and planets.
Binoculars help observe distant birds and animals.
A pH meter or litmus paper measures acidity.
A barometer measures air pressure, an anemometer measures wind speed, and a rain gauge measures rainfall.
A seismograph measures earthquake waves.
A compass shows direction.
A calculator and computer help scientists analyze data.
Computers are used to make models, analyze data, and share results.
Safety goggles protect the eyes during experiments with chemicals, heat, or flying objects.
Always wear safety goggles when working with chemicals or heating substances.
Gloves protect hands when handling chemicals, hot objects, or living specimens.
Lab aprons protect clothing and skin from spills.
Never eat or drink in the science laboratory.
Students should not bring food or drinks into the laboratory.
Tie back long hair and roll up loose sleeves when working near a flame.
Wash hands with soap and water after handling chemicals, soil, plants, or animals.
Report all spills, broken glass, and injuries to the teacher right away.
Never taste or directly smell chemicals in the laboratory; waft fumes toward your nose with your hand.
Point the open end of a heated test tube away from yourself and others.
Use tongs or heat resistant gloves to handle hot objects.
Follow the teacher's directions and read all instructions before starting an experiment.
Dispose of chemicals as directed by the teacher, not down the sink unless told to.
Know the location of the fire extinguisher, eyewash station, and safety shower.
Clean up the work area and return materials when finished.
Handle living organisms gently and humanely, and return them to their habitat.
Glassware should be checked for cracks before using it.
Electrical equipment should be kept away from water.
Scientists use evidence and reasoning to support their explanations.
Correlation does not always mean that one factor causes another.
An investigation should have a clear testable question.
A testable question can be answered by collecting measurable data.
Questions about opinions, such as which flower is prettiest, cannot be answered by science.
Scientists make observations, ask questions, and look for patterns.
Measurements should be precise and accurate.
Accuracy is how close a measurement is to the true value.
Precision is how close repeated measurements are to each other.
Averaging the results of several trials gives a more reliable result.
Outliers are data points that are very different from the rest of the data.
Units must be included with measurements.
Field studies are investigations done outdoors in natural settings.
Scientists keep a science journal or notebook to record observations, procedures, and results.
Labeled diagrams and drawings record observations.
Technology is the use of scientific knowledge to solve problems and make useful tools and products.
Engineers design and build solutions to problems.
The engineering design process includes identifying a problem, brainstorming, designing, building a prototype, testing, and improving.
A prototype is an early model of a design used for testing.
Criteria are the requirements a design must meet, and constraints are limits such as cost, time, and materials.
Engineers test designs and use the results to improve them.
Trade offs are made when one feature of a design is given up to improve another.
New technologies can have both benefits and drawbacks for society and the environment.
Inventions such as the telescope and microscope led to new scientific discoveries.
The invention of the printing press, telephone, computer, and internet changed how people share information.
Improvements in transportation, such as refrigerated trucks and ships, increased the types of food available in stores.
The ability to ship foods around the world has increased the variety of food available for sale.
Refrigeration allows food to be stored longer without spoiling.
Satellites improved weather forecasting and communication.
Medical technology such as X rays, MRI, vaccines, and antibiotics has improved human health.
Scientists from many countries work together and share knowledge.
Science is a way of learning about the natural world through observation and experiments.
Scientific explanations must be based on evidence that can be tested.
A procedure is a step by step list of directions for an experiment.
A materials list tells what supplies are needed for an experiment.
Changing more than one variable at a time makes it impossible to know which variable caused the result.
To test how sunlight affects plant growth, give plants different amounts of light but the same water, soil, and temperature.
To test the effect of fertilizer, use identical plants with the same light and water, and give only some of them fertilizer.
Using the same type and size of container keeps the test fair.
A thermometer should be read at eye level.
Scientists use estimation when an exact measurement is not needed.
The best way to make the results of an experiment more reliable is to repeat the experiment several times.
Doing many trials and averaging the results makes conclusions more reliable.
Scientists repeat investigations to make sure their results are consistent and not due to chance.
If an experiment is repeated and gives different results, the scientist should look for errors and test again.
When other scientists repeat an experiment and get the same results, the conclusion is more likely to be valid.
Scientists publish their results so that other scientists can review and repeat the experiments.
Sharing results lets other scientists check the work and build on it.
Scientists communicate results through journals, reports, presentations, and conferences.
A good experiment tests only one variable at a time.
In a controlled experiment, all conditions are kept the same except the one being tested.
The purpose of a control group is to provide a standard for comparison.
Without a control, a scientist cannot tell whether the variable caused the result.
The variable that is changed on purpose is the independent variable.
The variable that is measured is the dependent variable.
For example, if students test how the amount of water affects plant growth, the amount of water is the independent variable and plant height is the dependent variable.
When testing whether fertilizer helps plants grow, plants that get no fertilizer are the control group.
When testing the effect of temperature on how fast sugar dissolves, the temperature of the water is the independent variable.
When testing which paper towel absorbs the most water, the brand of paper towel is the variable being tested and the amount of water absorbed is measured.
When testing how the height of a ramp affects the distance a car rolls, the ramp height is changed and the distance is measured.
To test which brand of battery lasts longest, use the same kind of flashlight for every battery.
To test how salt affects the boiling point of water, use the same amount of water and heat for each trial.
To compare how fast different liquids evaporate, use the same amount of each liquid in the same type of container in the same place.
To find out if light affects plant growth, put identical plants in light and in darkness with the same water and soil.
To test whether music affects plant growth, all plants should get the same light, water, and soil.
Using the same kind of seeds, soil, and containers keeps a plant experiment fair.
Measuring plant height with a ruler every day gives quantitative data.
Recording the color and smell of a substance gives qualitative data.
Scientists record observations carefully and honestly in a notebook.
A good observation is detailed, accurate, and uses measurements when possible.
An observation describes what is seen, heard, smelled, felt, or measured.
An inference explains or interprets an observation.
Saying a rock is gray and rough is an observation; saying it was formed by a volcano is an inference.
Seeing wet streets and inferring that it rained is an example of an inference.
A prediction states what will happen in the future based on data or patterns.
Scientists use patterns in data to make predictions.
A hypothesis must be testable by an experiment or observation.
A hypothesis can be supported or refuted by evidence but never proven absolutely.
If results do not support a hypothesis, scientists form a new hypothesis.
A scientific question can be answered by making observations and measurements.
A question such as which animal is the cutest cannot be answered scientifically because it is an opinion.
Facts can be checked with evidence, but opinions cannot.
A theory is broader than a hypothesis and is supported by many experiments.
Scientific theories can be changed or revised when new evidence is found.
Models help scientists explain and predict, but every model has limitations.
A model of the solar system shows the order of planets but not their true sizes and distances.
A globe is a better model of Earth's shape than a flat map.
A map distorts the shapes and sizes of land areas because Earth is round.
A diagram of a cell is a model.
A computer simulation can model weather, climate, or the spread of a disease.
Scientists use models of molecules to show how atoms are arranged.
Data should be organized in tables and displayed in graphs to look for patterns.
A line graph is best for showing changes in temperature over time.
A bar graph is best for comparing the number of different kinds of animals.
A circle graph is best for showing what percent of the land is covered by forests, farms, and cities.
A scatter plot shows the relationship between two variables.
A trend is a general pattern in data.
The slope of a line graph shows the rate of change.
Labeling the axes of a graph with units makes it easier to understand.
An average is found by adding the values and dividing by the number of values.
The range of a data set is the difference between the highest and lowest values.
Errors in measurement can be reduced by using better tools and repeating measurements.
Using a more precise instrument, such as a digital scale, reduces measurement error.
Measuring to the nearest millimeter is more precise than measuring to the nearest centimeter.
An electronic balance measures mass more precisely than a spring scale.
A stopwatch is used to measure time in an experiment.
A metric ruler measures length in centimeters and millimeters.
A measuring tape is used to measure long distances or the circumference of an object.
A thermometer is used to measure the temperature of a liquid.
A graduated cylinder is used to measure the volume of a liquid.
A balance is used to measure the mass of an object.
A spring scale is used to measure the weight or pulling force on an object.
A hand lens is used to look closely at small objects such as insects, rocks, and leaves.
A microscope is used to look at cells, bacteria, and other things too small to see with a hand lens.
A telescope is used to look at the Moon, planets, and stars.
Binoculars are used to watch birds and animals from far away.
A compass is used to find direction.
A magnet is used to test whether an object contains iron.
A prism is used to separate light into colors.
A pH strip is used to test whether a liquid is an acid or a base.
A Petri dish is used to grow bacteria or mold.
A test tube holds small amounts of liquid for experiments.
A beaker is used to hold, mix, and heat liquids.
A funnel is used to pour liquids into a container with a small opening.
Tongs are used to pick up hot objects.
A dropper is used to add small amounts of liquid.
Safety goggles protect the eyes from splashes and flying objects.
An apron protects clothing from spills.
A fire extinguisher is used to put out a fire in the laboratory.
An eyewash station is used if chemicals get in the eyes.
When heating liquids, wear goggles and point test tubes away from people.
When cutting with sharp tools, cut away from your body.
If a chemical spills on your skin, rinse it with plenty of water and tell the teacher.
If glassware breaks, tell the teacher and do not pick up the pieces with bare hands.
Never mix chemicals unless told to by the teacher.
Never touch or taste substances in a lab unless the teacher says it is safe.
Long hair should be tied back when working with flames.
Students should wear gloves when handling bacteria, chemicals, or preserved specimens.
Students should wash their hands after the lab and before leaving the laboratory.
Living things used in experiments should be treated with care and respect.
Wild animals should be observed from a distance and not disturbed.
Students should get permission before going into a natural area for a field study.
When observing the Sun, never look directly at it, even with a telescope.
Scientists ask questions, investigate, and use evidence to explain the natural world.
Scientists work in teams and share ideas.
Science knowledge builds on the work of earlier scientists.
Scientific discoveries often lead to new questions.
Advances in technology often lead to new scientific discoveries.
Scientists should keep their personal opinions from affecting their results.
Scientists should report the results of their experiments even when the results are unexpected.
An experiment with a small sample size, such as one plant, may give unreliable results.
Using many plants or many test subjects makes the results more reliable.
Measurements should be recorded with the correct units, such as centimeters, grams, or milliliters.
Before an experiment, a scientist should research what is already known about the topic.
After an experiment, the scientist analyzes data and draws a conclusion.
A conclusion should be based on the data collected, not on what the scientist expected.
Students should write a procedure so another person can repeat the experiment exactly.
A clear procedure lists the materials and the steps in order.
A scientist who studies weather is a meteorologist.
A scientist who studies rocks is a geologist.
A scientist who studies fossils is a paleontologist.
A scientist who studies living things is a biologist.
A scientist who studies plants is a botanist.
A scientist who studies animals is a zoologist.
A scientist who studies insects is an entomologist.
A scientist who studies the oceans is an oceanographer.
A scientist who studies space is an astronomer.
A scientist who studies matter and its changes is a chemist.
A scientist who studies energy, forces, and motion is a physicist.
A scientist who studies ecosystems is an ecologist.
A scientist who studies earthquakes is a seismologist.
A scientist who studies volcanoes is a volcanologist.
A scientist who studies genes and heredity is a geneticist.
A scientist who studies microorganisms is a microbiologist.
A scientist who studies ancient human cultures is an archaeologist.
Pouring a mixture of sand and water through a filter separates the sand from the water.
A hand lens is a tool used to observe the details of small objects such as an insect's wing, a leaf, or a rock.
A magnifying glass is the best tool to observe the details of an insect, a flower, or a seed.
A microscope is the best tool to observe cells, bacteria, and the details of very tiny objects.
A telescope is the best tool to observe the Moon, planets, and stars.
A thermometer is the best tool to measure how hot or cold something is.
A balance is the best tool to measure the mass of an object such as a rock.
A graduated cylinder is the best tool to measure the volume of a liquid.
A ruler or meter stick is the best tool to measure the length or height of an object.
A stopwatch is the best tool to measure how long something takes.
A spring scale is the best tool to measure force or weight.
A rain gauge is the best tool to measure how much rain fell.
An anemometer is the best tool to measure how fast the wind blows.
A wind vane is the best tool to find the direction of the wind.
A barometer is the best tool to measure air pressure and predict weather changes.
A compass is the best tool to find north.
Binoculars are the best tool to watch birds or animals that are far away.
A calculator is used to compute averages and other numbers from data.
A camera can record observations of an experiment.
A data table is used to record measurements during an investigation.
A graph is used to show patterns in data.
A map is used to show locations.
A meterstick is used to measure the distance a toy car rolls.
A measuring cup is used to measure volumes of water in the kitchen.
A tape measure is used to measure the circumference of a tree.
A pH meter or litmus paper is used to test if a solution is acidic or basic.
A seismometer is used to detect earthquakes.
A Geiger counter detects radiation.
A voltmeter measures voltage, and an ammeter measures current.
A hygrometer measures humidity.
A light meter measures the brightness of light.
A sound meter measures loudness in decibels.
A hot plate or burner is used to heat substances in the laboratory.
A test tube holder or tongs is used to hold a hot test tube.
A funnel and filter paper are used to filter mixtures.
When two groups are kept at different temperatures and everything else is the same, the variable being tested is temperature.
When plants get different amounts of water and everything else is the same, the variable being tested is the amount of water.
When plants get different amounts of light and everything else is the same, the variable being tested is the amount of light.
When plants are grown in different types of soil, the variable being tested is the type of soil.
When some plants get fertilizer and others do not, the variable being tested is fertilizer.
When balls are dropped from different heights, the variable being tested is the height.
When ramps are set at different angles, the variable being tested is the steepness of the ramp.
When cars roll over different surfaces, the variable being tested is the type of surface.
When different amounts of salt are added to water, the variable being tested is the amount of salt.
When different brands of a product are compared, the variable being tested is the brand.
The variable that is different between the groups is the variable being tested.
The factor that is measured at the end of an experiment, such as plant height or temperature, is the dependent variable.
The conditions that are the same for all groups are the controlled variables.
An adaptation is a trait or behavior that helps an organism survive and reproduce in its environment.
Structural adaptations are physical features such as body shape, color, and body coverings.
Behavioral adaptations are things an organism does, such as migrating, hibernating, or hunting in groups.
Camouflage is an adaptation that helps an animal blend in with its surroundings to hide from predators or prey.
Camouflage helps animals hide from predators.
Animals with specialized cells that change color, such as chameleons and octopuses, use color change to hide from predators.
Stick insects look like twigs and leaf insects look like leaves to hide from predators.
A white coat helps an animal hide in the snow.
Stripes on tigers and zebras help them blend into tall grass or confuse predators.
Mimicry is an adaptation in which one species looks like another, more dangerous species.
The viceroy butterfly mimics the bad tasting monarch butterfly so predators avoid it.
Bright warning colors tell predators that an animal is poisonous or tastes bad, such as poison dart frogs.
Some harmless snakes look like poisonous coral snakes.
Migration is the seasonal movement of animals from one place to another to find food, warmth, or a place to breed.
Many birds migrate south in the fall to find food and warmer weather.
Monarch butterflies migrate to Mexico for the winter.
Whales migrate to warm waters to give birth.
Hibernation is a state of deep sleep in which body temperature, heart rate, and breathing slow down to save energy during winter.
Animals hibernate in winter when food is scarce.
Bears, groundhogs, bats, and some squirrels hibernate.
Before hibernating, animals eat a lot of food and store it as body fat.
Estivation is a period of inactivity during hot dry weather.
Dormancy is a period when an organism's growth and activity slow down, like seeds and trees in winter.
Some animals store food for the winter, such as squirrels burying nuts.
Animals grow thicker fur in winter to stay warm and shed it in spring.
An instinct is an inherited behavior that an animal is born knowing how to do.
Spiders spinning webs, birds building nests, and babies crying are instincts.
Migration and hibernation are instinctive behaviors.
A learned behavior is a behavior an animal acquires through experience or by watching others.
Reading, riding a bike, and a dog learning tricks are learned behaviors.
Young animals learn to hunt by watching their parents.
Training a dog to sit is an example of learned behavior.
Imprinting is when young animals such as ducklings follow the first moving object they see.
Variation is the differences in traits among individuals of the same species.
Natural selection is the process by which organisms with traits better suited to their environment survive and reproduce more.
Charles Darwin developed the theory of evolution by natural selection.
Darwin studied finches on the Galapagos Islands that had different beak shapes suited to different foods.
In natural selection, individuals with helpful traits survive and pass those traits to their offspring.
Over many generations, natural selection can cause a population to change.
Evolution is the change in the inherited traits of a species over many generations.
Overproduction, variation, competition, and survival of the fittest are parts of natural selection.
Organisms that are better adapted to their environment are more likely to survive and reproduce.
The peppered moth population became darker during the Industrial Revolution because dark moths were better camouflaged on sooty trees.
Bacteria can become resistant to antibiotics through natural selection.
Insects can become resistant to pesticides over many generations.
Mutations are changes in DNA that can produce new traits.
Mutations can be helpful, harmful, or have no effect.
Helpful mutations may be passed on and increase in a population by natural selection.
When an environment changes, species must adapt, move, or become extinct.
Organisms that cannot adapt to changes in their environment may die out.
Similar body structures in different species, such as the bones in a human arm, a bat wing, and a whale flipper, suggest a common ancestor.
Homologous structures are similar structures in different species inherited from a common ancestor.
Similar embryos and similar DNA show that species are related.
Selective breeding, or artificial selection, is when humans breed plants or animals for desired traits.
Dog breeds were developed by selective breeding.
Farmers use selective breeding to produce cows that give more milk and crops that resist disease.
Thick fur and a layer of fat are adaptations for living in cold climates.
Large ears help desert animals release heat.
Long roots help plants get water in dry places.
Spines on a cactus protect it from animals and reduce water loss.
Waxy leaves help plants keep water.
Plants in shady forests often have large leaves to capture more light.
Webbed feet are an adaptation for swimming.
Sharp claws and teeth are adaptations for catching and eating prey.
Long sticky tongues help anteaters and frogs catch insects.
Thick shells protect turtles and snails from predators.
Fish have gills to get oxygen from water and fins for swimming.
Gills are an adaptation for living in water.
Some fish living in deep dark water produce their own light.
Animals in the Arctic are often white to blend in with snow.
Desert animals often have light colors to reflect sunlight.
A long neck helps a giraffe reach food high in trees that other animals cannot reach.
Nocturnal behavior helps desert animals avoid daytime heat.
Herding helps animals protect themselves from predators.
Schooling helps fish confuse predators.
Plants that make seeds that can be carried by wind spread to new areas.
Bright flowers and sweet nectar attract pollinators.
Some plants make chemicals that keep insects from eating them.
An inherited trait is passed from parents to offspring through genes.
Acquired traits are developed during an organism's life, such as scars or learned skills.
A scar, a tan, big muscles from exercise, and knowing how to read are acquired traits.
Eye color, hair color, height potential, and blood type are inherited traits.
The environment can affect how traits are expressed, such as plants growing taller with more light.
Flamingos are pink because of the food they eat, an environmental effect.
Hydrangea flower color depends on the acidity of the soil.
Organisms produce more offspring than can survive, so they compete for resources.
Species that reproduce quickly can adapt to change more quickly.
Adaptations develop over many generations, not in a single lifetime.
An individual organism cannot evolve; populations evolve over time.
Birds migrate south in winter to find food, because insects, fruit, and seeds are scarce in the north in winter.
Scientists classify organisms into groups based on their shared characteristics.
The levels of classification are kingdom, phylum, class, order, family, genus, and species.
A species is a group of similar organisms that can mate and produce fertile offspring.
Carolus Linnaeus developed the system of classification and two part scientific names.
A scientific name is made of the genus and species, such as Homo sapiens for humans.
A dichotomous key uses pairs of choices to identify organisms.
Organisms are grouped into domains and kingdoms such as animals, plants, fungi, protists, and bacteria.
Animals are multicellular consumers that must eat other organisms for food.
Vertebrates are animals with a backbone.
Invertebrates are animals without a backbone.
Most animal species are invertebrates.
The five groups of vertebrates are fish, amphibians, reptiles, birds, and mammals.
Mammals have hair or fur, are warm blooded, and feed their young milk.
Most mammals give birth to live young.
The platypus and echidna are mammals that lay eggs.
Marsupials such as kangaroos and koalas carry their young in a pouch.
Whales, dolphins, and seals are mammals that live in water and breathe air with lungs.
Bats are the only mammals that can truly fly.
Humans, dogs, cats, horses, cows, mice, and monkeys are mammals.
Birds have feathers, wings, beaks, hollow bones, and lay eggs with hard shells.
Birds are warm blooded vertebrates.
Hollow bones make birds light for flying.
Penguins and ostriches are birds that cannot fly.
Reptiles have dry scaly skin, breathe with lungs, and lay eggs with leathery shells on land.
Snakes, lizards, turtles, alligators, and crocodiles are reptiles.
Reptiles are cold blooded, so their body temperature changes with their surroundings.
Lizards and snakes bask in the sun to warm their bodies.
Amphibians such as frogs, toads, and salamanders live part of their lives in water and part on land.
Amphibians have moist skin and lay jelly like eggs in water.
Young amphibians breathe with gills and adults usually breathe with lungs and through their skin.
Fish are cold blooded vertebrates that live in water, breathe with gills, and have fins and scales.
Gills take oxygen out of water.
Sharks are fish with skeletons made of cartilage.
Cold blooded animals, or ectotherms, get body heat from their environment.
Warm blooded animals, or endotherms, keep a constant body temperature using heat from food.
Insects have six legs, three body parts, and usually two antennae and wings.
The three body parts of an insect are the head, thorax, and abdomen.
Ants, bees, beetles, butterflies, flies, grasshoppers, and mosquitoes are insects.
Spiders have eight legs and two body parts and are arachnids.
Arachnids include spiders, scorpions, ticks, and mites.
Arthropods have jointed legs, segmented bodies, and an exoskeleton.
An exoskeleton is a hard outer covering that protects and supports the body.
Arthropods molt, shedding their exoskeleton as they grow.
Crustaceans such as crabs, lobsters, and shrimp are arthropods that live mostly in water.
Mollusks are soft bodied invertebrates, such as snails, clams, oysters, squid, and octopuses.
Many mollusks have shells.
Worms such as earthworms are invertebrates with long soft bodies.
Earthworms breathe through their moist skin.
Jellyfish and corals are invertebrates with stinging tentacles.
Sponges are simple invertebrates that filter food from water.
Starfish, or sea stars, and sea urchins are echinoderms with spiny skin.
A sea star can regrow a lost arm.
Animals have adaptations for getting food, moving, protecting themselves, and reproducing.
Herbivores eat only plants.
Carnivores eat only other animals.
Omnivores eat both plants and animals.
Deer, rabbits, cows, horses, and grasshoppers are herbivores.
Lions, wolves, hawks, owls, and snakes are carnivores.
Bears, raccoons, humans, and pigs are omnivores.
Scavengers such as vultures eat dead animals.
Predators hunt and eat other animals called prey.
Owls are nocturnal predators that hunt at night.
Nocturnal animals are active at night and sleep during the day.
Diurnal animals are active during the day.
A life cycle is the series of stages an organism goes through during its life.
Metamorphosis is a major change in body form during an animal's life cycle.
Complete metamorphosis has four stages: egg, larva, pupa, and adult.
Butterflies, moths, beetles, flies, and bees go through complete metamorphosis.
A caterpillar is the larva of a butterfly or moth.
A butterfly pupa is called a chrysalis, and a moth pupa forms inside a cocoon.
Incomplete metamorphosis has three stages: egg, nymph, and adult.
Grasshoppers, crickets, dragonflies, and cockroaches go through incomplete metamorphosis.
A nymph looks like a small adult without wings.
The life cycle of a frog is egg, tadpole, froglet, and adult frog.
A tadpole lives in water, has a tail, and breathes with gills.
As a tadpole grows into a frog it develops legs and lungs and loses its tail.
Mammals grow and develop inside their mother and look like small adults when born.
Birds hatch from eggs and are cared for by their parents.
Many fish and amphibians lay many eggs because few of the young survive.
Animals that care for their young usually have fewer offspring.
Animals reproduce to make more of their own kind and continue their species.
Animals grow from young to adult and eventually die.
Animals need food, water, oxygen, and shelter to survive.
Animals get energy by eating plants or other animals.
Animals breathe in oxygen and breathe out carbon dioxide.
Animals use lungs, gills, or their skin to get oxygen.
Animals move by walking, running, swimming, flying, crawling, slithering, or hopping.
Fins and a streamlined body help fish swim.
Webbed feet help ducks, frogs, and beavers swim.
Wings help birds, bats, and insects fly.
The shape of a bird's beak is adapted to the food it eats.
Hawks and eagles have sharp hooked beaks for tearing meat and sharp talons for catching prey.
Hummingbirds have long thin beaks to drink nectar from flowers.
Woodpeckers have strong pointed beaks to drill into trees for insects.
Ducks have flat wide bills to strain food from water.
Seed eating birds such as finches have short thick strong beaks to crack seeds.
Pelicans have pouched bills to scoop up fish.
Long legs help wading birds such as herons walk in water.
Animals with sharp teeth and claws are usually predators.
Animals with eyes on the sides of their head, such as rabbits and deer, can see predators coming from many directions.
Predators such as owls and cats have eyes in front of the head to judge distance.
Some animals live in groups, such as herds, packs, flocks, schools, and colonies, for protection and hunting.
Bees and ants live in colonies where different members have different jobs.
Honeybees perform a dance to communicate the location of food.
Animals communicate with sounds, smells, colors, and movements.
Fireflies flash light to attract mates.
Male birds sing songs and display bright feathers to attract mates.
Animals that live in cold places have thick fur, blubber, or feathers to stay warm.
Blubber is a thick layer of fat that keeps whales, seals, and walruses warm.
Polar bears have thick fur and white color that helps them blend in with snow.
Arctic foxes and snowshoe hares turn white in winter for camouflage.
Desert animals such as camels can survive with little water.
Camels store fat in their humps and can go a long time without water.
Many desert animals are nocturnal to avoid the heat of the day.
Fennec foxes and jackrabbits have large ears that release heat.
Elephants use their large ears to release heat and their trunks to grab food and drink water.
Giraffes have long necks to reach leaves high in trees.
Beavers have sharp teeth to cut down trees and build dams.
Skunks spray a bad smelling liquid to defend themselves.
Porcupines have sharp quills for protection.
Turtles have hard shells for protection.
Armadillos have bony plates for protection.
Octopuses and squid squirt ink to escape predators.
Chameleons and octopuses can change color to blend in with their surroundings.
Some animals play dead to avoid being eaten, such as the opossum.
Sea turtles return to the beach where they hatched to lay their eggs.
Salmon swim upstream to the stream where they hatched to lay eggs.
Snakes shed their skin as they grow.
Snakes smell with their tongues.
Frogs catch insects with their long sticky tongues.
Dogs have a keen sense of smell.
Bats use echolocation, sending out sounds and listening for echoes, to find insects in the dark.
Dolphins use echolocation to find food.
Owls have excellent hearing and eyesight to hunt at night.
Cats have whiskers that help them feel their surroundings.
The shell of a snail protects its soft body.
Sharks have keen senses of smell and can detect electric fields of prey.
Earthworms have no eyes but can sense light.
Moles have strong claws for digging underground.
Kangaroos have strong back legs for hopping.
Cheetahs are the fastest land animals and use speed to catch prey.
Horses, deer, and zebras run fast to escape predators.
The blue whale is the largest animal that has ever lived.
Corals are tiny animals related to jellyfish.
Sponges and corals stay attached in one place as adults.
Mosquitoes can spread diseases such as malaria.
Ticks can spread Lyme disease.
Fleas and lice are parasites that live on animals.
Tapeworms are parasites that live inside the intestines of animals.
The human body has many organ systems that work together.
The skeletal system is made of bones that support the body, protect organs, and allow movement.
The skeleton gives the body shape and support.
Bones make blood cells in the bone marrow.
Bones store minerals such as calcium.
The skull protects the brain.
The rib cage protects the heart and lungs.
The spinal column, or backbone, protects the spinal cord.
The adult human skeleton has 206 bones.
Joints are places where two bones meet and allow movement.
Ligaments connect bones to other bones at joints.
Tendons connect muscles to bones.
Cartilage is a flexible tissue that cushions joints and forms the nose and ears.
The muscular system works with the skeletal system to move the body.
Muscles move bones by contracting and pulling on them.
Muscles work in pairs; when one contracts the other relaxes.
The biceps and triceps are a muscle pair that bend and straighten the arm.
Skeletal muscles are voluntary muscles that we control.
Smooth muscles in the stomach and intestines are involuntary.
Cardiac muscle is found only in the heart.
The circulatory system transports blood, oxygen, nutrients, and wastes throughout the body.
The circulatory system includes the heart, blood, and blood vessels.
The heart is a muscle that pumps blood through the body.
The human heart has four chambers: two atria and two ventricles.
Arteries carry blood away from the heart.
Veins carry blood back to the heart.
Capillaries are tiny blood vessels where oxygen and nutrients pass into cells and wastes are picked up.
Red blood cells carry oxygen from the lungs to the body cells.
White blood cells fight infections and disease.
Platelets help blood clot to stop bleeding.
Plasma is the liquid part of blood.
The pulse is the beating of the heart felt in the arteries.
Heart rate increases during exercise to deliver more oxygen to muscles.
The respiratory system takes in oxygen and gets rid of carbon dioxide.
The lungs are the main organs of the respiratory system.
Air travels through the nose, trachea, bronchi, and into the lungs.
Alveoli are tiny air sacs in the lungs where oxygen enters the blood and carbon dioxide leaves the blood.
The diaphragm is a muscle that helps you breathe by moving air into and out of the lungs.
Breathing rate increases during exercise because muscles need more oxygen.
The respiratory system and circulatory system work together to bring oxygen to cells.
Smoking damages the lungs and can cause lung cancer and emphysema.
The digestive system breaks down food into nutrients the body can absorb.
Digestion begins in the mouth, where teeth chew food and saliva starts to break down starches.
The esophagus is a tube that carries food from the mouth to the stomach.
The stomach uses acid and enzymes to break down food.
Most digestion and absorption of nutrients takes place in the small intestine.
Villi in the small intestine increase surface area to absorb nutrients into the blood.
The large intestine absorbs water from undigested food.
The liver makes bile, which helps digest fats.
The pancreas makes digestive enzymes and insulin.
The digestive system and circulatory system work together to deliver nutrients to cells.
The excretory system removes liquid wastes from the body.
The kidneys filter wastes from the blood and make urine.
Urine is stored in the bladder.
The skin, lungs, and kidneys all remove wastes from the body.
Sweat glands in the skin release water and salts and help cool the body.
The integumentary system includes the skin, hair, and nails.
The skin is the largest organ of the body and protects the body from germs and water loss.
The nervous system controls and coordinates the body's activities.
The nervous system includes the brain, spinal cord, and nerves.
The brain is the control center of the body.
The cerebrum controls thinking, memory, and the senses.
The cerebellum controls balance and coordination.
The brain stem controls involuntary actions such as breathing and heartbeat.
Nerves carry messages between the brain and the rest of the body.
A reflex is an automatic response that happens without thinking, such as pulling a hand away from a hot stove.
The five senses are sight, hearing, smell, taste, and touch.
The eyes detect light, the ears detect sound, the nose detects smell, the tongue detects taste, and the skin detects touch.
The pupil is the opening that lets light into the eye, and the iris controls its size.
The retina at the back of the eye has cells that detect light and send signals to the brain through the optic nerve.
The lens of the eye focuses light on the retina.
The eardrum vibrates when sound waves reach it.
The cochlea in the inner ear changes vibrations into nerve signals.
The inner ear also helps control balance.
The endocrine system uses hormones to send chemical messages through the blood.
Glands of the endocrine system release hormones that control growth, development, and metabolism.
The endocrine system and the nervous system both send messages to control body functions.
Insulin is a hormone that controls the level of sugar in the blood.
Diabetes is a disease in which the body cannot control blood sugar properly.
Adrenaline is a hormone that prepares the body to react to danger.
The thyroid gland controls how fast the body uses energy.
The pituitary gland controls growth and other glands.
The immune system defends the body against germs and disease.
White blood cells attack and destroy pathogens that enter the body.
Antibodies are proteins made by white blood cells that fight specific germs.
The lymphatic system carries lymph and helps the immune system fight infection.
The reproductive system produces offspring.
The male reproductive system produces sperm, and the female reproductive system produces eggs.
Fertilization happens when a sperm cell joins with an egg cell.
A developing baby gets oxygen and nutrients from the mother through the placenta and umbilical cord.
Puberty is the stage when the body becomes able to reproduce.
The stages of the human life cycle are infancy, childhood, adolescence, adulthood, and old age.
Nutrients are substances in food that the body needs for energy, growth, and repair.
The six main nutrients are carbohydrates, proteins, fats, vitamins, minerals, and water.
Carbohydrates such as sugars and starches are the main source of energy for the body.
Bread, pasta, rice, and potatoes are rich in carbohydrates.
Proteins are needed to build and repair muscles and body tissues.
Meat, fish, eggs, beans, and nuts are good sources of protein.
Fats store energy and help the body absorb some vitamins.
Vitamins and minerals are needed in small amounts to keep the body healthy.
Calcium builds strong bones and teeth and is found in milk and dairy products.
Iron is a mineral needed to make red blood cells that carry oxygen.
Vitamin C is found in citrus fruits such as oranges and prevents scurvy.
Vitamin D helps the body absorb calcium and is made when skin is exposed to sunlight.
Fiber from fruits, vegetables, and whole grains helps digestion.
Water makes up about 60 percent of the human body and is needed for all body processes.
A balanced diet includes fruits, vegetables, grains, proteins, and dairy.
Food energy is measured in Calories.
Eating too much sugar and fat can lead to obesity and heart disease.
Regular exercise strengthens the heart, lungs, muscles, and bones.
Getting enough sleep helps the body and brain rest and repair.
Washing hands with soap helps prevent the spread of germs and disease.
Covering a cough or sneeze helps keep germs from spreading.
Sunscreen protects skin from ultraviolet rays that can cause sunburn and skin cancer.
Wearing a helmet protects the head and brain from injury.
Drugs, alcohol, and tobacco can damage organs and harm health.
Nicotine in tobacco is addictive.
Blood carries carbon dioxide from body cells to the lungs to be exhaled.
When you exhale you breathe out carbon dioxide and water vapor.
When you inhale you breathe in oxygen.
Body temperature is controlled by the brain and stays at about 37 degrees Celsius.
Shivering produces heat to warm the body.
Sweating cools the body as sweat evaporates from the skin.
Teeth have different shapes: incisors cut, canines tear, and molars grind food.
Saliva contains enzymes that begin digesting starch.
The appendix is a small organ attached to the large intestine.
The cell is the basic unit of structure and function in all living things.
All living things are made of one or more cells.
The cell theory states that all living things are made of cells, cells are the basic unit of life, and all cells come from existing cells.
Robert Hooke first observed and named cells when he looked at cork through a microscope.
Anton van Leeuwenhoek was the first to observe living single celled organisms with a microscope.
A microscope is used to see cells and other objects too small to see with the eye.
A compound light microscope uses lenses and light to magnify small objects such as cells.
Electron microscopes can magnify objects much more than light microscopes.
To find the total magnification of a microscope, multiply the eyepiece power by the objective lens power.
Unicellular organisms are made of only one cell, such as bacteria, amoebas, and paramecia.
Multicellular organisms are made of many cells, such as plants, animals, and most fungi.
In multicellular organisms cells are specialized to do different jobs.
Levels of organization in multicellular organisms are cells, tissues, organs, organ systems, and organisms.
A tissue is a group of similar cells that work together to do a job.
An organ is a group of different tissues that work together to perform a function.
An organ system is a group of organs that work together to perform a function.
The heart is an organ made of muscle tissue, nerve tissue, and connective tissue.
Muscle cells, nerve cells, blood cells, skin cells, and bone cells are specialized animal cells.
Nerve cells, or neurons, are long and thin to carry messages over long distances.
Red blood cells carry oxygen and have no nucleus.
Root hair cells have a large surface area to absorb water.
The cell membrane is a thin flexible layer that surrounds the cell and controls what enters and leaves the cell.
The cell membrane is selectively permeable, letting some substances pass but not others.
The nucleus is the control center of the cell and contains DNA.
The nucleus contains chromosomes, which carry genetic information.
Cytoplasm is the jelly like fluid inside the cell where organelles are found.
Organelles are small structures inside cells that perform specific functions.
Mitochondria are organelles that release energy from food through cellular respiration.
Mitochondria are called the powerhouse of the cell.
Chloroplasts are organelles in plant cells that capture light energy to make food by photosynthesis.
Chloroplasts contain chlorophyll, a green pigment that absorbs sunlight.
The cell wall is a rigid layer outside the cell membrane that supports and protects plant cells.
Plant cells have cell walls and chloroplasts, but animal cells do not.
Plant cells have a large central vacuole that stores water.
Animal cells have small vacuoles or none and do not have cell walls.
Both plant and animal cells have a cell membrane, nucleus, cytoplasm, and mitochondria.
Vacuoles store water, food, and waste.
Ribosomes are organelles that make proteins.
The endoplasmic reticulum transports materials within the cell.
The Golgi apparatus packages and ships proteins.
Lysosomes contain enzymes that break down wastes and old cell parts.
Bacteria cells have no nucleus; their DNA floats in the cytoplasm.
Prokaryotes are cells without a nucleus, such as bacteria.
Eukaryotes are cells with a nucleus, such as plant, animal, fungus, and protist cells.
Cells need energy, water, and nutrients to live and they produce wastes.
Cells get energy by breaking down sugar such as glucose in cellular respiration.
Cellular respiration uses oxygen and glucose to release energy, carbon dioxide, and water.
Diffusion is the movement of particles from an area of higher concentration to an area of lower concentration.
Osmosis is the diffusion of water across a membrane.
Oxygen enters cells and carbon dioxide leaves cells by diffusion.
Active transport uses energy to move materials across a cell membrane.
Cells divide to make new cells for growth and to repair damaged tissues.
Mitosis is cell division that produces two identical cells with the same number of chromosomes.
Meiosis is cell division that produces sex cells, or gametes, with half the number of chromosomes.
Sperm and egg cells are sex cells produced by meiosis.
Cancer is a disease in which cells divide uncontrollably.
As an organism grows, its cells divide and the number of cells increases.
Stem cells can develop into many different types of cells.
Cells are very small, and most can only be seen with a microscope.
An amoeba moves with pseudopods, or false feet.
A paramecium moves using tiny hairs called cilia.
Euglena has a flagellum, a whiplike tail, and chloroplasts to make food.
Some single celled organisms move using a flagellum.
Yeast is a single celled fungus used to make bread rise.
Proteins, carbohydrates, lipids, and nucleic acids are the main molecules of living things.
Enzymes are proteins that speed up chemical reactions in living things.
DNA is found in the nucleus of a cell.
Living things are made of cells, use energy, grow and develop, reproduce, respond to their environment, and get rid of wastes.
Characteristics of living things include growth, reproduction, using energy, responding to stimuli, and being made of cells.
Nonliving things do not grow, reproduce, or use energy on their own.
A virus is not considered alive because it is not made of cells and can only reproduce inside a host cell.
Homeostasis is the ability of an organism to keep stable internal conditions.
Sweating and shivering help the body maintain a stable temperature.
A stimulus is a change in the environment that causes an organism to respond.
Organisms need food, water, air, space, and shelter to survive.
Water makes up most of the mass of a cell.
Living things are made mostly of the elements carbon, hydrogen, oxygen, and nitrogen.
Trees, grass, flowers, and all other plants have cells with cell walls.
Oak trees, maple trees, and pine trees are plants made of cells with cell walls and chloroplasts.
Dogs, humans, frogs, and other animals have cells without cell walls.
Fungi such as mushrooms also have cell walls, but they have no chloroplasts.
Bacteria have cell walls but no nucleus.
Cells divide by mitosis to make copies of themselves for growth and repair.
Mitosis produces new body cells so organisms can grow and replace damaged cells.
An ecosystem is all the living and nonliving things in an area and how they interact.
Biotic factors are the living parts of an ecosystem, such as plants, animals, fungi, and bacteria.
Abiotic factors are the nonliving parts of an ecosystem, such as sunlight, water, air, temperature, soil, and rocks.
A population is all the members of one species living in the same area.
A community is all the populations of different species living in the same area.
A habitat is the place where an organism lives and gets food, water, shelter, and space.
A niche is the role an organism plays in its ecosystem, including what it eats and where it lives.
Ecology is the study of how organisms interact with each other and their environment.
The Sun is the main source of energy for almost all ecosystems.
Energy flows through an ecosystem from the Sun to producers to consumers.
Producers are organisms that make their own food, such as plants and algae.
Producers use energy from sunlight to make food by photosynthesis.
Consumers are organisms that get energy by eating other organisms.
Primary consumers are herbivores that eat producers.
Secondary consumers eat primary consumers.
Tertiary consumers eat secondary consumers and are often top predators.
Decomposers break down dead plants and animals and return nutrients to the soil.
Bacteria and fungi such as mushrooms and molds are decomposers.
Without decomposers, dead organisms and wastes would pile up and nutrients would not be recycled.
Decomposers recycle nutrients so that producers can use them again.
Worms, millipedes, and some insects help decompose dead material.
A food chain shows how energy passes from one organism to another as each organism eats another.
In a food chain the arrows point from the organism that is eaten to the organism that eats it, showing the flow of energy.
A food chain always begins with a producer.
An example food chain is grass, grasshopper, frog, snake, hawk.
An example food chain is algae, small fish, large fish, heron.
A food web is many connected food chains in an ecosystem.
A food web shows that most animals eat more than one kind of food.
An energy pyramid shows that energy decreases at each higher level of a food chain.
Only about 10 percent of the energy at one level of a food chain passes to the next level.
Most energy at each level of a food chain is used for life processes or lost as heat.
There are fewer organisms at the top of an energy pyramid than at the bottom.
Producers have the most energy in an energy pyramid and top predators have the least.
If a predator is removed from an ecosystem, its prey population usually increases.
If the prey population increases, the predator population may later increase because there is more food.
If a producer population decreases, the herbivores that eat it will decrease.
If the population of one organism in a food web changes, other populations in the web are also affected.
Predator and prey populations rise and fall in cycles, such as lynx and snowshoe hares.
Wolves reintroduced to Yellowstone reduced the elk population, which let trees and plants recover.
Limiting factors such as food, water, space, shelter, predators, and disease limit the size of a population.
Carrying capacity is the largest population an environment can support.
When a population exceeds the carrying capacity, organisms die from lack of resources.
Competition happens when organisms need the same limited resources such as food, water, light, or space.
Plants compete for sunlight, water, space, and nutrients in the soil.
Animals compete for food, water, mates, and territory.
Symbiosis is a close relationship between two different species.
Mutualism is a relationship in which both organisms benefit.
Bees and flowers have a mutualistic relationship: bees get nectar and flowers get pollinated.
Clownfish and sea anemones have a mutualistic relationship.
Lichens are a mutualism between a fungus and an alga.
Commensalism is a relationship in which one organism benefits and the other is not helped or harmed.
Barnacles living on a whale is an example of commensalism.
Birds nesting in trees is an example of commensalism.
Parasitism is a relationship in which one organism, the parasite, benefits and the other, the host, is harmed.
Ticks, fleas, lice, tapeworms, and mosquitoes are parasites.
Predation is when one organism hunts and eats another.
Organisms depend on other organisms for food, shelter, pollination, and seed dispersal.
All organisms in an ecosystem depend on bacteria and other decomposers in the soil to recycle nutrients.
Ecological succession is the gradual change in the kinds of organisms in an area over time.
Primary succession begins on bare rock where there is no soil, such as after a volcanic eruption or a glacier retreats.
Lichens and mosses are pioneer species that are the first to grow on bare rock and help form soil.
Secondary succession happens where soil remains after a disturbance such as a fire, flood, or abandoned farm.
A climax community is a stable community at the end of succession.
Biodiversity is the variety of living things in an area.
Ecosystems with high biodiversity are more stable and recover more easily from changes.
Keystone species have a large effect on their ecosystem, such as sea otters eating sea urchins that eat kelp.
Natural events such as floods, fires, droughts, and volcanic eruptions can change ecosystems.
Humans change ecosystems by clearing land, building, farming, polluting, and introducing new species.
When a habitat is destroyed, organisms must move, adapt, or die.
The carbon cycle moves carbon between the atmosphere, living things, oceans, and rocks.
Plants remove carbon dioxide from the air during photosynthesis and animals release it during respiration.
Burning fuels and decomposition release carbon dioxide into the atmosphere.
The oxygen and carbon dioxide cycles connect plants and animals.
The nitrogen cycle moves nitrogen between the air, soil, and living things.
Nitrogen fixing bacteria in soil and in roots of legumes change nitrogen gas into compounds plants can use.
Plants need nitrogen to make proteins.
Matter is recycled in ecosystems, but energy flows through and is not recycled.
Water, carbon, nitrogen, and oxygen are cycled through ecosystems.
A pond ecosystem includes algae, plants, insects, fish, frogs, and microorganisms.
Plankton are tiny organisms that float in water and are food for many sea animals.
Zooplankton are tiny animals that eat phytoplankton.
Kelp forests are underwater ecosystems of large brown algae.
Grassland ecosystems include grasses, prairie dogs, bison, hawks, coyotes, and snakes.
Forest ecosystems have layers such as the canopy, understory, and forest floor.
The forest floor gets little sunlight because the tree canopy blocks it.
Deserts have plants and animals adapted to survive with little water.
Owls eat mice, and owl pellets contain the bones and fur of the animals they ate.
Animals that eat the same food in the same area compete with each other.
A food chain that loses its producers will collapse.
Algae are producers at the base of many aquatic food chains.
Large carnivores need large territories to find enough food.
An organism's environment includes all the living and nonliving things around it.
Organisms are adapted to the conditions of the ecosystem they live in.
Changes in temperature or rainfall can change which organisms can live in an area.
Bacteria in the soil decompose dead organisms and release nutrients for plants.
Fungi absorb nutrients from dead and decaying matter.
Mold grows on old bread and fruit because it decomposes them.
Microorganisms in the soil help plants grow by breaking down organic matter.
Some animals change the environment, such as beavers building dams that create ponds.
Prairie dogs dig burrows that other animals use for shelter.
Elephants knock down trees, which changes forests into grasslands.
Coral reefs provide habitat for many ocean species.
Mangrove trees protect coastlines and provide habitat for young fish.
Population density is the number of individuals in a certain area.
Migration of animals into or out of an area changes the population size.
Birth rate and death rate affect population size.
Overpopulation of deer can happen when their predators are removed.
A balanced ecosystem has stable populations of producers, consumers, and decomposers.
Heredity is the passing of traits from parents to offspring.
Traits are characteristics that an organism has, such as eye color or flower color.
Genes are segments of DNA on chromosomes that carry instructions for traits.
DNA is the molecule that carries genetic information in all living things.
DNA has a double helix shape like a twisted ladder.
DNA is made of four bases: adenine, thymine, guanine, and cytosine.
Chromosomes are structures in the nucleus made of DNA and proteins.
Humans have 46 chromosomes, or 23 pairs, in each body cell.
Human sex cells, sperm and eggs, have 23 chromosomes.
Offspring receive half of their chromosomes from the mother and half from the father.
Each parent contributes one allele for each gene to its offspring.
Alleles are different forms of a gene.
A dominant allele hides the effect of a recessive allele.
A recessive trait only appears when an organism has two recessive alleles.
Dominant alleles are written with capital letters and recessive alleles with lowercase letters.
Genotype is the combination of alleles an organism has, such as Tt.
Phenotype is the physical appearance or trait that is expressed, such as tall or short.
Homozygous means having two identical alleles, such as TT or tt.
Heterozygous means having two different alleles, such as Tt.
A Punnett square shows the possible combinations of alleles in offspring.
When two heterozygous parents, Tt and Tt, are crossed, about 75 percent of offspring show the dominant trait and 25 percent show the recessive trait.
A cross of Tt and Tt gives a ratio of 3 dominant to 1 recessive.
A cross of TT and tt gives all offspring with the genotype Tt that show the dominant trait.
A cross of Tt and tt gives 50 percent dominant and 50 percent recessive offspring.
Gregor Mendel is called the father of genetics because he studied how traits are inherited in pea plants.
Mendel found that traits are passed on by factors, now called genes, that come in pairs.
Some traits show incomplete dominance, where the offspring have a blend, such as pink flowers from red and white parents.
Codominance is when both alleles are fully expressed, such as a roan coat or AB blood type.
Many traits such as height and skin color are controlled by more than one gene.
Sex is determined by chromosomes; females have XX and males have XY.
Genetic disorders such as cystic fibrosis and sickle cell anemia are caused by mutations in genes.
A mutation is a change in the DNA sequence.
Mutations can be caused by errors in copying DNA or by radiation and some chemicals.
Mutations in sex cells can be passed to offspring, but mutations in body cells are not inherited.
Sexual reproduction requires two parents and produces offspring with a mix of traits from both parents.
In sexual reproduction, offspring are genetically different from their parents and from each other.
Sexual reproduction produces variation, which helps species survive changes in the environment.
Asexual reproduction requires only one parent and produces offspring identical to the parent.
Bacteria reproduce asexually by binary fission, splitting into two cells.
Yeast and hydras reproduce asexually by budding.
Planaria and sea stars can reproduce by regeneration, growing new body parts.
Clones are genetically identical copies of an organism.
Offspring produced by asexual reproduction are clones of the parent.
Asexual reproduction is fast and does not require finding a mate.
Fertilization is the joining of an egg cell and a sperm cell to form a zygote.
A zygote is a fertilized egg that develops into an embryo.
In external fertilization, eggs are fertilized outside the body, as in most fish and frogs.
In internal fertilization, eggs are fertilized inside the female's body, as in mammals, birds, and reptiles.
Offspring look similar to their parents because they inherit genes from them.
Siblings look different from each other because each receives a different combination of genes from the parents.
Identical twins have the same DNA because they come from one fertilized egg that split.
Genetic engineering is changing the DNA of an organism to give it new traits.
Insulin for diabetes can be made by bacteria that have been given a human gene.
Genetically modified crops can resist insects or disease.
A pedigree is a chart that shows how a trait is passed through generations of a family.
Traits such as dimples, freckles, attached earlobes, and the ability to roll the tongue are inherited.
Puppies in the same litter can look different because of different combinations of genes from their parents.
The genetic code in DNA determines the proteins a cell makes.
Proteins made from genes determine an organism's traits.
Instructions for traits are found in the genes on the chromosomes in the nucleus.
An organism's traits are determined by both its genes and its environment.
Gametes are sex cells, sperm and eggs, that contain half the number of chromosomes.
Meiosis makes gametes, and mitosis makes body cells.
Dogs have 78 chromosomes in their body cells, and their sex cells have 39.
DNA fingerprinting can identify people because each person's DNA is unique.
Microorganisms are living things too small to see without a microscope, such as bacteria, some fungi, and protists.
Bacteria are single celled organisms without a nucleus.
Bacteria are found almost everywhere, in soil, water, air, and inside other organisms.
Most bacteria are helpful or harmless, but some cause disease.
Helpful bacteria in the intestines help digest food and make vitamins.
Bacteria are used to make yogurt, cheese, and other foods.
Bacteria in soil decompose dead matter and recycle nutrients.
Harmful bacteria cause diseases such as strep throat, food poisoning, and tuberculosis.
Pathogens are organisms or viruses that cause disease.
Germs are microorganisms or viruses that cause disease.
Viruses are tiny particles that can only reproduce inside the cells of a living host.
Viruses cause diseases such as the common cold, flu, chicken pox, measles, and COVID 19.
Antibiotics kill bacteria but do not work against viruses.
Penicillin was the first antibiotic and was discovered by Alexander Fleming from a mold.
Vaccines help the body build immunity to a disease by exposing it to a weakened or dead germ.
Vaccines cause the immune system to produce antibodies against a specific disease.
Edward Jenner developed the first vaccine, for smallpox.
Louis Pasteur showed that germs cause disease and developed pasteurization.
Pasteurization heats milk and juice to kill harmful bacteria.
Infectious diseases spread from person to person through air, water, food, contact, or animal bites.
