Noninfectious diseases such as cancer, diabetes, and heart disease are not spread from person to person.
Washing hands, cooking food thoroughly, and refrigerating food reduce the spread of bacteria.
Refrigeration slows the growth of bacteria and keeps food from spoiling.
Bacteria grow quickly in warm, moist places with food.
Food spoils when bacteria and mold grow on it.
Salting, drying, canning, freezing, and refrigerating preserve food by slowing the growth of microorganisms.
Fungi include mushrooms, molds, mildew, and yeast.
Fungi cannot make their own food; they absorb nutrients from living or dead organisms.
Fungi reproduce with spores.
Mold is a fungus that grows on bread, fruit, and damp places.
Yeast produces carbon dioxide gas that makes bread dough rise.
Athlete's foot and ringworm are diseases caused by fungi.
Protists are mostly single celled organisms with a nucleus, such as amoebas, paramecia, and algae.
Malaria is caused by a protist carried by mosquitoes.
Disinfectants and soap kill germs on surfaces and hands.
A fever is the body's way of fighting infection by raising body temperature.
The immune system remembers germs it has fought, so a person may not get the same disease twice.
Diseases can be spread by sneezing and coughing droplets into the air.
Sterilizing tools with heat or chemicals kills microorganisms.
Microscopes allowed scientists to discover bacteria and other microorganisms.
Some bacteria produce oxygen by photosynthesis, such as cyanobacteria.
Bacteria reproduce very quickly by dividing in two.
Overusing antibiotics can lead to antibiotic resistant bacteria.
Wearing gloves while handling samples and washing hands protect people from bacteria in experiments.
Students growing bacteria should not bring food or drinks into the laboratory.
Bacteria samples should be kept in sealed containers such as petri dishes.
A petri dish with agar is used to grow bacteria.
The skeletal system supports the body, protects organs, makes blood cells in the bone marrow, stores calcium, and works with muscles to allow movement.
The skeletal system produces blood cells in the marrow of bones.
The muscular system moves the body by contracting muscles attached to bones.
The circulatory system pumps blood that carries oxygen and nutrients to cells and removes carbon dioxide and wastes.
The respiratory system brings oxygen into the body and removes carbon dioxide.
The digestive system breaks food into small molecules that can be absorbed into the blood.
The nervous system sends electrical signals that control the body and respond to the environment.
The immune system protects the body from disease and fights infections.
The excretory system removes waste products such as urea and excess water from the body.
The endocrine system makes hormones that regulate growth, development, and body processes.
The reproductive system makes it possible to produce offspring.
The integumentary system, the skin, protects the body and helps control temperature.
The heart and lungs work together to supply the body with oxygen.
The brain and nerves work with muscles to move the body.
When a person exercises, the respiratory and circulatory systems work harder to deliver oxygen to the muscles.
Oxygen moves from the lungs into the blood, and carbon dioxide moves from the blood into the lungs.
Nutrients from digested food move from the small intestine into the blood.
The blood carries nutrients from the digestive system to all cells.
The kidneys clean the blood by removing wastes.
Skin is the body's first defense against germs.
Mucus and tiny hairs called cilia in the nose and airways trap dust and germs.
Stomach acid kills many germs in food.
The heart pumps blood through the lungs and then to the rest of the body.
Blood returning to the heart from the body is low in oxygen.
Blood leaving the lungs is rich in oxygen.
Exercise makes the heart and muscles stronger.
Food provides energy and materials for growth and repair.
Plants and animals both carry out cellular respiration to release energy from food.
Plants carry out both photosynthesis and respiration.
Photosynthesis stores energy in sugar, and respiration releases energy from sugar.
Photosynthesis uses carbon dioxide and releases oxygen, while respiration uses oxygen and releases carbon dioxide.
Animal cells get energy from food that the animal eats.
All living things need energy to carry out life processes.
All organisms need water to survive.
All organisms are made of cells.
All organisms reproduce, grow, respond to stimuli, and use energy.
Only plants, algae, and some bacteria can make their own food using sunlight.
Animals cannot make their own food, so they must eat plants or other animals.
Fungi cannot make their own food and are not plants.
Green plants are producers, and animals are consumers.
Cells come from other cells by cell division.
Single celled organisms carry out all life functions within one cell.
In many celled organisms, groups of specialized cells form tissues and organs.
Organs such as the heart, lungs, and stomach are made of several kinds of tissue.
Leaves, stems, and roots are plant organs.
The roots, stems, and leaves of plants work together to move water and food through the plant.
Water enters a plant through the roots, moves up the stem, and exits as vapor through the leaves.
Seeds contain food that the young plant uses until it can make its own food.
A seed grows into a new plant when it has water, air, and the right temperature.
Flowers produce seeds, and fruits protect and help spread the seeds.
Plants reproduce sexually with flowers and seeds, or asexually with runners, bulbs, and cuttings.
Animals reproduce sexually, and some simple animals can reproduce asexually.
Offspring of sexual reproduction get genes from two parents, so they show variation.
Offspring of asexual reproduction get genes from one parent and are identical to it.
Genes are passed from parents to offspring in sex cells.
Each body cell of an organism contains the same genetic information.
A trait such as flower color is controlled by genes inherited from both parents.
Two brown eyed parents can have a blue eyed child if both carry the recessive allele.
A purebred tall pea plant crossed with a purebred short pea plant produces all tall offspring.
Environmental factors like diet and exercise can affect a person's height and weight.
Identical twins raised apart may differ in traits affected by the environment.
Animals must find food, water, shelter, and space in their habitat.
When animals do not find enough food in an area, they may migrate, hibernate, or die.
A population grows when there are plenty of resources and few predators.
A population decreases when food is scarce, disease spreads, or predators increase.
The number of predators affects the number of prey and the number of prey affects the number of predators.
Producers convert sunlight into food energy that is passed to consumers.
Herbivores get energy by eating producers.
Carnivores get energy by eating other consumers.
Energy is lost as heat at each step of a food chain.
Most of the energy an animal eats is used for movement, growth, and keeping warm.
An organism's niche includes what it eats, where it lives, and how it interacts with other organisms.
Two species cannot occupy exactly the same niche for long because they compete.
Organisms that share the same habitat may compete for resources such as food and water.
Some organisms help each other, such as bees pollinating flowers.
Some organisms harm each other, such as parasites harming their hosts.
A tapeworm is a parasite that lives in the intestines of its host.
Mistletoe is a parasitic plant that grows on trees.
A remora fish attaches to a shark and eats its leftover food, which is commensalism.
Oxpecker birds eat ticks off of rhinos, which helps both animals.
Ecosystems change over time through succession.
A pond can slowly fill with sediment and plants and become a meadow and then a forest.
After a forest fire, the first plants to grow back are grasses and wildflowers.
Lichens are often the first organisms to grow on bare rock.
Living things change their environment: trees shade the ground, and earthworms loosen soil.
Humans change environments more than any other species.
Fish in a pond need dissolved oxygen in the water.
Warm water holds less dissolved oxygen than cold water.
Aquatic plants add oxygen to the water during photosynthesis.
A healthy stream has many different kinds of insects, fish, and plants.
Mayfly and stonefly larvae live only in clean water, so they indicate good water quality.
Frogs are sensitive to pollution because they absorb chemicals through their skin.
Bats eat many insects and help control insect populations.
Birds, bats, and insects pollinate plants and spread seeds.
Animals that eat fruit spread seeds in their droppings.
Wolves, mountain lions, and sharks are top predators that keep prey populations in balance.
Vultures and hyenas are scavengers that feed on dead animals.
Dung beetles break down animal waste.
Plants are producers because they make their own food by photosynthesis.
Photosynthesis is the process by which plants use sunlight, water, and carbon dioxide to make sugar and oxygen.
During photosynthesis plants change light energy into chemical energy stored in sugar.
The products of photosynthesis are glucose and oxygen.
The raw materials of photosynthesis are carbon dioxide and water.
Plants need sunlight, water, carbon dioxide, air, and nutrients from soil to grow.
Plants take in carbon dioxide from the air through tiny openings in leaves called stomata.
Stomata let carbon dioxide in and oxygen and water vapor out of leaves.
Guard cells open and close the stomata.
Photosynthesis takes place mainly in the leaves, in chloroplasts.
Chlorophyll is the green pigment in plants that absorbs light for photosynthesis.
Leaves are green because chlorophyll reflects green light.
In fall, chlorophyll breaks down and the yellow and orange pigments in leaves become visible.
Plants release oxygen into the air as a product of photosynthesis.
Animals depend on plants for oxygen and food.
Plants also carry out cellular respiration to release energy from the sugar they make.
Plants store extra food as starch in roots, stems, seeds, and fruits.
Potatoes are underground stems called tubers that store starch.
Carrots, beets, and radishes are roots that store food.
Roots absorb water and minerals from the soil and anchor the plant in the ground.
Root hairs increase the surface area of roots for absorbing water.
Taproots are large main roots, and fibrous roots are many thin branching roots.
The stem supports the plant and carries water and nutrients between the roots and the leaves.
Xylem tubes carry water and minerals up from the roots to the leaves.
Phloem tubes carry sugar made in the leaves to other parts of the plant.
Vascular plants have xylem and phloem tubes to transport water and food.
Nonvascular plants such as mosses and liverworts do not have tubes and grow low in moist places.
Leaves are the main part of the plant where food is made.
Broad flat leaves capture more sunlight.
Transpiration is the loss of water vapor from plant leaves.
The waxy cuticle on leaves reduces water loss.
Flowers are the reproductive parts of flowering plants.
The stamen is the male part of a flower and makes pollen.
The anther is the part of the stamen that produces pollen.
The pistil is the female part of a flower and contains the ovary.
The stigma is the sticky top of the pistil that catches pollen.
The ovary of a flower contains ovules, which become seeds after fertilization.
Petals are often brightly colored and scented to attract pollinators.
Sepals are leaf like parts that protect the flower bud.
Pollination is the transfer of pollen from the anther to the stigma.
Bees, butterflies, moths, birds, and bats are pollinators that carry pollen from flower to flower.
Bees get nectar from flowers and carry pollen to other flowers, helping plants reproduce.
Wind pollinates grasses, corn, and many trees.
Flowers that are pollinated by wind are usually small and not colorful.
After fertilization, the ovary develops into a fruit and the ovules become seeds.
A fruit is the part of a flowering plant that contains the seeds.
Apples, tomatoes, peppers, cucumbers, and pumpkins are fruits because they contain seeds.
A seed contains a plant embryo, stored food, and a protective seed coat.
The seed coat protects the embryo.
The cotyledon is the part of a seed that stores food for the young plant.
Germination is when a seed begins to sprout and grow into a seedling.
Seeds need water, oxygen, and the right temperature to germinate.
Most seeds do not need light to germinate because they use stored food.
Seeds are dispersed by wind, water, animals, and by bursting pods.
Dandelion and maple seeds are dispersed by the wind.
Coconuts are dispersed by water because they float.
Seeds with hooks or burrs stick to animal fur and are carried to new places.
Animals eat fruits and drop the seeds in their wastes in new places.
Squirrels bury acorns, and some of the forgotten acorns grow into oak trees.
Seed dispersal reduces competition between the parent plant and its offspring.
The life cycle of a flowering plant is seed, germination, seedling, adult plant, flower, pollination, fruit, and seed.
Flowering plants are called angiosperms and produce seeds in fruits.
Gymnosperms such as pine trees produce seeds in cones instead of flowers.
Conifers are trees with needles and cones that stay green all year.
Deciduous trees lose their leaves in fall to conserve water during winter.
Evergreen trees keep their needles or leaves all year.
Ferns and mosses reproduce with spores instead of seeds.
Some plants reproduce asexually from runners, bulbs, tubers, or cuttings.
Strawberry plants send out runners that grow into new plants.
Tulips and onions grow from bulbs.
A new potato plant can grow from a piece of potato with an eye.
Plants grown from cuttings are identical to the parent plant.
A tropism is the growth of a plant toward or away from a stimulus.
Phototropism is the growth of a plant toward light.
Plants on a windowsill bend toward the window because of phototropism.
Gravitropism, or geotropism, makes roots grow downward and stems grow upward.
Thigmotropism is a plant's response to touch, such as vines climbing a fence.
Some plants, such as the Venus flytrap, respond quickly to touch.
The Venus flytrap traps insects to get nitrogen because it grows in poor soil.
Plants respond to seasons and day length by flowering or dropping leaves.
Plants that do not get enough light become pale and spindly.
Plants that do not get enough water wilt.
Plants need nitrogen, phosphorus, and potassium from the soil.
Fertilizer adds nutrients to soil to help plants grow.
Too much fertilizer can harm plants and pollute water.
Cacti have thick stems that store water, spines instead of leaves, and a waxy coating to reduce water loss in the desert.
Desert plants have long roots or wide shallow roots to collect water.
Water lilies have wide flat leaves that float to capture sunlight.
Plants in the rain forest grow tall or climb to reach sunlight.
Thorns protect plants such as roses from being eaten.
Some plants produce poisons or bad tastes to protect themselves from animals.
Trees add a new growth ring each year, so counting rings shows the age of a tree.
Bark protects the trunk of a tree.
Algae are plant like protists that make food by photosynthesis and live in water.
Phytoplankton are tiny algae in the ocean that produce much of Earth's oxygen.
Plants are classified as vascular or nonvascular and as seed plants or spore plants.
Grasses have fibrous roots that hold soil and prevent erosion.
Legumes such as beans and peas have bacteria in their roots that add nitrogen to the soil.
Mushrooms are fungi that absorb food from dead matter.
Plants grow from the tips of their roots and stems.
Hydroponics is growing plants in water with nutrients instead of soil.
A greenhouse traps heat and lets plants grow in cold weather.
Selective breeding of plants produces crops with desired traits such as larger fruit.
Farmers plant seeds in spring so plants can grow during the warm summer.
Plant growth can be measured by height, number of leaves, or mass.
Sunflowers turn to follow the Sun.
Corn, wheat, and rice are grasses grown for food.
Cotton plants produce fibers used for cloth.
Trees provide wood, paper, fruit, nuts, shade, and oxygen.
Wood is a natural material from trees that is strong, floats, burns, and is a poor conductor of heat and electricity.
Wood is used for building houses, furniture, paper, and fuel.
Paper is made from wood pulp and is absorbent, flexible, and flammable.
Cardboard is thick paper that is used for boxes.
Cotton is a natural fiber from plants that is soft and absorbs water.
Wool is a natural fiber from sheep that keeps people warm even when wet.
Silk is a natural fiber made by silkworms.
Leather is made from animal skins.
Nylon and polyester are synthetic fibers made from petroleum.
Plastic is a synthetic material made from petroleum that is light, waterproof, durable, and an insulator.
Plastic can be molded into many shapes.
Plastic takes hundreds of years to break down in a landfill.
Rubber is elastic, waterproof, and an electrical insulator.
Natural rubber comes from the sap of rubber trees.
Glass is made by melting sand and is transparent, hard, waterproof, and brittle.
Glass can be recycled many times.
Ceramics such as pottery, tiles, and bricks are made by heating clay.
Clay can be shaped when wet and becomes hard when baked.
Concrete is made from cement, sand, gravel, and water and is very strong under compression.
Steel is a strong alloy of iron used in buildings, bridges, cars, and tools.
Iron is strong and magnetic but rusts easily.
Aluminum is lightweight, does not rust, conducts heat and electricity, and is used for cans, foil, and airplanes.
Copper conducts electricity and heat very well and is used for wires and pipes.
Gold is soft, shiny, and does not tarnish, so it is used for jewelry and electronics.
Silver is the best electrical conductor and is used in jewelry and mirrors.
Tin is used to coat steel cans to prevent rust.
Lead is heavy and dense and was once used in pipes and paint, but it is toxic.
Titanium is strong and light and is used in airplanes and artificial joints.
Stone and brick are strong and long lasting building materials.
Foam is light and full of air, so it is a good insulator and absorbs shock.
Sponges are absorbent because they have many holes.
Felt and fleece are soft materials that trap air and keep warm.
Aluminum foil reflects heat and light.
Wax is waterproof and melts at a low temperature.
Oil is a liquid that does not mix with water and can be used to reduce friction.
Salt dissolves in water and lowers its freezing point.
Sugar dissolves in water and is a source of energy.
Sand does not dissolve in water and can be used to make glass.
Charcoal is made by heating wood with little air and is used as fuel and in filters.
Diamond is the hardest natural material and is used in cutting tools.
Graphite is soft and slippery and is used in pencils.
Materials that are good conductors of heat are used for cooking pots and radiators.
Materials that are good insulators are used for oven mitts, coolers, and coats.
Transparent materials such as glass and clear plastic are used for windows.
Flexible materials such as rubber and cloth bend without breaking.
Rigid materials such as wood, metal, and stone do not bend easily.
Strong materials are needed for bridges and buildings.
Waterproof materials are used for raincoats, umbrellas, and boots.
Absorbent materials are used for towels, diapers, and sponges.
Lightweight materials are used for airplanes and bicycles.
Durable materials last a long time and resist wear.
Natural materials come from plants, animals, or the earth, such as wood, cotton, wool, and stone.
Synthetic materials are made by people, such as plastic, nylon, and polyester.
Renewable materials such as wood and cotton can be grown again.
Recycled materials can be made into new products, saving resources.
Aluminum cans can be recycled into new cans.
Glass bottles can be melted and made into new bottles.
Paper can be recycled into new paper.
Plastic bottles can be recycled into fleece clothing and carpet.
Old tires can be recycled into playground surfaces.
Choosing a material depends on its properties and its intended use.
A bridge builder chooses steel because it is strong.
A cook chooses a metal pan because it conducts heat.
A cook chooses a wooden spoon because it does not get hot.
An electrician wears rubber gloves because rubber is an insulator.
A boat is often made of wood, aluminum, or fiberglass because they float or can be shaped to float.
A window is made of glass so light can pass through.
A shoe sole is made of rubber because it is flexible and grips the ground.
A winter coat is filled with down feathers or synthetic fibers that trap air.
A soda can is made of aluminum because it is light, strong, and does not rust.
Electrical wires are made of copper inside a plastic coating.
A thermos has a vacuum layer because a vacuum stops conduction and convection.
A pencil eraser is made of rubber.
A mirror is made of glass coated with a thin layer of metal.
Brick houses are better insulated than thin wooden houses.
Hard hats are made of strong plastic to protect workers' heads.
Matter is anything that has mass and takes up space.
Mass is the amount of matter in an object.
Volume is the amount of space an object takes up.
Weight is the force of gravity pulling on an object's mass.
Density is the amount of mass in a given volume, calculated as mass divided by volume.
Objects that are less dense than water float, and objects that are more dense than water sink.
The density of water is 1 gram per cubic centimeter.
An object with a density greater than 1 gram per cubic centimeter sinks in water.
Oil floats on water because oil is less dense than water.
Ice floats on water because ice is less dense than liquid water.
Wood floats on water because it is less dense than water.
A rock or a steel nail sinks in water because it is denser than water.
Density does not depend on the size of the sample; a small piece and a large piece of the same material have the same density.
Cutting an object in half does not change its density.
Liquids of different densities form layers, with the densest at the bottom.
Mass is measured with a balance in grams or kilograms.
Volume of a liquid is measured with a graduated cylinder in milliliters or liters.
The volume of an irregular solid can be found by water displacement, measuring how much the water level rises.
The volume of a rectangular object equals length times width times height.
One milliliter equals one cubic centimeter.
Matter is made of tiny particles called atoms and molecules.
The three common states of matter are solid, liquid, and gas.
Plasma is a fourth state of matter found in stars, lightning, and neon signs.
A solid has a definite shape and a definite volume.
A liquid has a definite volume but takes the shape of its container.
A gas has no definite shape or volume and expands to fill its container.
In a solid, particles are packed tightly and vibrate in place.
In a liquid, particles are close together but can slide past each other.
In a gas, particles are far apart and move quickly and freely.
Gases can be compressed because there is a lot of space between their particles.
Particles move faster when they are heated and slower when they are cooled.
Adding heat increases the kinetic energy of particles.
A change of state is a physical change.
Melting is the change from a solid to a liquid.
Freezing is the change from a liquid to a solid.
Evaporation and boiling are changes from a liquid to a gas.
Condensation is the change from a gas to a liquid.
Sublimation is the change from a solid directly to a gas, such as dry ice.
Deposition is the change from a gas directly to a solid, such as frost.
Melting, evaporation, and boiling require adding heat energy.
Freezing and condensation release heat energy.
The melting point of ice is 0 degrees Celsius.
The boiling point of water is 100 degrees Celsius at sea level.
The temperature of water stays the same while it is boiling or while ice is melting.
Water boils at a lower temperature at high altitude because air pressure is lower.
Adding salt to water lowers its freezing point, which is why salt is spread on icy roads.
Each pure substance has its own melting point and boiling point that can be used to identify it.
Water expands when it freezes, so ice takes up more space than the same mass of liquid water.
A bottle of water left in a freezer may crack because water expands when it freezes.
Most substances expand when heated and contract when cooled.
Sidewalks and bridges have gaps so they can expand in hot weather without cracking.
A balloon gets bigger when warmed and smaller when cooled because gas expands and contracts.
Physical properties can be observed or measured without changing the substance, such as color, shape, size, mass, density, texture, hardness, and state.
Chemical properties describe how a substance reacts to form new substances, such as flammability and the ability to rust.
Flammability is the ability of a substance to burn.
Magnetism, conductivity, solubility, boiling point, and melting point are physical properties.
A physical change changes the size, shape, or state of a substance but does not make a new substance.
Cutting, folding, tearing, crushing, bending, melting, freezing, boiling, and dissolving are physical changes.
Breaking glass, chopping wood, and melting ice are physical changes.
Dissolving sugar in water is a physical change because the sugar can be recovered by evaporating the water.
A chemical change produces one or more new substances with different properties.
Burning, rusting, cooking, rotting, and digesting food are chemical changes.
Burning wood, baking a cake, frying an egg, toasting bread, and spoiling milk are chemical changes.
Signs of a chemical change include a gas being produced, a color change, light or heat being given off, a new smell, or a precipitate forming.
Bubbles of gas forming when baking soda and vinegar are mixed show a chemical change.
A precipitate is a solid that forms when two liquids are mixed in a chemical reaction.
Rusting is a chemical change in which iron combines with oxygen and water to form iron oxide.
Iron rusts faster in moist air or salty water.
Painting iron or coating it with oil or zinc prevents rusting.
Tarnish on silver is a chemical change.
Fireworks give off light, heat, and sound in a chemical change.
A mixture is a combination of two or more substances that are not chemically combined.
The parts of a mixture keep their own properties and can be separated by physical means.
Salad, trail mix, sand and water, and soil are mixtures.
A solution is a mixture in which one substance is dissolved evenly in another.
Salt water, sugar water, air, and brass are solutions.
In a solution, the solute is the substance that dissolves and the solvent is the substance that does the dissolving.
Water is called the universal solvent because it dissolves many substances.
Solubility is the amount of a substance that can dissolve in a given amount of solvent.
Sugar and salt are soluble in water.
Sand, oil, and pepper are insoluble in water.
Sugar dissolves faster in hot water than in cold water.
Stirring, heating, and crushing a solid into smaller pieces make it dissolve faster.
Crushing a solid increases its surface area, so it dissolves or reacts faster.
A saturated solution has dissolved as much solute as it can hold.
More solid can dissolve in warm water than in cold water.
Gases such as carbon dioxide dissolve better in cold water than in warm water.
A suspension is a mixture in which particles settle out over time, such as muddy water.
Mixtures can be separated by filtering, evaporation, magnets, sifting, decanting, and distillation.
A filter separates solid particles such as sand from a liquid.
A magnet can separate iron filings from a mixture of iron and sand.
Evaporation separates dissolved salt from water; the salt is left behind.
Distillation separates liquids by boiling and condensing them.
A sieve or screen separates particles of different sizes, such as gravel from sand.
Chromatography separates the colored pigments in ink.
A pure substance is made of only one kind of matter, such as an element or a compound.
An element is a substance made of only one kind of atom that cannot be broken down into simpler substances.
A compound is a substance made of two or more elements chemically combined in a fixed ratio.
Water, carbon dioxide, salt, and sugar are compounds.
Compounds have different properties from the elements that make them.
Sodium is a reactive metal and chlorine is a poisonous gas, but together they form table salt, sodium chloride.
Water is a compound made of hydrogen and oxygen, H2O.
Carbon dioxide, CO2, is a compound of carbon and oxygen.
A molecule is two or more atoms bonded together.
Conservation of mass means matter cannot be created or destroyed in a physical or chemical change.
In a closed container, the total mass before and after a change stays the same.
When ice melts, its mass stays the same.
When sugar dissolves in water, the mass of the solution equals the mass of the sugar plus the water.
If a gas escapes from an open container during a reaction, the measured mass will decrease.
Materials are chosen for their properties, such as strength, flexibility, hardness, and whether they conduct heat or electricity.
Metals are shiny, malleable, ductile, and good conductors of heat and electricity.
Malleable means a material can be hammered into thin sheets.
Ductile means a material can be pulled into thin wires.
Copper is used in electrical wires because it is a good conductor of electricity.
Aluminum is used for cans and foil because it is light, strong, and does not rust.
Plastic, rubber, wood, and glass are electrical insulators.
Glass is transparent, hard, and brittle.
Rubber is flexible and elastic and is used for tires.
Brittle materials break or shatter easily.
Elastic materials return to their original shape after being stretched.
Absorbent materials such as paper towels and sponges soak up liquids.
Waterproof materials such as plastic and rubber do not let water through.
Texture describes how the surface of something feels, such as smooth or rough.
Viscosity is a liquid's resistance to flowing; honey has a high viscosity and water has a low viscosity.
Liquids flow more easily when warmer because their viscosity decreases.
Surface tension lets some insects walk on water.
Air has mass and takes up space.
Gases have mass even though they are often invisible.
A balloon filled with helium rises because helium is less dense than air.
Hot air balloons rise because hot air is less dense than the cooler air around it.
Pressure of a gas increases when it is heated in a closed container.
Water exists naturally on Earth as a solid, liquid, and gas.
Steam is water in the gas state.
Ice cubes melt faster in warm water than in cold water.
Ice melts faster in the Sun than in the shade.
A puddle evaporates faster on a hot day.
Chocolate melts in your hand because body heat adds energy.
Candle wax melting is a physical change, but burning the wick is a chemical change.
Mixing paints of different colors is a physical change.
Making lemonade from lemon juice, sugar, and water is making a mixture.
When a solid dissolves, it seems to disappear but it is still present in the solution.
A new pencil is about 18 centimeters long.
A paper clip has a mass of about 1 gram.
A nickel coin has a mass of about 5 grams.
An apple has a mass of about 150 grams.
A textbook has a mass of about 1 kilogram.
A liter of water has a mass of 1 kilogram.
A teaspoon holds about 5 milliliters.
A soda can holds about 355 milliliters.
A classroom door is about 2 meters tall.
An adult person is about 1.7 meters tall.
A football field is about 100 meters long.
A fingernail is about 1 centimeter wide.
The thickness of a dime is about 1 millimeter.
Walking speed is about 5 kilometers per hour.
Comfortable room temperature is about 21 degrees Celsius.
A hot summer day is about 30 to 35 degrees Celsius.
A cold winter day may be below 0 degrees Celsius.
A kilometer is used to measure distances between cities.
Centimeters are used to measure the length of a pencil or a leaf.
Millimeters are used to measure very small lengths, such as the length of an ant.
Meters are used to measure the length of a room or a hallway.
Grams are used to measure the mass of small objects such as a seed or a coin.
Kilograms are used to measure the mass of a person or a bag of potatoes.
Milliliters are used to measure small volumes of liquid, such as medicine.
Liters are used to measure larger volumes of liquid, such as a bottle of soda.
Degrees Celsius are used to measure temperature.
Seconds, minutes, and hours are units of time.
Newtons are units of force.
Joules are units of energy and work.
Watts are units of power.
Volts are units of electrical potential.
Amperes are units of electric current.
Hertz are units of frequency.
Decibels are units of loudness.
Light years are units of distance in space.
Astronomical units measure distances in the solar system; one AU is the distance from Earth to the Sun.
Grams per cubic centimeter are units of density.
Meters per second are units of speed.
Square meters are units of area, and cubic centimeters are units of volume.
When a seed germinates, the root, called the radicle, grows out first.
The young shoot of a seedling grows upward toward light.
The bubbles in boiling water are bubbles of water vapor.
Yeast changes sugar into carbon dioxide and alcohol by fermentation.
Fermentation is used to make bread, yogurt, and cheese.
Muscles make lactic acid when they do not get enough oxygen.
The bubbles in soda are carbon dioxide gas.
Carbonated drinks lose their fizz faster when warm because gases are less soluble in warm liquids.
When a match is struck, chemical energy is changed into heat and light.
Striking a match uses friction to start a chemical reaction.
Baking soda and vinegar in a sealed bag make the bag puff up because carbon dioxide gas is produced.
Mixing two clear liquids that form a cloudy solid shows that a precipitate formed in a chemical reaction.
Flammability is a chemical property.
Reactivity with acid is a chemical property.
The ability to rust is a chemical property.
Color, odor, density, and melting point are physical properties.
The ocean holds most of Earth's water.
Glaciers and ice caps hold most of Earth's fresh water.
The trachea is part of the respiratory system.
The esophagus is part of the digestive system.
Arteries and veins are part of the circulatory system.
The spinal cord is part of the nervous system.
The kidneys and bladder are part of the excretory system.
Glands are part of the endocrine system.
Ligaments and tendons connect parts of the skeletal and muscular systems.
The lunar cycle goes from new moon to waxing crescent, first quarter, waxing gibbous, full moon, waning gibbous, third quarter, waning crescent, and back to new moon.
The first step of the engineering design process is to identify the problem.
After testing a prototype, engineers analyze the results and improve the design.
Engineers often build and test several designs to find the best one.
A design that meets the criteria within the constraints is a good solution.
Cost, safety, materials, and time are common constraints on a design.
When planning an investigation, the first step is to ask a question.
After making observations, a scientist forms a hypothesis.
The last step in an experiment is to draw a conclusion and communicate the results.
Height and weight of a child increase as the child grows.
Growth happens when cells divide and get larger.
A seedling's growth can be measured by measuring its height every day.
Old cells are replaced by new cells made by mitosis.
Skin cells are constantly replaced.
Hair and fingernails keep growing throughout life.
Most adults stop growing taller in their late teens or early twenties.
Different species have different life spans; some insects live only days and some trees live thousands of years.
A dog has a life span of about 10 to 15 years.
Humans have a life span of about 70 to 80 years.
Bristlecone pine trees can live more than 4,000 years.
A mayfly adult lives only about one day.
Gestation is the time a baby mammal develops inside its mother.
An elephant's gestation is about 22 months, the longest of any land animal.
Chickens hatch from eggs after about 21 days.
Frogs lay eggs in water in the spring.
Many birds lay eggs in spring so their young hatch when food is plentiful.
Many trees flower in spring.
Many plants make seeds in summer and fall.
Leaves change color and fall in autumn.
In winter, many plants are dormant and many animals hibernate or migrate.
The kinetic molecular theory states that all matter is made of particles in constant motion.
Temperature increases when the average kinetic energy of particles increases.
At absolute zero, particles have the least possible motion.
Gas pressure is caused by particles colliding with the walls of their container.
Increasing the temperature of a gas in a closed container increases its pressure.
Decreasing the volume of a gas increases its pressure if the temperature stays the same.
A balloon shrinks in the cold because its gas particles move slower and hit the walls less.
Diffusion happens faster at higher temperatures because particles move faster.
Thermal expansion happens because particles move farther apart when heated.
During a phase change, energy goes into breaking or forming attractions between particles, so temperature stays constant.
The energy needed to melt ice comes from its surroundings.
Elements are arranged in the periodic table by atomic number.
Elements in the same group react in similar ways.
Reactivity of metals increases going down the first group of the periodic table.
Atoms bond to become more stable by filling their outer electron shells.
Metals tend to lose electrons and nonmetals tend to gain electrons.
Ionic compounds such as salt have high melting points and conduct electricity when dissolved.
Covalent compounds such as sugar and water are made of molecules.
Chemical equations show reactants on the left and products on the right of an arrow.
In the equation for photosynthesis, carbon dioxide plus water plus light energy produces glucose plus oxygen.
In the equation for respiration, glucose plus oxygen produces carbon dioxide plus water plus energy.
In a balanced equation, the number of atoms of each element is the same on both sides.
Counting atoms before and after a reaction shows that matter is conserved.
A reaction in an open container may appear to lose mass because a gas escapes.
A reaction in a sealed container keeps the same total mass.
Exothermic reactions feel warm because they release energy.
Endothermic reactions feel cold because they absorb energy.
Instant cold packs use an endothermic reaction.
Hand warmers use an exothermic reaction such as iron rusting.
Activation energy is the energy needed to start a reaction.
A match needs friction to supply activation energy.
Enzymes in the body lower the activation energy of reactions.
The rate of a reaction increases with temperature, concentration, surface area, and catalysts.
Cells are organized into tissues, tissues into organs, and organs into organ systems.
Cellular respiration takes place in the mitochondria.
Photosynthesis takes place in the chloroplasts.
The energy released by respiration is stored in a molecule called ATP.
Plants release carbon dioxide at night when photosynthesis stops but respiration continues.
Unicellular organisms take in nutrients and get rid of wastes through their cell membrane.
Multicellular organisms need transport systems such as blood or xylem to move materials.
The surface area of the lungs is very large because of millions of alveoli.
Root hairs and intestinal villi both increase surface area for absorption.
Energy flows in one direction through an ecosystem, but matter cycles.
Each feeding level in a food chain is called a trophic level.
Primary producers are at the first trophic level.
A food web with many species is more stable than a simple food chain.
Biomagnification is the buildup of toxins at higher levels of a food chain.
Top predators such as eagles and orcas have the highest levels of toxins like mercury and DDT.
Population growth curves level off when the population reaches carrying capacity.
Exponential growth happens when resources are unlimited.
Density dependent factors such as food, disease, and competition have greater effects on crowded populations.
Density independent factors such as weather and natural disasters affect populations regardless of size.
Speciation happens when populations become isolated and change so much they can no longer interbreed.
Geographic isolation, such as a river or mountain range, can separate populations and lead to new species.
Adaptive radiation happens when one species gives rise to many species adapted to different niches.
The Galapagos finches are an example of adaptive radiation.
Fossils, DNA, and similar body structures are evidence for evolution.
Vestigial structures, such as the human appendix and whale hip bones, are remnants of structures in ancestors.
Analogous structures such as bird wings and insect wings have similar functions but different origins.
Earth's interior layers were identified using seismic waves.
The lithosphere floats on the denser asthenosphere.
Isostasy is the balance of Earth's crust floating on the mantle, like ice floating on water.
Mountains have deep roots of crust that extend into the mantle.
Continental crust rises when glaciers melt and their weight is removed.
The theory of plate tectonics combines continental drift and sea floor spreading.
Earthquakes produce seismic waves that travel through Earth and are recorded by seismographs.
The intensity of an earthquake describes the shaking and damage at a location.
The magnitude of an earthquake describes the total energy released.
Stars produce energy by fusing hydrogen into helium in their cores.
The color of a star tells its surface temperature; the brightness depends on size, temperature, and distance.
The Sun's core temperature is about 15 million degrees Celsius.
The Sun's surface, the photosphere, is about 5,500 degrees Celsius.
The corona is the Sun's outer atmosphere, visible during a total solar eclipse.
Sunspots appear dark because they are cooler than the surrounding surface.
The number of sunspots changes in an 11 year cycle.
Our solar system is about 4.6 billion years old.
The inner planets are rocky because only rock and metal could condense near the hot young Sun.
The outer planets are gas giants because ices and gases could condense far from the Sun.
The Kuiper belt beyond Neptune contains icy bodies such as Pluto.
The Oort cloud far beyond the planets is the source of many comets.
Gravity depends on mass and distance; it decreases with the square of the distance.
Escape velocity is the speed needed to leave a planet's gravity.
Electromagnetic waves are produced by vibrating electric charges.
All electromagnetic waves travel at 300,000 kilometers per second in space.
The electromagnetic spectrum in order of increasing frequency is radio, microwave, infrared, visible, ultraviolet, X ray, and gamma ray.
Higher frequency electromagnetic waves carry more energy and can be more harmful.
Ultraviolet rays, X rays, and gamma rays can damage living cells.
Lead aprons protect patients from X rays.
Radio waves are used in radios, television, cell phones, and radar.
Infrared cameras detect heat given off by warm objects.
Night vision goggles detect infrared radiation.
Waves transfer energy without transferring matter.
Mechanical waves include sound waves, water waves, and seismic waves.
The speed of sound depends on the medium and its temperature.
Light refracts when it changes speed moving from one medium to another.
White light refracts into colors because each color bends a different amount.
The law of reflection says the angle of incidence equals the angle of reflection.
Total internal reflection keeps light inside fiber optic cables.
Deer are herbivores that eat grass, leaves, and twigs and are hunted by wolves and mountain lions.
Rabbits are herbivores that eat grass and are hunted by foxes, hawks, owls, and snakes.
Mice eat seeds and grains and are eaten by owls, snakes, hawks, and cats.
Squirrels eat nuts and seeds and store them for winter.
Foxes are omnivores that eat rabbits, mice, insects, and berries.
Wolves hunt in packs and eat deer, elk, and moose.
Bears eat berries, fish, insects, and small mammals, and many hibernate in winter.
Grizzly bears catch salmon swimming upstream.
Raccoons are nocturnal omnivores that eat almost anything.
Owls are nocturnal birds of prey that eat mice and other small animals.
Hawks and eagles are birds of prey with sharp eyesight, hooked beaks, and talons.
Bald eagles eat fish and are the national bird of the United States.
Robins eat earthworms and insects.
Hummingbirds drink nectar and can hover in the air.
Frogs eat insects such as flies and mosquitoes.
Snakes swallow their prey whole.
Spiders catch insects in webs.
Bees make honey from nectar and live in hives.
Ants live in colonies with a queen, workers, and soldiers.
Termites eat wood and can damage houses.
Mosquitoes lay their eggs in standing water.
Grasshoppers eat grass and leaves and are eaten by birds and frogs.
Butterflies drink nectar and pollinate flowers.
Caterpillars eat leaves.
Earthworms eat soil and decaying matter and improve soil.
Snails and slugs eat plants and leave a trail of slime.
Crayfish live in streams and are scavengers.
Salmon hatch in rivers, live in the ocean, and return to rivers to spawn.
Sharks are predators in the ocean and have rows of sharp teeth.
Whales are the largest mammals and some eat tiny krill.
Dolphins are intelligent mammals that live in groups called pods.
Sea otters eat sea urchins and help protect kelp forests.
Penguins live in the Southern Hemisphere and use their wings to swim.
Polar bears live in the Arctic and hunt seals on sea ice.
Seals and walruses have blubber to keep warm.
Camels live in deserts and have humps that store fat.
Lizards and snakes in deserts hide under rocks or in burrows during the hottest part of the day.
Roadrunners and kangaroo rats live in deserts.
Kangaroo rats get water from the seeds they eat.
Jackrabbits have long ears that help them lose heat.
Rattlesnakes sense the body heat of their prey.
Scorpions live in deserts and have a stinger on their tail.
Cacti store water and have spines.
Saguaro cactus can expand to hold water after rain.
Monkeys live in trees in tropical rain forests and have tails to grip branches.
Toucans have large colorful beaks and live in rain forests.
Sloths move slowly and hang upside down in rain forest trees.
Jaguars are predators in rain forests.
Orchids and ferns grow on trees in rain forests to reach light.
Lions live in grasslands called savannas and hunt zebras and antelope.
Zebras and gazelles are herbivores that graze on grass.
Elephants are the largest land animals and are herbivores.
Giraffes are the tallest animals and eat leaves from acacia trees.
Bison once roamed the prairies of North America in huge herds.
Prairie dogs live in underground burrows in grasslands.
Moose live in northern forests and eat plants in lakes and ponds.
Caribou migrate across the tundra.
Arctic foxes have small ears and thick white fur in winter.
Snowy owls live in the tundra.
Lemmings are small rodents of the tundra.
Musk oxen have long thick hair to survive tundra winters.
Pine trees have needles and cones and grow in cold climates.
Oak trees produce acorns and lose their leaves in fall.
Maple trees produce sap that can be made into syrup.
Redwood and sequoia trees are among the tallest and oldest trees.
Mosses grow in damp shady places and have no roots.
Ferns have fronds and reproduce with spores.
Cattails and reeds grow in wetlands.
Water lilies float on ponds.
Duckweed is a tiny floating plant.
Algae can be single celled or as large as seaweed.
Kelp is a large brown alga that grows in cold ocean water.
Lichens grow on rocks and trees and are made of a fungus and an alga.
Mushrooms grow on dead logs and in soil.
Bread mold is a fungus that grows from spores.
Yeast is used in baking and makes carbon dioxide gas.
Dandelions have seeds with fluffy parachutes carried by wind.
Maple seeds have wings that spin as they fall.
Coconuts float to new islands.
Burdock burrs stick to animal fur.
Berries are eaten by birds, which spread the seeds.
Sunflowers produce many seeds that birds eat.
Corn is a grass that is pollinated by wind.
Apple trees need bees to pollinate their flowers.
Venus flytraps catch insects with leaves that snap shut.
Pitcher plants trap insects in a pool of liquid to get nutrients.
Poison ivy causes an itchy rash.
Tulips grow from bulbs.
Potatoes grow from tubers.
Strawberries spread by runners.
Bamboo is a fast growing grass.
Sea stars eat clams and mussels.
Jellyfish use stinging cells to catch prey.
Coral polyps build coral reefs.
Clams and oysters filter food from water.
Octopuses have eight arms and can change color.
Squid have ten arms and squirt ink.
Crabs have hard exoskeletons and pincers.
Lobsters are crustaceans that live on the ocean floor.
Barnacles attach to rocks, ships, and whales.
Plankton float near the ocean surface and are the base of ocean food chains.
Krill are small crustaceans that are eaten by whales.
Bats eat insects or fruit and are active at night.
Moles live underground and eat earthworms and insects.
Beavers build dams and lodges in streams.
Turtles lay eggs on land, even sea turtles.
Alligators live in swamps in the southeastern United States.
Crocodiles and alligators are reptiles with strong jaws.
Chickens are birds raised for eggs and meat.
Cows are mammals raised for milk and meat and they eat grass.
Sheep provide wool.
Horses were used for transportation and farm work.
Dogs were domesticated from wolves.
Cats are carnivores that hunt mice.
Sound travels fastest through solids such as steel, iron, wood, and rock.
Sound travels faster through water than through air, and slowest through gases such as air.
Sound cannot travel through a vacuum because there are no particles to vibrate.
Sound travels through the air as a wave of compressions.
Light travels fastest through a vacuum and slows down in air, water, and glass.
Light can travel through space, but sound cannot.
The particles in a solid are closest together, so vibrations pass most quickly through solids.
Particles in a gas are farthest apart and move the fastest.
Particles in a solid have the least energy of motion and particles in a gas have the most.
A gas spreads out to fill any container it is in.
A liquid poured into a different container keeps the same volume but changes shape.
A solid keeps its shape when moved to a different container.
Gases are the easiest state of matter to compress.
Heating a solid enough makes it melt into a liquid, and heating a liquid enough makes it boil into a gas.
Removing heat from a gas can make it condense into a liquid, and removing more heat can make the liquid freeze.
The temperature at which a substance melts is the same as the temperature at which it freezes.
Water can exist as solid ice, liquid water, and gaseous water vapor.
Ice, water, and steam are the same substance, water, in different states.
Water is the only common substance found naturally on Earth in all three states.
Mercury is the only metal that is liquid at room temperature.
Oxygen and nitrogen are gases at room temperature.
Iron melts at a very high temperature, about 1,500 degrees Celsius.
Butter, chocolate, and wax melt at low temperatures.
The mass of an object stays the same when its shape or state changes.
Breaking a cookie into pieces does not change its total mass.
When water evaporates, the water still exists as water vapor in the air.
When paper burns, its mass seems to decrease because gases escape into the air.
A closed container of ice that melts has the same mass before and after melting.
Weight changes depending on gravity, but mass stays the same everywhere.
A bowling ball has more mass and more inertia than a soccer ball.
A larger force is needed to stop a heavy moving object than a light one.
A car that speeds up, slows down, or turns is accelerating.
A ball rolled on a level surface slows down because friction and air resistance act against its motion.
An object moving at a constant speed in a straight line has balanced forces acting on it.
A book resting on a table has balanced forces: gravity pulls down and the table pushes up.
In a tug of war, the team that pulls with greater force wins because the forces are unbalanced.
If two people push a box from opposite sides with equal force, the box does not move.
The greater the mass of an object, the greater the force of gravity on it.
The Sun's gravity is much stronger than Earth's because the Sun has much more mass.
Jupiter has the strongest gravity of the planets because it has the most mass.
The farther apart two objects are, the weaker the gravitational force between them.
The more mass an object has, the more force is needed to change its motion.
A magnet's force gets weaker as distance from the magnet increases.
A stronger magnet can pick up more paper clips.
Magnets can attract objects through paper, cloth, water, and plastic.
Opposite charges attract and like charges repel, just as opposite magnetic poles attract and like poles repel.
A wire wrapped around an iron nail and connected to a battery becomes an electromagnet.
An electromagnet becomes stronger with more turns of wire, more batteries, or more current.
An electromagnet loses its magnetism when the electric current is switched off.
Electric current flows only when a circuit is closed.
A switch in the off position opens the circuit and stops the current.
Adding batteries to a circuit increases the voltage and makes a bulb glow brighter.
Bulbs in a parallel circuit stay bright when more bulbs are added.
Bulbs in a series circuit get dimmer when more bulbs are added.
Copper wire is a better conductor than iron wire.
Silver is the best conductor of electricity, but copper is used in wires because it is cheaper.
Rubber gloves protect electricians because rubber is an insulator.
Electrical cords are covered in plastic because plastic is an electrical insulator.
Metals are good conductors of both heat and electricity.
Air is a poor conductor of heat, which makes it a good insulator.
Styrofoam is a good insulator because it contains trapped air.
Feathers and fur trap air that insulates animals from the cold.
A metal pan heats faster than a glass pan because metal conducts heat better.
On a sunny day, a black car gets hotter than a white car.
A white roof stays cooler than a black roof because white reflects more sunlight.
Dark soil warms up faster in the spring than light colored soil.
Land heats faster in sunlight than water does.
Sand at the beach is hotter than the water in the afternoon, but cooler than the water at night.
Thermal energy moves from the hot cocoa to the cooler cup and the air around it.
A cup of hot cocoa cools down because heat moves from the cocoa to the cooler air.
A cold drink warms up because heat moves from the warmer air into the drink.
Heat moves from a hot object to a cold object, never from cold to hot on its own.
When you hold an ice cube, heat moves from your hand to the ice, so your hand feels cold.
When two objects reach the same temperature, heat stops flowing between them.
Heat transfer by conduction is fastest in metals.
Convection only happens in liquids and gases because their particles can move from place to place.
Radiation does not need matter to transfer energy.
The Sun's energy travels to Earth through space by radiation.
The light energy from the Sun is changed into heat energy when it is absorbed by the ground.
Energy changes form, but the total amount of energy stays the same.
A ball bounces lower each time because some energy is changed into heat and sound.
A bouncing ball never bounces higher than the height from which it was dropped.
A falling object gains kinetic energy and loses potential energy.
The higher an object is lifted, the more potential energy it has.
A heavier object at the same height has more potential energy than a lighter object.
A faster moving object has more kinetic energy than the same object moving slowly.
A truck moving at the same speed as a car has more kinetic energy because it has more mass.
A stretched spring, a raised weight, a charged battery, and food all store energy.
Electrical energy can be changed into light, heat, sound, and motion.
A hair dryer changes electrical energy into heat and motion.
A blender changes electrical energy into motion.
A television changes electrical energy into light and sound.
A cell phone battery changes chemical energy into electrical energy.
A gas stove changes chemical energy in natural gas into heat and light.
A campfire changes chemical energy in wood into heat and light.
A solar panel changes light energy from the Sun into electrical energy.
A windmill changes wind energy into mechanical energy.
A water wheel changes the energy of moving water into mechanical energy.
The human body changes chemical energy from food into motion and heat.
Green plants change light energy into chemical energy.
In a car engine, chemical energy in fuel changes into thermal energy and then into mechanical energy.
A guitar changes the mechanical energy of a plucked string into sound energy.
Lightning changes electrical energy into light, heat, and sound.
A glow stick changes chemical energy into light energy.
Fireflies change chemical energy into light energy.
A wave with a higher frequency has a higher pitch for sound or a bluer color for light.
Waves with longer wavelengths have lower frequencies.
Radio waves have longer wavelengths than visible light.
Ultraviolet waves have more energy than visible light.
X rays have more energy than ultraviolet light.
A louder sound has a greater amplitude.
A higher pitched sound has a higher frequency.
Sound waves are compression waves, and light waves are transverse waves.
Water waves move energy across the ocean, but the water itself mostly moves up and down.
A floating cork bobs up and down as a wave passes but does not move forward with the wave.
The energy of an ocean wave comes from wind.
Light reflects off a mirror at the same angle it hits it.
Concave mirrors, like the inside of a spoon, can focus light.
