A natural resource is a material from nature that people use, such as water, air, soil, minerals, trees, and fuels.
Renewable resources can be replaced naturally in a short time.
Renewable energy sources include solar, wind, hydroelectric, geothermal, tidal, and biomass energy.
Sunlight, wind, and moving water are renewable energy resources.
Trees, crops, and fish are renewable resources if they are managed carefully.
Nonrenewable resources cannot be replaced in a human lifetime once they are used up.
Coal, oil, petroleum, natural gas, and gasoline are nonrenewable energy resources.
Fossil fuels are nonrenewable because they take millions of years to form.
Fossil fuels formed from the remains of plants and animals that lived millions of years ago.
The energy in fossil fuels originally came from the Sun and was stored by ancient plants through photosynthesis.
Uranium used in nuclear power plants is a nonrenewable resource.
Minerals and metals are nonrenewable resources.
Gasoline is made from petroleum, or crude oil.
Plastics are made from petroleum.
Burning fossil fuels releases carbon dioxide, sulfur dioxide, nitrogen oxides, and particles into the air.
Burning fossil fuels causes air pollution, acid rain, and global warming.
Acid rain forms when sulfur dioxide and nitrogen oxides from burning fuels mix with water in the air.
Acid rain harms lakes, fish, trees, and buildings and statues made of limestone and marble.
Solar energy is energy from the Sun.
Solar panels, or solar cells, change sunlight directly into electrical energy.
Solar energy does not produce air pollution, but it only works when the Sun is shining.
Wind turbines change the kinetic energy of wind into electrical energy.
Wind energy does not pollute but only works when the wind blows.
Hydroelectric power plants use the energy of falling or flowing water in dams to generate electricity.
Dams provide hydroelectric power but can block fish migration and flood habitats.
Geothermal energy uses heat from inside Earth to make electricity or heat buildings.
Tidal energy uses the rise and fall of ocean tides to generate electricity.
Biomass energy comes from burning plant material, wood, or animal waste, or from biofuels like ethanol.
Ethanol is a biofuel made from corn or sugarcane.
Nuclear power plants use fission of uranium atoms to produce heat that makes electricity.
Nuclear power plants produce radioactive waste that stays dangerous for thousands of years.
Nuclear power plants produce almost no air pollution.
Most electricity in power plants is made by spinning turbines connected to generators.
In a coal power plant, burning coal heats water into steam that turns a turbine.
Conservation is the careful use of natural resources so they last longer.
People can conserve energy by turning off lights, using energy efficient appliances, and walking or riding bikes.
People can conserve water by taking shorter showers, fixing leaks, and turning off the faucet.
Carpooling and using public transportation save fuel and reduce air pollution.
The three Rs of conservation are reduce, reuse, and recycle.
Reducing means using less of a resource.
Reusing means using an item again instead of throwing it away.
Recycling means processing used materials such as paper, glass, aluminum, and plastic into new products.
Recycling aluminum cans saves energy and resources and reduces waste in landfills.
Recycling paper saves trees.
Composting food and yard waste reduces garbage and makes fertilizer for soil.
Landfills are places where garbage is buried.
Biodegradable materials can be broken down by decomposers such as bacteria and fungi.
Plastics are not biodegradable and can last hundreds of years in the environment.
Pollution is the addition of harmful substances to the environment.
Air pollution comes from burning fuels in cars, factories, and power plants.
Water pollution comes from sewage, factory waste, oil spills, and runoff of fertilizers and pesticides.
Fertilizer runoff into lakes causes algae to grow quickly, which uses up oxygen and kills fish.
Pesticides can harm organisms other than pests and can build up in food chains.
DDT was a pesticide that built up in food chains and caused bald eagles and other birds to lay eggs with thin shells.
Oil spills coat birds and sea animals and harm ocean ecosystems.
Litter and plastic in the ocean can harm sea turtles, birds, and fish that eat it or get tangled.
Thermal pollution from power plants warms rivers and reduces dissolved oxygen for fish.
Noise pollution and light pollution can disturb people and animals.
Groundwater can be polluted by leaking landfills, septic tanks, and chemicals soaking into the ground.
Water treatment plants filter and disinfect water to make it safe to drink.
Clean water is a limited resource that must be protected.
Deforestation is the cutting down of forests.
Deforestation destroys habitats, increases erosion, and adds carbon dioxide to the atmosphere.
Habitat destruction is the main reason many species are endangered.
Building cities, roads, and farms destroys natural habitats.
Urban sprawl replaces fields and forests with buildings and pavement.
Overfishing reduces fish populations faster than they can reproduce.
Overgrazing by cattle removes plants and can turn grasslands into deserts.
Invasive species are organisms brought to a new area where they spread and harm native species.
Invasive species often have no natural predators in their new environment.
Zebra mussels, kudzu, and cane toads are invasive species.
Protecting habitats, creating wildlife refuges, and passing laws help protect endangered species.
National parks and wildlife preserves protect natural habitats.
Replanting trees after logging, called reforestation, helps restore forests.
Wetlands should be protected because they filter pollutants and provide habitat.
Scrubbers and filters on smokestacks reduce air pollution from factories.
Catalytic converters on cars reduce harmful exhaust gases.
Electric cars and hybrid cars use less gasoline and produce less pollution.
Energy efficient light bulbs such as LEDs use less electricity than incandescent bulbs.
Insulating a house reduces the energy needed for heating and cooling.
Humans affect the environment through farming, building, mining, pollution, and use of resources.
Human population growth increases the demand for food, water, energy, and land.
Mining can damage land and pollute water.
Strip mining removes the top layers of soil and rock to reach coal.
Reclamation is restoring land after mining by replacing soil and planting vegetation.
Sustainable practices meet present needs without harming the ability of future generations to meet their needs.
Wildfires can be started by lightning or by people and destroy forests.
After a forest fire, grasses and small plants grow first, followed by shrubs and trees.
Natural disasters such as floods, hurricanes, earthquakes, and volcanoes affect people and ecosystems.
People can reduce damage from natural hazards by building levees, using warning systems, and planning evacuations.
Levees are walls built along rivers to prevent flooding.
Air quality is lowered by smoke, dust, pollen, and exhaust.
People with asthma are affected by air pollution.
Lead, mercury, and other heavy metals are toxic pollutants.
Ozone near the ground is a pollutant in smog, but ozone high in the stratosphere protects Earth.
Farmers use fertilizers to add nutrients such as nitrogen, phosphorus, and potassium to soil.
Irrigation brings water to crops from rivers, lakes, or wells.
Drip irrigation conserves water by delivering it directly to plant roots.
Organic farming does not use synthetic pesticides or fertilizers.
Integrated pest management uses natural predators such as ladybugs to control pests.
Ladybugs eat aphids and help gardeners control pests.
Desalination removes salt from ocean water to make fresh water.
Precipitation returns water from the atmosphere to Earth's surface as rain, snow, sleet, or hail.
Evaporation moves water from Earth's surface into the atmosphere as water vapor.
Condensation of water vapor in the atmosphere forms clouds.
Rain falling on the ground can soak in, run off into streams, or evaporate.
Water that soaks into the ground becomes groundwater and can supply wells and springs.
Rivers carry water from land back to the ocean.
The water we drink today has been recycled through the water cycle for billions of years.
On a hot day, water evaporates from a lake faster than on a cold day.
A cloud forms when warm moist air rises, expands, and cools.
Rain falls when water droplets in a cloud join together and become too heavy to stay in the air.
Dark clouds and falling air pressure are signs that a storm is coming.
A sudden drop in temperature and strong winds often come with a cold front.
In winter, lakes and ponds freeze on the surface first.
The coldest temperatures of the day usually happen just before sunrise.
The warmest temperatures of the day usually happen in the afternoon.
The air near the ground is warmer than the air on top of a mountain.
Snow stays on mountain tops longer because temperature drops with altitude.
Coastal cities have smaller temperature changes between day and night than inland cities.
A sea breeze cools beaches on summer afternoons.
Hurricanes lose strength when they move over land because they lose their source of warm water.
Hurricane season in the Atlantic is from June to November when ocean water is warmest.
Tornadoes form from severe thunderstorms when warm moist air meets cool dry air.
A weather forecaster uses a barometer reading that is falling to predict rain.
Weather satellites help forecasters track hurricanes before they reach land.
Scientists use weather maps to predict where fronts and storms will move.
A thermometer outside a window shows the temperature changes during the day.
A rain gauge collected 2 centimeters of rain during a storm.
The amount of daylight changes with the seasons because of Earth's tilt.
In New York State, days are longest in June and shortest in December.
In the Northern Hemisphere, summer begins in June and winter begins in December.
In the Southern Hemisphere, such as Australia, it is summer in December.
At noon in the summer the Sun is high in the sky and shadows are short.
In winter the Sun's rays are spread out over a larger area, so the ground heats less.
At the equator, temperatures stay warm all year and there are no big seasonal changes.
Near the poles the Sun stays above the horizon all day in summer and below it in winter.
A shadow of a flagpole is shortest at noon and longest in the early morning and late evening.
The Sun appears to move across the sky during the day because Earth rotates.
If you watch the stars for several hours they appear to move in circles around the North Star.
The Moon rises about 50 minutes later each day.
The Moon looks different each night because we see different amounts of its sunlit half.
It takes about 29 days to go from one full moon to the next.
A crescent moon is seen when only a thin sliver of the lit side faces Earth.
When the Moon is full, the whole sunlit side faces Earth.
When the Moon is new, it cannot be seen because its lit side faces away from Earth.
A solar eclipse is rare at any one place because the Moon's shadow on Earth is small.
During a total solar eclipse the sky becomes dark during the day.
During a lunar eclipse the Moon may look red or copper colored.
Looking directly at the Sun can damage your eyes, even during an eclipse.
Earth's atmosphere protects it from most meteoroids, which burn up as meteors.
The Moon has more craters than Earth because Earth's atmosphere burns up meteoroids and weathering erases craters on Earth.
An astronaut would weigh less on the Moon but have the same mass.
A person who weighs 600 newtons on Earth would weigh about 100 newtons on the Moon.
On the Moon the sky is black even in the daytime because there is no atmosphere to scatter light.
There is no sound on the Moon because there is no air to carry sound waves.
Water on Earth's surface exists as liquid because Earth is at the right distance from the Sun.
A planet that is closer to the Sun than Earth would be hotter.
Venus is hotter than Mercury even though Mercury is closer to the Sun, because of Venus's thick atmosphere.
Telescopes on Earth are often placed on high mountains where the air is thin and clear.
A student who sees the Big Dipper in a different position at different times of night is observing Earth's rotation.
A star that looks very bright may be close to Earth or may be very large and bright.
The Sun looks bigger than other stars because it is much closer.
Light from the Sun takes about eight minutes to reach Earth, but light from other stars takes years.
A river carries more sediment during a flood because the water moves faster.
After a heavy rain, rivers look muddy because they carry eroded soil.
A river's water slows down when it enters a lake, so sediment settles to the bottom.
The fastest moving water in a river is on the outside of a curve, where erosion happens.
Sand and pebbles at the bottom of a hill were moved there by water and gravity.
Rocks at the bottom of a cliff broke off by weathering and fell because of gravity.
Gravestones made of marble become hard to read over time because acid rain weathers them.
Old statues made of limestone wear away faster in cities with acid rain.
Planting trees and grass on a bare hillside reduces erosion.
Building a wall of rocks along a beach can protect it from erosion by waves.
A farmer can reduce soil erosion by planting cover crops, plowing along contour lines, and building terraces.
Plowing up and down a hill increases erosion because water runs straight down the furrows.
Cutting down a large forest increases soil erosion because no roots hold the soil.
Cutting down forests reduces habitats for animals and increases carbon dioxide in the air.
After a volcanic eruption, the land is covered with lava and ash, and new soil forms slowly.
A deep canyon was carved by a river over millions of years.
A fossil of a seashell found on a mountain top shows that the rock formed under the sea and was later pushed up.
A layer of volcanic ash in rock layers can be used to match layers from different places.
The bottom layer in a cliff wall is usually the oldest.
A rock containing large crystals formed from magma that cooled slowly deep underground.
A rock with holes like a sponge formed from lava with gas bubbles.
A rock with layers and rounded pebbles formed from sediment deposited by water.
A banded rock formed under heat and pressure is metamorphic.
Soil in a forest has more humus than soil in a desert.
Desert soil is sandy and holds little water.
Earthquakes are common in California because it lies along the San Andreas Fault, a plate boundary.
Japan has many earthquakes and volcanoes because it lies where plates meet.
Iceland has volcanoes because it sits on the Mid Atlantic Ridge, where plates spread apart.
Mount St. Helens erupted in Washington State in 1980.
Most of the world's active volcanoes are around the Pacific Ocean in the Ring of Fire.
Hawaii's volcanoes formed over a hot spot.
Scientists use seismographs to measure the strength of earthquakes.
People in earthquake zones build flexible buildings that sway without breaking.
Coal mined today formed from swamp plants that lived about 300 million years ago.
Using public transportation and riding bicycles reduces air pollution.
Using reusable bags instead of plastic bags reduces waste.
Turning off lights when leaving a room saves energy.
Recycling newspapers saves trees and reduces waste in landfills.
Taking shorter showers and fixing leaky faucets conserves water.
Running the dishwasher only when it is full saves water and energy.
Watering lawns in the early morning reduces water lost to evaporation.
Solar panels on a roof can provide electricity for a house.
Wind farms are built in places where strong winds blow often.
A town near a fast flowing river might build a hydroelectric dam.
Geothermal power plants are built in places like Iceland where hot rock is near the surface.
Burning coal in power plants releases gases that cause acid rain.
Cars burn gasoline and release carbon dioxide, a greenhouse gas.
Using wind turbines or solar panels instead of burning coal reduces carbon dioxide released into the atmosphere.
Planting trees and using less fossil fuel reduce the amount of carbon dioxide in the atmosphere.
People can reduce greenhouse gases by driving less, carpooling, and using energy efficient appliances.
Throwing trash on the ground pollutes the land and water.
Chemicals poured down a drain can pollute rivers and groundwater.
Fertilizer washing off farms into rivers can cause algae to grow and fish to die.
A factory releasing hot water into a river can harm fish by lowering the oxygen in the water.
A plant kept in a dark closet turns yellow and stops growing because it cannot make food without light.
A plant that is not watered wilts and may die.
Plants placed near a sunny window grow toward the light.
Seeds planted in moist soil in spring germinate when the temperature warms.
A bean seed needs water and warmth to sprout.
Plants given fertilizer usually grow taller and greener than plants without fertilizer.
Overwatering a plant can rot its roots.
Plants grown in sandy soil may need more water because sand does not hold water.
Plants in a sealed jar can survive because they recycle water and gases.
Bees visiting flowers carry pollen on their bodies from one flower to another.
If bees disappeared, many fruit crops would not be pollinated and would produce less fruit.
Fruit trees need pollinators such as bees to make fruit.
A caterpillar eats leaves and grows before it forms a chrysalis and becomes a butterfly.
A tadpole grows legs and loses its tail as it changes into a frog.
Baby birds hatch from eggs that the parent birds keep warm in a nest.
A puppy grows into an adult dog, and a kitten grows into an adult cat.
Young animals often resemble their parents because they inherit traits from them.
Kittens from the same litter can have different fur colors because they inherit different combinations of genes.
A dog's ability to learn a trick is a learned behavior, but its tail wagging is instinctive.
A bird building a nest without being taught is an instinct.
A chimpanzee using a stick to get termites learned the behavior by watching others.
Geese fly south for the winter, which is migration, a behavioral adaptation.
Birds flying south in the fall to find food is a behavioral adaptation called migration.
A bear sleeping through the winter is hibernation, a behavioral adaptation.
A squirrel gathering and burying nuts in fall is a behavior that helps it survive the winter.
A rabbit's fur turning white in winter helps it hide in the snow.
A lizard lying on a warm rock in the morning raises its body temperature.
A dog panting on a hot day cools its body by evaporation.
People sweat when they exercise because the evaporation of sweat cools the body.
A person shivers in the cold because muscle movement produces heat.
Your heart beats faster and you breathe harder when you run because your muscles need more oxygen.
After exercise, a person's breathing rate and heart rate slowly return to normal.
When you touch a hot stove, you pull your hand away quickly in a reflex controlled by the spinal cord.
Your pupils get smaller in bright light and larger in dim light.
Eating a balanced diet with fruits and vegetables provides vitamins and minerals.
Drinking milk helps build strong bones because it contains calcium.
Brushing teeth removes bacteria and food that can cause tooth decay.
A cut on the skin can let germs into the body.
A scab forms over a cut when platelets help the blood clot.
A person with a cold should wash hands often and cover coughs to avoid spreading the virus.
Getting a flu shot helps the body make antibodies against the flu virus.
Bread left in a warm damp place grows mold faster than bread kept in a refrigerator.
Food kept in a refrigerator lasts longer because cold temperatures slow the growth of bacteria.
A fallen log in a forest slowly breaks down because of decomposers such as fungi, bacteria, and insects.
Leaves that fall to the ground decompose and add nutrients to the soil.
A pumpkin left outside rots because decomposers break it down.
When a forest is cleared, animals that lived there lose their habitat and food.
When a new highway is built through a forest, some animal populations may decrease because of habitat loss.
When a dam is built, the land behind it floods and land habitats are lost.
If all the hawks in an area were removed, the number of mice and rabbits would probably increase.
If a disease killed the grass in a field, the rabbits that eat the grass would decrease.
If the number of frogs in a pond decreases, the number of insects they eat may increase.
When there are too many deer and few predators, the deer may eat most of the plants and then starve.
When rabbits were brought to Australia, they had no natural predators and their population grew out of control.
Owls hunting mice helps keep the mouse population under control.
A spider spinning a web to catch insects is an instinctive behavior.
Ants working together to carry food to their nest show cooperation in a colony.
A bird with a long thin beak is adapted to get nectar from deep flowers.
A duck's webbed feet help it paddle through water.
A polar bear's thick layer of fat keeps it warm in icy water.
A cactus can survive in the desert because it stores water in its thick stem.
A camel can survive in the desert because it can go many days without drinking water.
A fish dies when taken out of water because its gills cannot get oxygen from air.
A whale must come to the surface to breathe air because it has lungs.
A frog can breathe through its moist skin.
A snake uses its tongue to smell its surroundings.
A chameleon changes color to blend in with its surroundings.
A moth that looks like tree bark is hard for birds to see.
A monarch butterfly's bright orange color warns predators that it tastes bad.
A porcupine's quills protect it from predators.
A turtle pulls into its shell for protection.
A skunk sprays a bad smell to scare predators away.
A deer's brown color helps it blend in with the forest.
A woodpecker drills holes in trees to find insects to eat.
A hawk has excellent eyesight to spot prey from high in the air.
An owl can turn its head far around and hunts at night.
A mole has strong front claws for digging tunnels underground.
An earthworm moves through soil and eats dead plant material.
Maple trees lose their leaves in the fall, and pine trees keep their needles all winter.
A tree grows a new ring of wood every year.
An acorn grows into an oak tree.
A seed grows into a seedling and then into an adult plant that makes flowers and seeds.
A sunflower seed planted in spring can grow into a tall plant by summer.
Plants make oxygen that animals need, and animals make carbon dioxide that plants need.
A fish tank with plants needs less added oxygen because the plants release oxygen.
Algae in a pond use sunlight to make food and are eaten by small animals.
A pond with too much fertilizer runoff may have an algae bloom that kills fish.
Wearing gloves and washing hands after handling animals protects you from germs.
Students should wash their hands after handling soil, plants, or animals in an experiment.
Owls cough up pellets containing the bones of animals they ate.
Scientists study owl pellets to learn what owls eat.
A food chain in a garden could be lettuce, slug, toad, snake.
A food chain in the ocean could be phytoplankton, zooplankton, small fish, shark.
A desert food chain could be cactus, mouse, snake, hawk.
A forest food chain could be acorn, squirrel, fox.
Plants are at the start of every food chain because they capture energy from the Sun.
A lion gets its energy from the Sun indirectly by eating zebras that eat grass.
Mushrooms growing on a dead tree are decomposers.
Mold on an orange is a fungus that decomposes the fruit.
Earthworms in a compost pile help break down food scraps.
A sea turtle that eats a plastic bag may die because it thinks the bag is a jellyfish.
Birds covered in oil from an oil spill cannot fly or stay warm.
A frog's long sticky tongue is an adaptation for catching insects.
Male peacocks show bright tail feathers to attract females.
A baby kangaroo, called a joey, develops in its mother's pouch.
A dolphin is a mammal because it breathes air, has hair at birth, and feeds its young milk.
A bat is a mammal because it has fur and feeds its young milk.
A penguin is a bird because it has feathers and lays eggs, even though it cannot fly.
A whale is a mammal because it breathes with lungs and nurses its young.
A spider is an arachnid because it has eight legs and two body parts.
A snake is a reptile because it has dry scales and breathes with lungs.
A frog is an amphibian because it begins life in water with gills and later lives on land with lungs.
A shark is a fish because it breathes with gills and has fins.
A mushroom is a fungus because it does not make its own food.
A tomato is a fruit because it contains seeds.
Corn, beans, and peas are seeds.
Carrots and potatoes store food for the plant underground.
Lettuce and spinach are leaves that people eat.
Celery and asparagus are stems that people eat.
Broccoli and cauliflower are flowers that people eat.
When water in a freezer turns to ice, the water molecules lose energy and move more slowly.
When ice is heated and melts, its molecules gain energy and move faster.
When water is heated, its molecules move faster and spread farther apart.
When a liquid cools, its particles slow down and move closer together.
When a gas is cooled enough, it condenses into a liquid.
A puddle disappears on a sunny day because the water evaporates into water vapor.
Water droplets form on the outside of a cold glass of lemonade because water vapor in the air condenses on the cold surface.
A mirror in the bathroom fogs up after a hot shower because water vapor condenses on the cool mirror.
Breath forms a cloud on a cold day because water vapor in the breath condenses.
An ice cube left on a kitchen counter melts because heat flows from the warm air into the ice.
A pot of water left boiling on the stove gets less water over time because the water evaporates.
Clothes hung on a line dry because the water in them evaporates.
Wet towels dry faster in the sun and wind than in a closed room.
Perfume can be smelled across a room because its particles diffuse through the air.
Food coloring spreads through water faster in hot water than in cold water because the particles move faster.
Sugar dissolves faster in hot tea than in iced tea.
A balloon left in a hot car expands, and a balloon put in a freezer shrinks.
A basketball bounces lower on a cold day because the air inside contracts.
Tires look flatter on cold mornings because air contracts when it cools.
Power lines sag on hot summer days because the metal wires expand in the heat.
A metal lid on a glass jar can be loosened by running hot water over it because the metal expands.
Railroad tracks have small gaps so the rails can expand in summer heat.
Cracks in roads and sidewalks form when water freezes and expands.
Water pipes can burst in winter when the water inside freezes and expands.
A can of soda left in the freezer may burst because the water in it expands as it freezes.
An ice cube floats in a glass of water because ice is less dense than liquid water.
Lakes freeze from the top down because ice is less dense than water and floats, which lets fish survive under the ice.
A cork, a wooden block, and a plastic bottle cap float in water, while a coin, a marble, and a rock sink.
A ship made of steel floats because its hollow shape displaces a large amount of water, making its overall density less than water.
A ball of clay sinks, but the same clay shaped into a boat can float.
An egg sinks in fresh water but floats in very salty water because salt water is denser.
It is easier to float in the ocean than in a swimming pool because salt water is denser than fresh water.
Pouring oil, water, and syrup into a jar forms layers because the liquids have different densities.
A helium balloon rises because helium is less dense than air.
When baking soda is added to vinegar, bubbles of carbon dioxide gas form, which shows a chemical reaction.
When a cake bakes, a chemical change occurs and new substances form that cannot be changed back.
An iron nail left outside in the rain rusts, which is a chemical change.
A bicycle left in the rain gets rusty because iron in the steel reacts with oxygen and water.
A sliced apple turns brown because of a chemical reaction with oxygen in the air.
Milk turns sour because bacteria cause a chemical change.
Burning a candle produces light, heat, carbon dioxide, and water vapor.
A match burning is a chemical change that releases heat and light.
Tearing paper, crushing a can, and sharpening a pencil are physical changes.
Freezing juice into ice pops is a physical change.
Dissolving salt in water is a physical change, and the salt can be recovered by evaporating the water.
Mixing sand and iron filings makes a mixture that can be separated with a magnet.
Muddy water can be cleaned by pouring it through a filter.
Salt can be separated from salt water by letting the water evaporate.
A sponge soaks up water because it is absorbent and full of tiny holes.
A raincoat keeps you dry because it is made of waterproof material.
A ball rolls farther on a smooth wooden floor than on a carpet because the carpet has more friction.
A sled slides faster on ice than on grass because ice has less friction.
Rubbing your hands together on a cold day warms them by friction.
The brakes on a bicycle get warm after stopping because friction changes kinetic energy into heat.
Shoes with rubber soles and deep treads keep you from slipping because they increase friction.
Putting oil on a squeaky door hinge reduces friction.
A heavy box is harder to push than a light box because it has more mass and more friction.
Pushing a shopping cart harder makes it speed up faster.
An empty wagon is easier to pull than a wagon full of rocks because it has less mass.
When a moving bus stops suddenly, the passengers lurch forward because of inertia.
When a car turns sharply, passengers feel pushed toward the outside of the curve because of inertia.
A soccer ball rolling on grass slows down and stops because of friction with the grass.
A ball thrown into the air falls back down because gravity pulls it toward Earth.
An apple falls from a tree because of gravity.
A dropped book and a dropped pencil hit the floor at about the same time because gravity accelerates them equally.
A sheet of paper falls more slowly than a book because of air resistance.
A skydiver opens a parachute to increase air resistance and fall more slowly.
A kicked ball changes direction because a force acts on it.
A skateboarder pushes back on the ground and the ground pushes the skateboarder forward.
A swimmer pushes water backward to move forward.
A hammer is used to pull a nail out of wood, acting as a lever.
A flagpole uses a pulley so that pulling down on the rope raises the flag.
A screwdriver turning a screw is an example of a wheel and axle.
A knife and an axe are wedges that split materials apart.
Moving a piano onto a truck is easier using a ramp, which is an inclined plane.
A seesaw is a lever with the fulcrum in the middle.
A child on a swing has the most potential energy at the highest point and the most kinetic energy at the lowest point.
A roller coaster car moves fastest at the bottom of the first big hill.
A ball rolling down a hill speeds up as its potential energy changes to kinetic energy.
A wind up toy stores energy in a coiled spring.
A bow and arrow store elastic potential energy when the bow string is pulled back.
A trampoline stores elastic energy when it stretches and pushes the jumper back up.
A flashlight that does not work may have dead batteries, a burned out bulb, or a broken circuit.
A light bulb will not light if the wire is disconnected, because the circuit is open.
Holiday lights wired in series all go out when one bulb burns out.
A doorbell uses an electromagnet.
A junkyard crane uses an electromagnet to lift cars and drop them when the current is turned off.
A compass needle points north because it is a magnet that lines up with Earth's magnetic field.
A refrigerator magnet sticks to the door because the door is made of steel.
A magnet will pick up paper clips, iron nails, and steel pins.
Pennies, aluminum foil, plastic, and wood are nonmagnetic.
A balloon rubbed on a sweater sticks to a wall because of static electricity.
You may get a shock after walking on carpet and touching a doorknob because of static electricity.
Hair stands up after being brushed on a dry day because of static electricity.
Clothes stick together after coming out of the dryer because of static electricity.
A metal pot on a stove gets hot by conduction.
The handle of a metal spoon left in hot soup gets hot by conduction.
Warm air near the ceiling and cool air near the floor are caused by convection.
The Sun warms your face by radiation.
Standing near a campfire, you feel heat by radiation.
Water in a pot heats up as hot water at the bottom rises and cool water sinks, which is convection.
A dark car seat gets hotter in the sun than a light colored seat because dark colors absorb more light.
Snow melts faster under a dark cloth than under a white cloth because dark materials absorb more sunlight.
Wearing a black shirt on a sunny day feels hotter than wearing a white shirt.
A thermos keeps soup hot because it has a vacuum layer that stops conduction and convection.
An oven mitt protects a hand from a hot pan because it is an insulator.
A wooden spoon is better than a metal spoon for stirring hot soup because wood is a poor conductor of heat.
A winter coat keeps a person warm by trapping air and slowing the loss of body heat.
A blanket keeps you warm because it slows heat loss from your body, not because it makes heat.
An ice chest or cooler keeps food cold by insulating it from the warm air.
A sound gets quieter as you move farther from it.
When you pluck a rubber band, it vibrates and makes a sound.
Placing a hand on your throat while talking lets you feel vibrations of the vocal cords.
A drum makes a louder sound when it is hit harder.
A tight guitar string makes a higher pitch than a loose one.
A short string on a guitar makes a higher pitch than a long one.
A whistle makes a high pitched sound because the air vibrates very fast.
A big bell makes a lower sound than a small bell.
Water glasses filled to different levels make different pitches when tapped.
Sound echoes in an empty room with hard walls.
Furniture, curtains, and carpets absorb sound and reduce echoes in a room.
You hear an echo when you shout in a canyon because the sound reflects off the canyon walls.
You see a flash of lightning before you hear thunder because light travels faster than sound.
Fireworks are seen before they are heard because light travels faster than sound.
A pencil in a glass of water looks bent or broken because light refracts.
A spoon looks bent in water because light bends as it moves from water to air.
You see your reflection in a still pond or a mirror because light reflects off the smooth surface.
A shadow is longer when the light source is low and shorter when it is high.
A shadow puppet gets bigger when the hand moves closer to the light.
Wax paper is translucent, clear plastic wrap is transparent, and aluminum foil is opaque.
Sunglasses reduce the amount of light that reaches the eyes.
A magnifying glass can focus sunlight to a small hot spot.
A prism or a glass of water can split sunlight into a rainbow of colors.
A green leaf looks green because it reflects green light.
A white shirt reflects all colors of light, and a black shirt absorbs all colors.
Solar powered calculators use solar cells to change light into electricity.
A wind turbine spins when the wind blows and generates electricity.
A hand crank flashlight changes mechanical energy into electrical energy.
A toaster gets hot because electricity flows through wires with high resistance.
A light bulb gets hot because some electrical energy changes to heat.
Plugging too many appliances into one outlet can overheat wires and cause a fire.
Food is cooked in a microwave oven by microwaves that make water molecules move faster.
A pressure cooker cooks food faster because higher pressure raises the boiling point of water.
Water takes longer to boil on a mountain top than at sea level because it boils at a lower temperature there.
A pendulum with a longer string swings more slowly than one with a shorter string.
The mass of the bob does not change how long a pendulum takes to swing.
A car uses gasoline, which stores chemical energy, to move.
A person running changes chemical energy from food into kinetic energy and heat.
A car's speedometer measures its speed.
To find the speed of a toy car, measure the distance it travels and the time it takes, then divide distance by time.
A runner who goes 100 meters in 20 seconds has a speed of 5 meters per second.
When water is heated to boiling, bubbles of water vapor form at the bottom of the pot and rise to the top.
The bubbles that rise in a beaker of heated water contain water vapor.
Wood is less dense than water, so wood floats.
A rock is denser than water, so a rock sinks.
The Sun and other stars are alike because they are all huge balls of hot gas that give off light.
The Moon and the planets are alike because they reflect light from the Sun rather than making their own.
The Sun and the Moon both appear to rise in the east and set in the west because Earth rotates.
Earth and Mars are alike because both are rocky planets with seasons and polar ice caps.
Earth and Venus are similar in size.
Jupiter and Saturn are alike because both are gas giants with rings and many moons.
Comets and asteroids are both small bodies that orbit the Sun.
Plant and animal cells are alike because both have a nucleus, cell membrane, cytoplasm, and mitochondria.
Plants and animals are alike because both are made of cells, need water, grow, and reproduce.
Plants and animals both carry out cellular respiration.
Birds and bats both have wings for flying, but birds have feathers and bats have fur.
Whales and fish both live in water, but whales breathe air with lungs and fish use gills.
Frogs and toads are both amphibians.
Butterflies and moths both go through complete metamorphosis.
Lungs in mammals and gills in fish both take oxygen into the body.
The heart works like a pump.
The eye works like a camera; both use a lens to focus light.
The brain is like a computer that controls the body.
The kidneys work like a filter.
The skin is like a protective covering or armor.
The nucleus is like the control center or brain of the cell.
Mitochondria are like power plants for the cell.
The cell membrane is like a gate or security guard that controls what enters and leaves.
Chloroplasts are like solar panels that capture energy from sunlight.
The cell wall is like a brick wall that supports and protects.
Arteries and veins are like roads that carry blood through the body.
Melting and evaporation are alike because both require heat energy to be added.
Freezing and condensation are alike because both release heat energy.
Weathering and erosion both change Earth's surface over time.
Earthquakes and volcanoes both occur mostly along plate boundaries.
Hurricanes and tornadoes are both storms with rotating winds, but hurricanes are much larger.
Sound and light both travel as waves and carry energy.
Sound and water waves need a medium, but light does not.
Magnetism and gravity both act at a distance without touching.
Conductors of heat are often also conductors of electricity.
Levers and pulleys are both simple machines that make work easier.
Coal and oil are both fossil fuels formed from ancient living things.
Solar and wind energy are both renewable and do not pollute the air.
Mitosis and meiosis are both kinds of cell division.
Photosynthesis and respiration are opposite processes.
Photosynthesis stores energy and respiration releases energy.
A student can model day and night by shining a flashlight on a globe while spinning the globe.
A flashlight and a ball can model the phases of the Moon when the ball is moved around a person's head.

A Punnett square is a model used to predict the traits of offspring.

A globe is a model of Earth.
A planetarium projects a model of the night sky.
A computer model can predict the path of a hurricane.
Physical models are often made smaller than the real object to study it easily.
Models of atoms show the nucleus in the center with electrons around it.
A model can be changed when new information is discovered.
Earth rotates on its axis once every 24 hours, causing day and night.
Rotation is the spinning of a planet or moon on its axis.
Revolution is the movement of one object in an orbit around another object.
Earth revolves around the Sun once every year, about 365 days.
Day and night are caused by Earth's rotation on its axis.
The side of Earth facing the Sun has day, and the side facing away from the Sun has night.
The Sun appears to rise in the east and set in the west because Earth rotates from west to east.
The Sun rises in the east and sets in the west.
The stars and the Moon appear to move across the sky from east to west each night because of Earth's rotation.
Shadows are longest in the early morning and late afternoon when the Sun is low in the sky.
Shadows are shortest at noon when the Sun is highest in the sky.
Shadows point away from the Sun, so in the morning shadows point west and in the evening shadows point east.
In the Northern Hemisphere the noon Sun is in the southern part of the sky.
Earth's axis is tilted about 23.5 degrees.
Seasons are caused by the tilt of Earth's axis as Earth revolves around the Sun.
Earth is closest to the Sun in early January, which is winter in the Northern Hemisphere.
When the Northern Hemisphere is tilted toward the Sun it has summer, with more direct sunlight and longer days.
When the Northern Hemisphere is tilted away from the Sun it has winter, with less direct sunlight and shorter days.
When it is summer in the Northern Hemisphere it is winter in the Southern Hemisphere.
Direct rays of sunlight heat Earth's surface more than slanted indirect rays.
In summer the Sun is higher in the sky and there are more hours of daylight.
In winter the Sun is lower in the sky and there are fewer hours of daylight.
The summer solstice, around June 21, is the day with the most hours of daylight in the Northern Hemisphere.
The winter solstice, around December 21, is the day with the fewest hours of daylight in the Northern Hemisphere.
On the spring and fall equinoxes, day and night are about equal everywhere, about 12 hours each.
The North Pole has six months of daylight and six months of darkness.
The North Star, Polaris, stays almost in the same place in the sky because it is nearly above Earth's axis.
The Moon revolves around Earth about once every 27 days, and its phases repeat about every 29.5 days.
The Moon rotates once on its axis in the same time it takes to orbit Earth, so the same side always faces Earth.
The Moon does not make its own light; moonlight is sunlight reflected off the Moon.
Moon phases are caused by the changing positions of the Moon, Earth, and Sun, which change how much of the lit side we see.
The Moon's phases are new moon, waxing crescent, first quarter, waxing gibbous, full moon, waning gibbous, third quarter, and waning crescent.
During a new moon the Moon is between Earth and the Sun and the lit side faces away from Earth.
During a full moon Earth is between the Sun and the Moon and we see the whole lit side.
A full moon happens about two weeks after a new moon.
Waxing means the lit part of the Moon we see is growing, and waning means it is shrinking.
A complete cycle of Moon phases takes about one month.
A solar eclipse happens when the Moon passes between the Sun and Earth and blocks sunlight.
A solar eclipse can only happen during a new moon.
A lunar eclipse happens when Earth passes between the Sun and the Moon and Earth's shadow falls on the Moon.
A lunar eclipse can only happen during a full moon.
The Moon has no atmosphere, no liquid water, and no wind or weather.
Footprints left on the Moon can last for millions of years because there is no wind or water to erase them.
The Moon's surface has craters made by meteorites crashing into it.
The Moon has many craters because it has no atmosphere to burn up meteoroids and no weathering or erosion to erase them.
The Moon's gravity is about one sixth of Earth's gravity, so objects weigh less on the Moon.
An object's mass is the same on the Moon as on Earth, but its weight is less.
The Moon is Earth's only natural satellite.
The Moon is about 384,000 kilometers from Earth.
The Moon looks about as big as the Sun in the sky because the Sun is much farther away.
The Sun is a star at the center of our solar system.
The Sun is the closest star to Earth.
The Sun is a medium sized, middle aged yellow star.
The Sun appears larger and brighter than other stars because it is much closer to Earth.
The Sun produces energy by nuclear fusion, which joins hydrogen atoms to form helium.
The Sun is made mostly of hydrogen and helium gas.
The Sun provides light and heat energy for Earth.
The Sun is the source of almost all energy on Earth.
Light from the Sun takes about 8 minutes to reach Earth.
Earth is about 150 million kilometers, or 93 million miles, from the Sun.
Sunspots are dark cooler areas on the surface of the Sun.
Solar flares are bursts of energy from the Sun.
The solar wind is a stream of charged particles from the Sun.
Auroras, the northern lights, happen when charged particles from the Sun hit gases in the atmosphere near the poles.
Earth's magnetic field protects it from the solar wind.
The solar system includes the Sun, eight planets, their moons, dwarf planets, asteroids, comets, and meteoroids.
Gravity keeps the planets in orbit around the Sun.
The Sun's gravity holds the solar system together because the Sun has the most mass.
The Sun contains more than 99 percent of the mass of the solar system.
The planets in order from the Sun are Mercury, Venus, Earth, Mars, Jupiter, Saturn, Uranus, and Neptune.
The inner planets Mercury, Venus, Earth, and Mars are small rocky terrestrial planets.
The outer planets Jupiter, Saturn, Uranus, and Neptune are large gas giants made mostly of hydrogen and helium.
Mercury is the planet closest to the Sun and the smallest planet.
Mercury has almost no atmosphere and has very hot days and very cold nights.
Venus is the hottest planet because its thick carbon dioxide atmosphere traps heat by a strong greenhouse effect.
Venus is about the same size as Earth and is the brightest planet in the night sky.
Earth is the third planet from the Sun and the only planet known to support life.
Earth has liquid water, an atmosphere with oxygen, and a temperature range that supports life.
Mars is called the red planet because its soil contains iron oxide, or rust.
Mars has polar ice caps, dust storms, and the largest volcano in the solar system, Olympus Mons.
Mars has two small moons and a thin atmosphere of carbon dioxide.
Scientists have found evidence that liquid water once flowed on Mars.
Rovers have explored the surface of Mars.
The asteroid belt lies between the orbits of Mars and Jupiter.
Asteroids are rocky or metal objects that orbit the Sun, mostly in the asteroid belt.
Jupiter is the largest planet in the solar system.
Jupiter has a Great Red Spot, which is a giant storm.
Jupiter has many moons, including the four large Galilean moons discovered by Galileo.
Saturn is known for its large bright rings made of ice and rock.
Saturn is the least dense planet and would float in water.
Uranus rotates on its side and appears blue green because of methane in its atmosphere.
Neptune is the farthest planet from the Sun and has the strongest winds.
Pluto is a dwarf planet in the Kuiper Belt beyond Neptune.
Pluto was reclassified as a dwarf planet in 2006 because it has not cleared its orbit of other objects.
Planets farther from the Sun take longer to orbit the Sun and have longer years.
Planets farther from the Sun are colder because they receive less solar energy.
Planets closer to the Sun move faster in their orbits.
Planets reflect light from the Sun; they do not make their own light.
Planets appear to wander among the stars over weeks and months.
Planets orbit the Sun in elliptical paths.
A comet is a ball of ice, dust, and rock that orbits the Sun and forms a glowing tail when it comes near the Sun.
A comet's tail always points away from the Sun because of the solar wind.
Halley's Comet returns about every 76 years.
A meteoroid is a small rock in space.
A meteor is a streak of light seen when a meteoroid burns up in Earth's atmosphere, called a shooting star.
A meteorite is a piece of rock from space that hits Earth's surface.
Craters on Earth and the Moon are formed by impacts of meteorites and asteroids.
A star is a huge ball of hot glowing gas that produces its own light and heat by nuclear fusion.
Stars differ in color, size, temperature, brightness, and distance from Earth.
The color of a star shows its surface temperature.
Blue and white stars are the hottest stars, and red stars are the coolest stars.
Yellow stars like the Sun have medium temperatures.
A star's apparent brightness depends on its actual brightness and its distance from Earth.
Stars appear as tiny points of light because they are very far away.
Stars are not visible during the day because sunlight is much brighter.
Stars form in nebulas, which are huge clouds of gas and dust.
Gravity pulls gas and dust in a nebula together to form a star.
A star spends most of its life on the main sequence fusing hydrogen into helium.
A star like the Sun becomes a red giant, then a white dwarf at the end of its life.
Very massive stars end in a supernova explosion and may become a neutron star or a black hole.
A black hole has gravity so strong that not even light can escape.
The life cycle of a star depends on its mass; more massive stars have shorter lives.
A constellation is a group of stars that forms a pattern in the sky.
Constellations such as Orion and the Big Dipper appear in different places in the sky in different seasons because Earth revolves around the Sun.
Different constellations are visible in different seasons because Earth's position in its orbit changes.
A light year is the distance light travels in one year.
Light years are used to measure distances to stars and galaxies.
Proxima Centauri is the closest star to the Sun, about 4.2 light years away.
When we look at distant stars we see light that left them long ago.
A galaxy is a huge group of billions of stars, gas, and dust held together by gravity.
Our solar system is in the Milky Way galaxy.
The Milky Way is a spiral galaxy.
Galaxies can be spiral, elliptical, or irregular in shape.
The universe contains billions of galaxies.
The Big Bang theory states that the universe began about 13.8 billion years ago and has been expanding ever since.
The red shift of light from distant galaxies shows that the universe is expanding.
A telescope collects light and makes distant objects appear closer and larger.
Galileo used a telescope to discover moons orbiting Jupiter and phases of Venus.
The geocentric model placed Earth at the center of the universe.
The heliocentric model, proposed by Copernicus, placed the Sun at the center of the solar system.
Kepler discovered that planets move in elliptical orbits.
Newton explained that gravity keeps the Moon and planets in their orbits.
Space telescopes like the Hubble Space Telescope orbit above the atmosphere and get clearer images.
The atmosphere blurs and blocks some light, which is why telescopes are put on mountains or in space.
Radio telescopes detect radio waves from objects in space.
Satellites orbit Earth and are used for communication, weather forecasting, navigation, and GPS.
The International Space Station orbits Earth and astronauts live and do experiments there.
Astronauts in orbit feel weightless because they are in free fall around Earth.
Astronauts need spacesuits to provide oxygen, pressure, and protection from temperature extremes.
Rockets move forward by pushing hot gases out the back, an example of Newton's third law.
Space probes such as Voyager have traveled to the outer planets.
Neil Armstrong was the first person to walk on the Moon in 1969 during the Apollo 11 mission.
Sound cannot travel in space because space is a vacuum with no matter.
Earth's gravity keeps the Moon in orbit around Earth.
The Moon's gravity causes ocean tides on Earth.
A year is the time it takes Earth to revolve once around the Sun.
A day is the time it takes Earth to rotate once on its axis.
A month is roughly the time it takes the Moon to go through all its phases.
A leap year has 366 days because Earth takes about 365 and one quarter days to orbit the Sun.
Time zones exist because Earth rotates and different places face the Sun at different times.
An astronomer is a scientist who studies stars, planets, and other objects in space.
Earth is the only planet with large amounts of liquid water on its surface.
Earth's distance from the Sun lets water exist as a liquid.
Jupiter and Saturn have many moons.
Titan is Saturn's largest moon and has a thick atmosphere.
Europa, a moon of Jupiter, may have an ocean of liquid water under its icy surface.
Gravity is weaker on small planets and moons with less mass.
A sundial uses the shadow of a stick to tell the time of day.
The path of the Sun across the sky is higher in summer and lower in winter.
The Sun is about 109 times wider than Earth.
A planet is a large round body that orbits a star and has cleared its orbit.
A moon is a natural satellite that orbits a planet.
The Moon's surface has dark flat areas called maria and bright highlands.
The Moon formed from debris when a large object collided with early Earth.
Some stars are larger than the Sun and some are smaller.
Binary stars are two stars that orbit each other.

Students should label their data with units.

Students should repeat their trials and calculate the average.

Students who make a claim should support it with evidence from data.
The data from several groups is more reliable than the data from one group.
If a student's results differ greatly from all the other groups, the student may have made an error.
A student should not change data to fit the hypothesis.

It takes Earth about 24 hours to spin around once on its axis.
It takes Earth about 365 days to travel once around the Sun.
It takes the Moon about one month to travel once around Earth.
Earth's spinning on its axis is what causes the Sun to rise and set each day.
Earth's movement around the Sun causes the yearly cycle of seasons.
The Sun is in the eastern sky in the morning and in the western sky in the evening.
At sunrise the Sun appears on the eastern horizon.
At sunset the Sun disappears below the western horizon.
At noon the Sun is at its highest point in the sky for the day.
The length of daylight is longest in summer and shortest in winter.
In winter there are fewer hours of daylight, so there is less time for the Sun to heat the land.
In summer the Sun's rays hit Earth more directly, so the same energy is concentrated on a smaller area.
In the Northern Hemisphere, the first day of summer is about June 21.
In the Northern Hemisphere, the first day of winter is about December 21.
In the Northern Hemisphere, the first day of spring is about March 20 and the first day of fall is about September 22.
The tilt of Earth's axis stays pointed in the same direction in space as Earth orbits the Sun.
The North Pole always points toward Polaris.
If Earth were not tilted, every place would have the same length of day all year.
Places near the equator have about the same length of day all year.
Places far from the equator have large changes in day length during the year.
Alaska has very long summer days and very long winter nights.
A shadow stick shows that shadows change length and direction during the day.
A shadow points west in the morning and east in the afternoon in the Northern Hemisphere.
Shadows are shorter in summer than in winter at noon.
The Moon is lit by the Sun, and half of it is always lit.
As the Moon orbits Earth, we see different amounts of its lit half, which are the phases.
A new moon is between Earth and the Sun.
A full moon is on the opposite side of Earth from the Sun.
A full moon rises at about sunset.
The Moon goes from new to full in about two weeks.
The Moon goes from full back to new in about two weeks.
A gibbous moon is more than half lit.
A crescent moon is less than half lit.
A quarter moon looks like a half circle.
The Moon appears to change shape, but its actual shape does not change.
The Moon is a sphere.
The Moon does not have its own light.
The Moon has no air, so astronauts must bring oxygen.
The Moon has no liquid water on its surface.
The Moon's surface has craters, mountains, and dark plains of old lava.
Earth has one moon, Mars has two, and Jupiter and Saturn have dozens.

Tides rise and fall about twice a day.
Ocean tides are highest when the Sun, Moon, and Earth are lined up.
An eclipse of the Sun happens when the Moon blocks the Sun's light from reaching part of Earth.
An eclipse of the Moon happens when the Moon passes through Earth's shadow.
The Sun is about 400 times wider than the Moon and about 400 times farther away.
The Sun is the only star in our solar system.
The Sun gives off light and heat because of nuclear reactions in its core.
Without the Sun, Earth would be dark and frozen.

Sunlight takes about eight minutes to travel from the Sun to Earth.

Earth is a planet that orbits a star called the Sun.
Earth is one of eight planets in the solar system.
Earth is the only planet known to have life.
The Moon orbits Earth, and Earth orbits the Sun.
The Sun, Earth, and Moon are all shaped like spheres.
Earth is larger than the Moon, and the Sun is much larger than Earth.
About 1.3 million Earths could fit inside the Sun.

Water is made of molecules with two hydrogen atoms and one oxygen atom.
Water molecules are attracted to each other, which is called cohesion.
Water molecules are attracted to other surfaces, which is called adhesion.
Capillary action lets water climb up narrow tubes and move up through plant stems and paper towels.
Surface tension is caused by cohesion of water molecules at the surface.
Surface tension of water lets a needle or paper clip float on the surface and lets water striders walk on water.
Water dissolves more substances than any other liquid, so it is called the universal solvent.
Water has a high specific heat, so it takes a lot of energy to change its temperature.
Water is densest at about 4 degrees Celsius.
Water expands when it freezes, unlike most substances.
Pure water does not conduct electricity well, but water with dissolved salts does.
Pure water has no color, taste, or smell.
Water covers about 71 percent of Earth's surface.
Living things are made mostly of water.
All living things need water to survive.
Humans can survive only a few days without water.
Air is matter because it has mass and takes up space.
A balloon or ball filled with air weighs more than when it is empty.
An upside down cup pushed into water stays dry inside because air takes up space.
Air pressure pushes in all directions.
Air pressure is what holds a suction cup to a wall.
Air pressure lets you drink through a straw.
Air pressure is greater at sea level than on a mountain.
Ears pop when air pressure changes quickly, as in an airplane or elevator.
Moving air has less pressure than still air, which helps airplane wings lift.
Air contains oxygen that living things need for respiration and fires need for burning.
Air contains carbon dioxide that plants need for photosynthesis.
Air contains water vapor that forms clouds and rain.
Wind is air moving from high pressure to low pressure.
Living things need energy, water, air, nutrients, and a place to live.
Plants need sunlight, water, air, nutrients, and space to grow.
Animals need food, water, air, and shelter.
Shelter protects animals from weather and predators.
Space is needed so organisms can find enough food and water.
Living things grow, change, reproduce, need energy, respond to their surroundings, and are made of cells.
A rock is nonliving because it does not grow, reproduce, eat, or breathe.
Water, air, sunlight, rocks, and soil are nonliving parts of the environment.
Fire is not alive even though it grows and uses oxygen, because it is not made of cells and does not reproduce.
A seed is alive even though it seems inactive, because it can grow into a plant.
Wood from a tree was once living.
Coal, oil, and natural gas come from things that were once living.
Shells, bones, and leaves are remains of living things.
Dead leaves and logs are no longer living but were once part of living things.
The best evidence that something is alive is that it grows, reproduces, and uses energy.
An oak tree is a living thing because it grows, makes seeds, and uses energy from sunlight.
Living things depend on nonliving things such as water, air, and sunlight.
Living things depend on other living things for food, shelter, and pollination.
Organisms live in habitats that meet their needs.
A pond habitat provides water, food, and shelter for frogs, fish, insects, and ducks.
A forest habitat provides trees for shelter, nuts, berries, and insects for food.
A desert habitat has little water, so organisms there must save water.
An ocean habitat has salt water, and many organisms there use gills to get oxygen.
A grassland habitat has wide open spaces with grasses and few trees.
An arctic habitat is very cold, so organisms there have thick fur, fat, or feathers.
A rain forest habitat is warm and wet, with many layers of plants.
A wetland habitat has shallow water and plants such as cattails and reeds.
Animals that live in trees often have claws or tails for climbing.
Animals that live underground often have strong claws for digging and small eyes.
Animals that live in water often have fins, flippers, webbed feet, or gills.
Animals that fly have wings and light bodies.
A wave is a disturbance that transfers energy from one place to another without transferring matter.
Waves carry energy from place to place.
A medium is the matter through which a wave travels, such as air, water, or a solid.
Mechanical waves such as sound and water waves need a medium to travel.
Electromagnetic waves such as light do not need a medium and can travel through empty space.
In a transverse wave, the particles of the medium move perpendicular to the direction the wave travels.
Light waves and waves on a rope are transverse waves.
In a longitudinal or compression wave, the particles of the medium move back and forth parallel to the direction the wave travels.
Sound waves are longitudinal waves made of compressions and rarefactions.
The crest is the highest point of a wave and the trough is the lowest point.
Wavelength is the distance from one crest to the next crest.
Amplitude is the height of a wave from its rest position and is related to the energy of the wave.
Waves with a larger amplitude carry more energy.
Frequency is the number of waves that pass a point each second, measured in hertz.
Higher frequency waves have shorter wavelengths.
Wave speed equals frequency times wavelength.
Reflection is when a wave bounces off a surface.
Refraction is the bending of a wave as it passes from one medium into another and changes speed.
Diffraction is the bending of waves around obstacles or through openings.
Interference happens when two waves meet and combine.
Sound is produced by vibrations.
Sound is a form of energy produced when objects vibrate.
A guitar string vibrates to make sound.
Vocal cords vibrate to produce the sound of the voice.
Sound needs a medium and cannot travel through a vacuum or outer space.
Sound travels fastest through solids, slower through liquids, and slowest through gases.
Sound travels about 343 meters per second in air.
Sound travels faster in warm air than in cold air.
Pitch is how high or low a sound is and depends on the frequency of the vibrations.
Faster vibrations produce higher pitched sounds.
Short, thin, or tight strings vibrate faster and make higher pitches than long, thick, or loose strings.
A small drum or short tube makes a higher pitch than a large drum or long tube.
Volume, or loudness, depends on the amplitude of the sound wave.
Hitting a drum harder makes a louder sound because the vibrations have a larger amplitude.
Loudness is measured in decibels.
Very loud sounds can damage hearing.
An echo is a reflected sound wave.
Soft materials such as curtains, carpets, and foam absorb sound and reduce echoes.
Hard smooth surfaces reflect sound.
Sonar uses reflected sound waves to find the depth of water or locate objects underwater.
Ultrasound uses high frequency sound waves to make images of the inside of the body.
Humans can hear sounds with frequencies from about 20 to 20,000 hertz.
Dogs and bats can hear higher pitched sounds than humans.
The Doppler effect is the change in pitch heard as a sound source moves toward or away from a listener.
A siren sounds higher pitched as it approaches and lower as it moves away.
Light is a form of energy that travels as electromagnetic waves.
Light travels in straight lines.
Light travels at about 300,000 kilometers per second in a vacuum, the fastest speed in the universe.
Light travels faster than sound.
Light can travel through a vacuum such as space.
Luminous objects such as the Sun, a candle, and a light bulb give off their own light.
We see most objects because light reflects off them and enters our eyes.
The Moon is seen because it reflects light from the Sun.
A shadow forms when an object blocks light.
Shadows are larger when the object is closer to the light source.
Transparent materials such as clear glass and water let almost all light pass through.
Translucent materials such as wax paper and frosted glass let some light through but scatter it.
Opaque materials such as wood, metal, and cardboard block light from passing through.
A mirror reflects light; the angle of incidence equals the angle of reflection.
Smooth shiny surfaces such as mirrors reflect light clearly.
A plane mirror makes an image that is reversed left to right.
Refraction of light makes a straw look bent in a glass of water.
Light bends, or refracts, when it passes from air into water or glass because it changes speed.
A lens is a curved piece of glass or plastic that refracts light.
A convex lens is thicker in the middle and bends light rays together to a focus; it is used in magnifying glasses.
A concave lens is thinner in the middle and spreads light rays apart.
A magnifying glass is a convex lens that makes objects look larger.
Eyeglasses use lenses to correct vision.
A prism separates white light into the colors of the rainbow.
White light is a mixture of all colors of light.
The colors of the visible spectrum are red, orange, yellow, green, blue, indigo, and violet.
Red light has the longest wavelength and violet light has the shortest wavelength of visible light.
An object's color is the color of light it reflects.
A red apple looks red because it reflects red light and absorbs other colors.
A black object absorbs all colors of light, and a white object reflects all colors.
The sky looks blue because air scatters blue light from the Sun more than other colors.
The electromagnetic spectrum includes radio waves, microwaves, infrared, visible light, ultraviolet, X rays, and gamma rays.
Radio waves have the longest wavelengths and lowest frequencies in the electromagnetic spectrum.
Gamma rays have the shortest wavelengths, highest frequencies, and most energy.
Microwaves are used for cooking and communication.
Infrared radiation is felt as heat.
Ultraviolet light from the Sun causes sunburn and can be blocked by sunscreen.
X rays pass through soft tissue but not bone, so they are used to see bones.
Radio waves carry radio, television, and cell phone signals.
Visible light is the only part of the electromagnetic spectrum humans can see.
All electromagnetic waves travel at the speed of light in a vacuum.
Lasers produce a narrow beam of light of a single color.
Fiber optic cables carry light signals for communication.
Telescopes and microscopes use lenses or mirrors to observe objects.
Cameras use a lens to focus light onto a sensor or film.
The human eye works like a camera, with a lens focusing light on the retina.
Plants use light energy for photosynthesis.
Solar panels absorb light and change it into electricity.
Sound is louder when you are closer to the source.
Sound waves spread out and lose energy as they travel away from their source.
Earthquake waves are seismic waves that travel through Earth.
Ocean waves get taller and break when they reach shallow water near shore.
Higher energy waves can cause more damage, such as tsunami waves.
A tuning fork vibrates at a specific frequency.
Musical instruments make sound by vibrating strings, air columns, or surfaces.
Blowing across a bottle makes sound because the air inside vibrates.
Bottles with less air space produce higher pitches when blown across.
A string telephone works because sound vibrations travel along the string.
Sound travels faster through water than through air.
Whales communicate with sounds that travel long distances underwater.
Clouds are made of tiny drops of liquid water or ice crystals.
Cloud formation requires cooling of moist air to its dew point.
Air rises and cools when it is pushed up over mountains, which can cause rain.
Thunderstorms are most common on hot humid summer afternoons.
Lightning can strike tall objects such as trees and towers.
During a thunderstorm, people should go indoors and stay away from water and tall objects.
Hurricane winds rotate counterclockwise in the Northern Hemisphere.
Hurricanes are measured on a scale from category 1 to category 5.
Tornadoes can destroy buildings with winds over 300 kilometers per hour.
The Fujita scale measures tornado strength by the damage caused.
Weather forecasts are less accurate the further into the future they predict.
A barometer reading that is rising means fair weather is likely.
A wind vane points into the wind, showing where the wind is coming from.
A north wind blows from the north toward the south.
Wind speed increases when the difference in air pressure between two places is large.
Heat waves are periods of unusually hot weather.
Frost can damage crops in spring and fall.
Snow insulates the ground and protects plant roots from very cold air.
An ice storm coats trees and power lines with ice that can make them break.
Fog forms on cool mornings when air near the ground cools to its dew point.
Humidity is higher near oceans and lower in deserts.
On a humid day sweat evaporates slowly, so people feel hotter.
Dry air on cold winter days can cause static electricity shocks.
The amount of water vapor air can hold increases with temperature.
Rainfall is measured in millimeters or inches with a rain gauge.
Average monthly temperature and rainfall describe the climate of a place.
A climate graph shows average temperature and precipitation for each month.
Weather can change from hour to hour, but climate changes over decades or centuries.
The Sun heats the land, the land heats the air, and warm air rises.
Earth's surface is heated unevenly because land, water, and different surfaces absorb heat differently.
In the morning, the land heats faster than the ocean, so cool air from the ocean blows onto land.
Cities are warmer than the countryside because pavement and buildings absorb heat.
The Sun's energy reaches Earth as visible light, infrared radiation, and ultraviolet radiation.
Most of the Sun's energy that reaches Earth is visible light.
Earth gives off heat as infrared radiation.
The Moon reflects about 12 percent of the sunlight that hits it.
The Moon is brightest in the night sky at full moon.
A waxing crescent moon is seen in the western sky shortly after sunset.
The first quarter moon looks like a half circle lit on the right side in the Northern Hemisphere.
The third quarter moon looks like a half circle lit on the left side in the Northern Hemisphere.
About one week after a new moon, the Moon is at first quarter.
About one week after a full moon, the Moon is at third quarter.
About two weeks after a full moon, the Moon is new.
The Moon's gravity is weaker than Earth's because the Moon has less mass.
Without gravity, the planets would move in straight lines away from the Sun.
The force of gravity between the Sun and a planet depends on their masses and the distance between them.
Planets farther from the Sun feel a weaker pull from the Sun's gravity.
Objects in orbit are constantly falling toward the body they orbit while moving sideways.
Satellites must travel very fast to stay in orbit around Earth.
The Hubble Space Telescope orbits Earth above the atmosphere.
Astronauts on the International Space Station see about 16 sunrises a day.
The Sun is about 330,000 times more massive than Earth.
Jupiter is more than 11 times wider than Earth.
Earth's diameter is about 12,700 kilometers.
The Moon's diameter is about 3,500 kilometers.
The Sun is the brightest object in our sky.
After the Sun and Moon, Venus is the brightest object in the night sky.
Venus is sometimes called the morning star or evening star.
Mars has seasons because its axis is tilted like Earth's.
Uranus has extreme seasons because it is tilted on its side.
Gas giants do not have solid surfaces.
The rings of Saturn are made of countless pieces of ice and rock.
Jupiter's moon Io has active volcanoes.
Our solar system formed about 4.6 billion years ago from a spinning cloud of gas and dust called a nebula.
The planets formed from material left over after the Sun formed.
Stars are born, change, and die over billions of years.
The Sun will become a red giant in about 5 billion years.
Red dwarf stars are smaller and cooler than the Sun and live much longer.
The brightness of a star as seen from Earth is called its apparent magnitude.
The true brightness of a star is called its absolute magnitude or luminosity.
A Hertzsprung Russell diagram plots the temperature and brightness of stars.
Most stars, including the Sun, are main sequence stars.
The light from a star can tell scientists what elements it is made of.
A spectroscope separates the light from a star into a spectrum.
Galaxies are moving away from each other because the universe is expanding.
The Andromeda galaxy is the nearest large galaxy to the Milky Way.
The Milky Way contains about 100 billion to 400 billion stars.
Our solar system is located in one of the spiral arms of the Milky Way.
It takes the Sun about 225 million years to orbit the center of the Milky Way.
Many stars have planets orbiting them, called exoplanets.
Scientists search for planets in the habitable zone where liquid water could exist.
Clouds form when water vapor in rising air cools and condenses on tiny particles of dust.
Rain forms when tiny droplets in clouds combine into drops heavy enough to fall.
Snow forms when water vapor freezes into ice crystals in cold clouds.
Hail forms in tall thunderstorm clouds with strong updrafts.
Sleet forms when raindrops freeze as they fall through freezing air.
Fog forms when air near the ground cools to its dew point.
Dew forms on grass on cool clear nights.
Frost forms instead of dew when the temperature is below freezing.
Puffy white cumulus clouds usually mean fair weather.
Tall dark cumulonimbus clouds usually mean thunderstorms.
Thin wispy cirrus clouds high in the sky can mean a change in weather is coming.
Gray layered stratus clouds can bring drizzle or light rain.
A sudden drop in air pressure often means a storm is coming.
Steady high air pressure usually means fair weather.
Wind blows from areas of high air pressure to areas of low air pressure.
The faster the change in pressure over a distance, the stronger the wind.
A weather vane points to the direction the wind comes from.
A wind sock shows the direction and strength of the wind.
Weather in most of the United States moves from west to east.
A cold front often brings a quick change to cooler, drier weather after storms.
A warm front often brings gentle rain and then warmer, more humid weather.
Thunderstorms can bring lightning, heavy rain, strong winds, and hail.
Tornadoes form from strong thunderstorms, usually in spring in the central United States.
Hurricanes form over warm tropical oceans in late summer and fall.
Blizzards bring heavy snow, strong winds, and very cold temperatures.
Droughts happen when an area gets much less rain than normal for a long time.
Floods happen when heavy rain or melting snow causes rivers to overflow.
Weather forecasters use data from weather stations, balloons, satellites, and radar.
A weather forecast predicts what the weather will be in the coming days.
Climate describes the typical weather of a region over 30 years or more.
A region's climate determines what plants and animals can live there.
Deserts have dry climates, rain forests have wet climates, and the poles have cold climates.
Water covers most of Earth's surface, but most of it is salty ocean water.
Fresh water is found in lakes, rivers, ponds, glaciers, ice caps, and underground.
People get drinking water from rivers, lakes, reservoirs, and wells.
Water is a renewable resource because the water cycle replaces it, but clean fresh water can run short.
Water evaporates from oceans, lakes, rivers, soil, and plants.
Water vapor rises into the atmosphere, cools, and condenses into clouds.
Water returns to Earth as precipitation and flows back to the ocean through rivers and groundwater.
The water cycle is powered by energy from the Sun and by gravity.
Water moves from the ocean to the air by evaporation.
Water moves from the air to the ground by precipitation.
Water moves from the land to the ocean by runoff and rivers.
Water moves from plants to the air by transpiration.
Water moves into the ground by infiltration.
Glaciers store water as ice for thousands of years.
Ice melts into water when it warms above 0 degrees Celsius.
Water freezes into ice when it cools below 0 degrees Celsius.
Water boils into steam at 100 degrees Celsius.
